on the right hand side also matches with the result from the ensemble proposed in section
2
.

After these preliminaries, let us go back to gravity and compute the partition function, following
SSS
. The path integral localizes to an integral over the moduli space of
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic spaces.
If we ignore the gluing on the boundaries, the only moduli of the cylinder are
b,\phi
.
From the point of view of
BF
theory, these moduli arise as follows.
A flat connection on the cylinder can be described by its holonomy around the interior geodesic. We denote this holonomy by
U\in G
. Two holonomies related by conjugation
U\to RUR^{-1}
,
R\in G
, are gauge-equivalent, so we only care about the conjugacy class of
U
. In order for a flat connection with holonomy
U
to have a geometric interpretation in terms
of a hyperbolic metric, the conjugacy class of
U
must be hyperbolic (meaning that, after reducing modulo the odd variables, the
{\rm SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
component of
U
is
hyperbolic).
A hyperbolic element
U
can be conjugated to a diagonal form:

\displaystyle U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}e^{\frac{b}{2}}&0&0\\
0&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}e^{-\frac{b}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\end{array}\right).

Here
b
is the length of the interior geodesic in fig.
5
and
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}
is the
R
-symmetry holonomy around this geodesic.
The minus sign appearing in the
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,\mathbb{R})
block indicates that when
\phi=0
, fermions are antiperiodic around this geodesic. At
\phi=\pi
, the fermions are periodic.
As opposed to the case of

{\cal N}=1
supergravity, we do not need to consider spin structures separately, because changing the
spin structure would be equivalent to shifting
\phi
by
\pi
.

For convenience, we have represented
U
as a
3\times 3
supermatrix,
but we remind the reader that the gauge group is really a
\widehat{q}
-fold cover of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
. This cover has no convenient matrix representation, so it is often convenient to write formulas
projected to
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.
In the
\widehat{q}
-fold cover, the parameter
\phi
runs from
0
to
2\pi
, although in
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
itself, a shift of
\phi
by
2\pi/\widehat{q}
would give the same group element.

One can separate the cylinder into two “trumpets” by cutting along the interior geodesic in
fig.
5
. Each trumpet is a cylinder with one asymptotically NAdS boundary and one geodesic boundary. An
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian mode propagates on each
NAdS boundary. Taking this into account, we now have additional moduli that arise from the way we glue the two trumpets along the interior geodesic.
These moduli are present because a global rotation or
R
-symmetry rotation of either trumpet would act non-trivially on the Schwarzian modes of the other one. Hence
one can make a rotation or
R
-symmetry transformation of one trumpet relative to the other before gluing.
Again, the
BF
theory perspective is useful. Gauge transformations are constrained to be trivial along an NAdS boundary, so with two such boundaries, one can define
a gauge-invariant
“holonomy”
V
by parallel transport from one boundary to the other. Flatness of the connection implies that
V
must commute with
U
, so they are diagonal in the same
basis,
V={\rm diag}(-e^{\varrho/2+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\varphi},-e^{-\varrho/2+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\varphi},e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\varphi})
, with “gluing parameters”
\varrho,\varphi
. In order not to overcount geometries, we restrict to
0\leq\varrho\leq b
,
0\leq\varphi\leq 2\pi
. To summarize, the interior moduli of the cylinder are
b
,
\phi
together with the geometrical twist parameter

\varrho
and the
R
-symmetry twist parameter
\varphi
.

In standard approaches to the path integral, as in
SSS
, the integration measure
for these parameters is
{\mathrm{d}}b\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\varrho\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi
. (The torsion, which we use in section
4
, suggests a slightly different though ultimately equivalent
gluing procedure.)
The path integral over the bulk is independent of
\varrho
and
\varphi
and therefore they can be integrated out from the beginning, producing an effective measure
2\pi b\,{\mathrm{d}}b\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi
.

In this example of the trumpet, all interior moduli are bosonic, with fermionic moduli coming only from the Schwarzian modes that we study next. As soon as we
increase the number of boundaries or handles, there are also bulk fermionic moduli.

The final ingredient we need to complete the evaluation of
Z_{0,2}
is the path integral of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian modes. The Schwarzian action arises in gluing the bulk geometry
to the worldline of the boundary quantum mechanics, so it depends on the parameters
b,\phi
of the bulk geometry. We will focus on the Schwarzian path integral on one NAdS
2

boundary,

\beta,\alpha
b,\phi
\to
x
2\pi
T

Figure 6:
On the left panel we represent the trumpet geometry which is a cylinder with one NAdS boundary (left) with inverse temperature
\beta
and chemical potential
\alpha
, and one geodesic boundary (right) with length
b
and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy
\phi
. On the right panel we represent the trumpet in the complex
x
plane with
x\cong x+2\pi
. The NAdS boundary is at
x={\mathrm{i}}T+y
, for
T\gg 1
, while the geodesic boundary is along the real axis.

The calculation is very similar to the one that computes the disk partition function. We view the trumpet as the complex
x
plane with the equivalence
x\cong x+2\pi
, and a metric of scalar curvature
-2
. Let us put the geodesic at
{\rm Im}~x=0
and the outer NAdS boundary at
{\rm Im}~x=T
, for some large
T
. Including the fermions, the trumpet is described by coordinates
x,\psi,\overline{\psi}
with equivalence

(x,\psi,\overline{\psi})\cong(x+2\pi,-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\psi,-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\overline{\psi}).

(61)

On the upper boundary, write
x={\mathrm{i}}T+y
with real
y
. Thus the upper boundary is described by
y,\psi,\overline{\psi}
with

(y,\psi,\overline{\psi})\cong(y+2\pi,-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\psi,-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\overline{\psi}).

(62)

Just like in the case of the disk, the boundary quantum mechanics system is formulated on a supercircle with coordinates
t,\theta,\overline{\theta}
and an equivalence relation (
44
).
For the case of the trumpet, the fixed points take the form

\displaystyle y(t,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{\beta}t,

\displaystyle\psi(t,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi-\alpha)t/\beta}\theta,

(63)

with
\alpha\to\alpha+2\pi n
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. These fixed points are annihilated by an operator similar to the one appearing in the case of the disk

D=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}-\widehat{q}\frac{\phi-\alpha}{\beta}{\mathrm{i}}(\psi\partial_{\psi}-\overline{\psi}\partial_{\overline{\psi}}).

(64)

The action on these fixed points can be computed from the bosonic sector only. The rotation eigenvalues from the bosonic sector are also straightforward. The fermionic rotation eigenvalues
are independent of
\phi
. The calculation is explained in appendix
A
. The trumpet partition function with bulk parameters

b,\phi
is given by

\displaystyle Z_{\rm tr}(\beta,\alpha;b,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\exp(2\pi{\mathrm{i}}n\delta)\frac{\widehat{q}\,\cos\left(\pi\widehat{q}\,(n+\frac{\alpha}{2\pi})\right)}{\pi\beta}e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}-\frac{4\pi^{2}\,\widehat{q}^{\,2}}{\beta}(n+\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}-\frac{\phi}{2\pi})^{2}},

(65)

The exponential term is precisely the sum of the bosonic Schwarzian action for the trumpet and the bosonic
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
mode action, with the same boundary conditions for bosonic fields as in
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity. Using Poisson resummation, the expression (
65
) can be written as a sum over supermultiplets

\displaystyle Z_{\rm tr}(\beta,\alpha;b,\phi)=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}e^{-\beta E_{0}(q)}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}}}{\sqrt{4\pi\beta}}.

(66)

This expression is particularly simple. Other than the chemical potential dependence, which reproduces the expectation for the charge spectrum of a supermultiplet, the rest is almost the same as if one considered bosonic JT gravity coupled to a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge field. We remind the reader that the bosonic JT gravity trumpet is indeed
Z^{\rm bos.}_{\rm tr}(\beta,b)=e^{-b^{2}/4\beta}/\sqrt{4\pi\beta}
in our conventions.

Formally, we expect the trumpet path integral to be invariant under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
, but eqn. (
39
) generically does not have this property; under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
,
it is multiplied by
\exp(-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}(\delta-1/2))
.
We have introduced a charge
\delta
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}

in the boundary quantum mechanics. The bulk theory must describe the same charge propagating on the outer boundary.
The Schwarzian mode on the outer boundary carries a charge
\pm\widehat{q}/2=1/2
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}
. So it accounts for a half-unit of boundary charge.
To reproduce the boundary quantum mechanics, an
additional charge
\delta-1/2
on the outer boundary is needed; the bulk theory must have a gauge theory theta-angle
2\pi(\delta-1/2)
, as claimed in eqn. (
39
). This
induces a charge
\delta-1/2
mod
{\mathbb{Z}}
on the outer boundary, which together with the contribution of the Schwarzian mode reproduces the charge in the boundary
quantum mechanics. But this theta-angle will also place a charge
1/2-\delta
on the inner boundary. We have not included the corresponding Wilson operator
on the inner boundary; doing so would give a factor
\exp({\mathrm{i}}\phi(\delta-1/2))
in the path integral, canceling the anomaly.
When we combine two trumpets to make the double trumpet, the charges on the inner boundaries cancel
and the anomaly under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
disappears.

We are ready now to assemble the pieces by gluing together the two trumpets along their common interior geodesic:

\varrho,\varphi
b,\phi
b,-\phi

(67)

Twist or gluing parameters are shown in red.
After integrating over the twist parameters, the path integral on the cylinder is

\displaystyle Z_{0,2}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\pi)\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi\int_{0}^{\infty}b{\mathrm{d}}b~Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};b,\phi)Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{2},\alpha_{2};b,-\phi).

When we glue together the two trumpets, we reverse the orientation of the inner boundary prior to gluing (differently put, the orientation of the geodesic that comes by viewing it as the boundary of one trumpet is opposite to the orientation that comes from viewing it as the boundary of the other trumpet). This has the effect of reversing the sign of
\phi
on one side. In appendix
D
we recall the analogous result for bosonic JT gravity coupled to a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge field
Kapec:2019ecr
.

To compare with the matrix model, it is convenient to use the representation of the trumpet given in (
66
). After integrating over the geodesic length, we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{0,2}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\phi}{2\pi}\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q_{1}-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q_{1}+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q_{2}-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q_{2}+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)

(68)

\displaystyle\times\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi(q_{2}-q_{1})}\,\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{2\pi(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}e^{-\beta_{1}E_{0}(q_{1})-\beta_{2}E_{0}(q_{2})}.

The anomaly under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
has disappeared, as expected.

We now integrate over the internal holonomy. This produces a delta function imposing that
q_{1}=q_{2}\equiv q
. The final answer is

Z_{0,2}=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{2\pi(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})E_{0}(q)}.

(69)

This is consistent with the statistical independence of the Hamiltonian on each supermultiplet, a feature present in the ensemble described in section
2
.
As in the discussion of the disk path integral, the factors
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}
represent a sum over the two states in a supermultiplet, labeled by the average charge
q
.
Going back to the random matrix interpretation in eqn. (
55
), the result (
69
) implies

\displaystyle\langle{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{1}}(e^{-\beta_{1}H})~{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{2}}(e^{-\beta_{2}H})\rangle_{{\rm conn.},g=0}=\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{2\pi(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})E_{0}(q_{1})}\delta_{q_{1},q_{2}}.

(70)

This agrees with the prediction based on the random matrix theory analysis of section
2.3
, since eqn. (
70
) is the prediction of a
\upbeta=2
AZ ensemble if
either
E_{0}(q)>0
(in which case the value of
\upalpha
does not matter) or
E_{0}(q)=0
and

\upalpha
is of order 1 (not of order
e^{S_{0}}
). In
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity,
generically
E_{0}(q)>0
, and when
E_{0}(q)=0
,
\upalpha=1
.

\beta_{1},\alpha_{1}
\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}
\beta_{3},\alpha_{3}

Figure 7:
The leading connected three-boundary wormhole: a three-holed sphere with three NAdS boundaries, each of which is labeled by its
inverse temperature
\beta
and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)

R
-charge chemical potential
\alpha
.

From (
69
), we see that the cylinder path integral receives contributions only from the non-BPS multiplets. Therefore, the numbers

N_{\rm BPS}(k)
of BPS states have trivial dispersion, at least at leading order in
e^{S_{0}}
. This justifies a claim made
in section
3.1
, following eqn. (
48
). The computations in the rest of this article will show that the absence of dispersion in

N_{\rm BPS}(k)
remains true to all orders in the topological expansion; the path integral for every topology except the disk receives contributions only from the non-BPS
multiplets (see footnote
29
).

3.3
Preliminaries on the Three-Boundary Wormhole

So far we have considered the simple cases of the disk and the cylinder, which did not require integrals over complicated moduli space of
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic surfaces. We will study the general case for an arbitrary number of handles and boundaries in the next two sections.

As an introduction, to familiarize the reader with what the final answers look like, we anticipate the result for the leading
three-boundary wormhole. This corresponds to the gravitational path integral
Z_{0,3}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2};\beta_{3},\alpha_{3})
over a spacetime that is a three-holed sphere with three asymptotically NAdS boundaries labeled by inverse temperatures
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}
and chemical potentials
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
; see fig.
7
. The goal is to study to what extent the gravity path integral can be identified with

Z_{0,3}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2};\beta_{3},\alpha_{3})=\big\langle{\rm Tr}(e^{-\beta_{1}H+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}J})~{\rm Tr}(e^{-\beta_{2}H+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}J})~{\rm Tr}(e^{-\beta_{3}H+{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{3}J})\big\rangle_{{\rm conn.},g=0}.

(71)

Similar to the case with two boundaries, if supermultiplets are statistically independent, we expect to obtain that the fixed multiplet connected correlator is zero unless the three multiplets coincide

\big\langle{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{1}}(e^{-\beta_{1}H})~{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{2}}(e^{-\beta_{2}H})~{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{3}}(e^{-\beta_{3}H})\big\rangle_{{\rm conn.},g=0}\propto\delta_{q_{1},q_{2}}\delta_{q_{2},q_{3}},

(72)

In the rest of this section we reproduce this result from gravity. Moreover, we also match the function of multiplet and temperature appearing as a prefactor in the equation above with the prediction from the topological recursion of the AZ matrix ensemble.

We begin as in the previous cases. The first step consists, in
BF
language, in localizing the path integral on flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connections that correspond to
hyperbolic geometries. This guarantees the presence of a geodesic homotopic to each asymptotically NAdS boundary. As in
SSS
, we
can cut along those geodesics and divide the geometry into three trumpets and an internal manifold that is a hyperbolic three-holed sphere with geodesic boundaries.
The path integral on the trumpets was already computed in (
66
). We denote the interior or bulk contribution by

~~~~V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})~~~~\to~~~~\hbox to198.44pt{\vbox to81.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 99.21774pt\lower-38.56233pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-95.88474pt}{31.89365pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{47.5185pt}{31.89365pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.47585pt}{-32.97932pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

(73)

We introduced a notation we will use throughout, where
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
combines the length
b
of a boundary with the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}
around the boundary. The final answer for
Z_{0,3}
is given by gluing this quantity
V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})
to the three trumpets, including the integral over the twist parameters,

\displaystyle Z_{0,3}=\left[\prod_{j=1,2,3}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{j}{\mathrm{d}}b_{j}\int_{0}^{2\pi}2\pi{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{j}\,Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{j},\alpha_{j};\overline{{\sf b}}_{j})\right]V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3}),

where
\overline{{\sf b}}=(b,-\phi)
is the orientation reversed version of
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
. As explained before, one needs to reverse the orientation of the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy around one of the geodesics involved in the gluing; in this expression we chose to apply this reversal on the trumpet. To avoid clutter, we omitted the arguments of
Z_{0,3}
. The factors of
b_{j}
come from the integral over the length-twist parameter while the factors of
2\pi
come from the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-twist parameter.

We leave a detailed derivation of the gravity path integral on spaces with geodesic boundaries and handles for the following two sections. For now we quote the result derived in section
4.2
for the particular case of
V_{0,3}
;

V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}).

(74)

The delta function imposes the constraint
\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}=0
. A flat
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
connection on the three-holed sphere with boundary holonomies
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}

exists if and only if
\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}=0
. Accordingly, in
BF
theory with gauge group
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, we would get a delta function
\delta(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3})
.
In
BF
theory of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, the three-holed sphere has fermionic moduli which appear as corrections to the argument of the delta function.
Integrating over those fermionic moduli produces the two derivatives acting on the delta function. Details of this calculation are spelled out in section
4
.

The overall prefactor in (
74
) depends on the normalization of the
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity path integral measure. The calculation in the next section also clarifies our choice of
normalization.

Accepting for now this result for
V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})
, we are ready to put the pieces together. The answer is actually quite simple. For concreteness, integrate first over the third holonomy
\phi_{3}
. Using the delta function appearing in (
74
), the only remaining dependence on the other holonomies is

e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{1}(q_{1}-q_{3})+{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{2}(q_{2}-q_{3})}.

(75)

We observe again that although the individual
q_{i}
are valued in
\delta+{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
\delta
, the differences
q_{1}-q_{3}
and
q_{2}-q_{3}
are always integers. So the
expression that results from integration over
\phi_{3}
is periodic under
\phi_{1}\sim\phi_{1}+2\pi
and
\phi_{2}\sim\phi_{2}+2\pi
. Next we perform the integrals over
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
, which sets
q_{1}=q_{3}
and
q_{2}=q_{3}
. So the three supermultiplets propagating in the three trumpets are all the same. The final answer after including the trumpet
partition functions and integrating over the three geodesic lengths is given by

\displaystyle Z_{0,3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{1}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{2}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{3}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{3}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)

(76)

\displaystyle~~~~\times(2\pi)\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta_{3}}}{\pi^{3/2}}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3})E_{0}(q)}.

The first line is completely fixed by the structure of the
{\cal N}=2
supermultiplet and the constraint of the three of
them being the same. The second line has more information regarding the details of the matrix model spectral curve.
The factor of
2\pi
is the answer one would obtain from a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge theory on the three-holed sphere.
The factor of
q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}=E_{0}(q)
comes from the details inherent to the
{\cal N}=2
supergravity. The temperature
dependence is similar to the one appearing in bosonic JT gravity. We would like to emphasize that these features are
highly non-trivial consequences of the explicit evaluation. This will become clear from the derivation in the following sections.

The gravity prediction for
\langle{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{1}}(e^{-\beta_{1}H})~{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{2}}(e^{-\beta_{2}H})~{\rm Tr}_{{\mathrm{M}},q_{3}}(e^{-\beta_{3}H})\rangle_{{\rm conn.},g=0}
,
the random matrix partition function correlator for fixed supermultiplet, is:

\displaystyle(2\pi)E_{0}(q_{3})\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta_{3}}}{\pi^{3/2}}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3})E_{0}(q_{3})}\delta_{q_{1},q_{2}}\delta_{q_{2},q_{3}}.

(77)

Consistent with the discussion in section
2
, the different supermultiplets are statistically independent.
The temperature dependence reproduces precisely the matrix model topological recursion for an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)

ensemble, with
\nu
either zero or proportional to
e^{S_{0}}
, applied to the spectral curve (
52
). We leave it for the reader to verify this, by a
straightforward application of the matrix model loop equation presented in appendix
B
.
In the next two sections we will provide a procedure to compute the gravitational path integral of
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity for any topology and
prove to all orders in the genus expansion the match with the random matrix topological recursion. This check will therefore become a particular case of a more general analysis.

4
Gravity Path Integral for Spaces with Wormholes

4.1
The Path Integral of
BF
Theory

With the exception of the disk, which we have already studied,
any oriented NAdS two-manifold can be built by gluing trumpets onto an “interior” manifold, a hyperbolic two-manifolds with geodesic boundary.
This decomposition leads to a convenient evaluation of the path integral of JT gravity
SSS
. This analysis has been to
{\mathcal{N}}=1
JT supergravity
SW
,
and here we will describe the generalization to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
.

The path integral on the trumpet requires special treatment, but in any event we have already discussed this, in arriving at eqn. (
65
).
The remaining JT gravity path integral on the interior manifold
C
is naturally formulated in
BF
theory. The action of
BF
theory with group
G
,
on a surface
Y
, is
I=-{\mathrm{i}}\int_{Y}{\rm Tr}\,BF
.
B
is an adjoint-valued spin zero field and
F={\mathrm{d}}A+A\wedge A
is the field strength of a gauge connection
A
.
Integrating over
B
produces a delta function
\delta(F)
, localizing the path integral to the moduli space
15
15

15

We use the same letter
{\mathcal{T}}
for the full moduli
space of flat connections, and for the geometric component relevant for gravity. We hope this will not cause confusion. The motivation for the name
{\mathcal{T}}
is that in the case of
gauge group
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
,
{\mathcal{T}}
is an
{\mathcal{N}}=2
version of Teichmüller space.

{\mathcal{T}}
of flat connections on
C
modulo gauge transformations.
In defining
{\mathcal{T}}
, one wants to specify the conjugacy classes of the geodesics around the boundaries of
C
; then in gluing onto the external trumpets, one integrates
over those holonomies.
In the
BF
description, the boundary holonomy is specified by fixing the gauge connection
A
along the boundary.
In gravity one has to further reduce to a component of
{\mathcal{T}}
that is related to hyperbolic metrics, and also mod out by the mapping class group.
In the present section, we do not consider such global questions, and merely aim to
determine the appropriate measure on
{\mathcal{T}}
, deferring global questions to section
5
.

To derive the measure on the moduli space, one can expand the path integral around a chosen flat connection
A_{0}
, which represents a point in
\mathcal{T}
. After
integrating over
B
to get the delta function
\delta(F)=\delta({\mathrm{d}}A+A\wedge A)
, one integrates over fluctuations in
A
and over the ghosts that are introduced
for gauge-fixing. With a standard gauge choice, this leads to a measure
\mu
on
{\mathcal{T}}
that differs from a classical measure
\mu_{0}
by a certain ratio of determinants:

\displaystyle\mu=\mu_{0}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{{\rm det}^{\prime}\Delta_{0}}}{\sqrt{{\rm det}^{\prime}\Delta_{2}}}.

(78)

In eqn. (
78
),
\Delta_{q}=D^{*}D+DD^{*}
is the Laplacian acting on adjoint-valued
q
-forms, constructed from the exterior derivative
D={\mathrm{d}}+[A_{0},\cdot]
and its adjoint
D^{*}
.
The numerator arises from the ghost path integral, while the denominator arises from the integral over
A
. The primes denote the removal of zero-modes.
One way to describe the classical measure
\mu_{0}
is the following:
{\mathcal{T}}
is naturally a Riemannian manifold,
16
16

16

For compact
G
, if
one imposes the harmonic gauge condition
{\mathrm{d}}\star\delta A=0
on a zero-mode
\delta A
of
A
(where
\star
is the Hodge star),
then the metric on the space of zero-modes of
A
and the Riemannian structure of
{\mathcal{T}}
are defined
by
|\delta A|^{2}=\int_{C}{\mathrm{Tr}}\,\delta A\wedge\star\delta A
. For non-compact
G
, this definition needs to be slightly modified.

and
\mu_{0}
is the Riemannian measure on
{\mathcal{T}}
;
physically,
\mu_{0}
is the natural measure on the space of zero-modes of
A
.

On an oriented two-manifold, an alternative description of
\mu_{0}
is generally more useful.
If
C
is oriented, then
{\mathcal{T}}
is a symplectic manifold and
\mu_{0}
is its natural symplectic measure.
In addition, if
C
is oriented, then
the ratio
\frac{\sqrt{{\rm det}^{\prime}\Delta_{0}}}{\sqrt{{\rm det}^{\prime}\Delta_{2}}}
is equal to 1 because Hodge duality between 0-forms and 2-forms implies
that
\Delta_{0}
and
\Delta_{2}
have the same spectrum. Therefore, in this case, the quantum measure
\mu
coincides with the classical measure
\mu_{0}
.

Yet another way to look at the measure
\mu
that comes from
BF
theory is as follows.
On any two-manifold, orientable or not, this measure is the analytic torsion of Ray and Singer
RaySinger
. This observation, or more precisely its generalization to
any spacetime dimension, was made by Schwarz
Schwarz:1978cn
.
In any dimension, analytic torsion is a certain ratio of determinants times a classical measure
\mu_{0}
on the space of zero-modes; in two dimensions,
the definition reduces to precisely what we have written
in eqn. (
78
).
Analytic torsion is equivalent to combinatorial torsion, originally defined by Reidemeister
Reidemeister
, as was proved in

Cheeger
;
Muller1
;
Muller2
;
BismutZhang
. In physical terms, this means that
BF
theory has the unusual property that it can be defined by a lattice theory with
a result that is completely independent of the choice of lattice. By taking a very fine lattice, one can motivate the idea that the lattice theory has a continuum limit (namely
BF
theory).
On the other hand, one can choose a very crude lattice so that the path integral can be directly calculated, leading to explicit formulas.

Although the measure
\mu_{0}
in eqn. (
78
)
can be understood as a Riemannian measure, as far as we know this does not lead to an effective method of calculation. On an orientable two-manifold,
the symplectic interpretation of
\mu_{0}
definitely does lead to an effective method of calculation. However, as far as we know, such calculations are not simpler than
calculations based on the torsion. We have chosen to base our calculations on the relationship of the measure to the torsion, because this is directly related to the physics,
as the derivation of eqn. (
78
) showed, and because this approach is equally valid on orientable or unorientable two-manifolds.

In this section and the next one, we consider orientable two-manifolds only. Unorientable two-manifolds are considered in section
6
.
Any orientable two-manifold can be constructed by gluing three-holed spheres along their boundaries. By determining the moduli space measure for the three-holed sphere
as well as the procedure for gluing, we can determine the moduli space measure for an arbitrary oriented two-manifold.

The computation of the torsion of a three-holed sphere
and the gluing procedure were described in
SW
, section 3, in a way that is valid for any possibly non-compact gauge group or supergroup. (The examples considered in that
paper were
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathrm{OSp}}(1|2)
.) Here we will not repeat those general explanations and will just apply the procedure for the gauge group
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
that
is relevant to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity.

There is a subtlety in the statement that the symplectic structure and the torsion define the same measure. On an oriented two-manifold
C
without boundary, this is true as stated:
if
{\mathcal{T}}_{g}
is the moduli space of flat connections with gauge group
G
on a closed surface of genus
g
, then the symplectic structure and the torsion define the same measure

\mu
on
{\mathcal{T}}_{g}
(assuming the symplectic structure is properly normalized). On the other hand, if
C
is an oriented two-manifold of genus
g
with
n
boundary circles, then the symplectic structure and the torsion define measures on two closely related but slightly different spaces.
The symplectic structure determines a measure
\mu
on what we will call
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}
(or
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}(\vec{w})
if we wish to be more precise), the moduli space of flat bundles with
prescribed conjugacy classes
\vec{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
of the holonomies
around the boundaries. The torsion defines a measure
\tau
on what we will call
{\mathcal{R}}_{g,n}
, the moduli space of flat bundles
over
C
without a restriction on the boundary holonomies. (In the case of gravity, we restrict the holonomies to be hyperbolic, but
\tau
can be defined without this restriction.)
The two spaces and measures are closely related, and in the case of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity, we will ultimately determine the precise relationship in eqn. (
163
).

4.2
Measure On Moduli Space of
{\cal N}=2
Hyperbolic Surfaces

In this section, after some preliminaries, we determine the torsion for a three-holed sphere, the torsion for a circle and the associated gluing procedure, and the final answer for
the measure that comes from
the
BF
path integral on an arbitrary orientable surface.

4.2.1
JT Gravity as a
BF
Theory

We start by reviewing some aspects of
BF
theory with gauge group
{\rm SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
on an oriented two-manifold
C
. We study flat connections on
C
,
with the proviso that if
C
has a non-trivial boundary, then
the holonomy of the flat connection around any boundary component is a hyperbolic
element of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, that is, an element that is conjugate to

\displaystyle U_{b}^{\pm}=\pm\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{b/2}&0\\
0&e^{-b/2}\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),

with some
b>0
. With this specification, the
moduli space of flat
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
connections on
C
has several disconnected components, one of which parametrizes hyperbolic metrics on
C
that have
curvature
R=-2
and such that, for any given boundary, the corresponding parameter
b
is the circumference of that boundary.
17
17

17

The group
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
is contractible onto
its maximal compact subgroup
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, so topologically any
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
bundle over
C

is equivalent to a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
bundle and therefore has a first Chern class. (This first Chern class can be expressed as an integral over
C
, once one
picks a reduction of the structure group of the bundle from
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
to
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.) If
C
is a closed oriented surface of genus
g
, then the values of the first Chern class of a flat

{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
bundle over
C

range from
g-1
to
-(g-1)
, and hyperbolic metrics on
C
are related to the component of first Chern class
g-1
. The first Chern class can still be defined if
C

is an oriented two-manifold with boundary and the flat connection on
C
is constrained to have hyperbolic conjugacy around the boundary, because hyperbolic holonomy around
a given boundary component reduces
the structure group of the flat bundle along that boundary to a contractible subgroup (the one-parameter subgroup of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
that contains the holonomy around
the given boundary). Again the component of
{\mathcal{T}}

with maximum possible first Chern class is the one related to hyperbolic metrics on
C
.
That is the component that we will study.

In purely bosonic JT gravity, the sign in eqn. (
4.2.1
) is irrelevant. One can take the gauge group to be
{\mathrm{PSL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and disregard this sign.
In a theory with fermions, one wants to endow
C
with a spin structure as well as a hyperbolic metric. In that case, one must take the gauge group to be
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})

and the sign in eqn. (
4.2.1
) is meaningful: it determines the spin structure on the given boundary component of
C
.

Figure 8:
Decomposition of a genus 2 surface as a union of three-holed spheres
Y_{1},

Y_{2}
, glued on circles
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
.

The flat connections on
C
that are related to hyperbolic metrics on
C
have hyperbolic holonomy around any homotopically nontrivial embedded
loop in
C
(not just the boundaries
of
C
). As a result, if we “cut”
C
along some embedded circles to decompose it into a union of three-holed spheres, there will be hyperbolic
holonomy around each of the boundaries of these three-holed spheres. The loops along which we cut can always be chosen to be geodesics, so the cutting decomposes
C
into
a union of three-holed spheres with geodesic boundaries. In brief, we will call these geodesic three-holed spheres. In fig.
8
, we give an example of
building a genus 2 surface by gluing together two three-holed spheres.

In
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity, the gauge group is
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
(or more precisely a
\widehat{q}
-fold cover thereof). Since a maximal
bosonic subgroup of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
is
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)\cong{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, we will briefly discuss
BF
theory of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
before
studying
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.

BF
theories based on
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times\widetilde{G}
, with any compact
\widetilde{G}
, were studied in
Kapec:2019ecr
, the main result being a match
with a random matrix theory in which the different representations of
\widetilde{G}
are statistically independent.
The case
\widetilde{G}={\mathrm{U}}(1)
, relevant to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity, is important for the present article and has special properties because
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is abelian.
In
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge theory, the holonomy around a given boundary can be an arbitrary element
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi}\in{\mathrm{U}}(1)
. If
C
has
n
boundary components, then
the holonomies
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{1}},\cdots,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{n}}
around these boundaries cannot be chosen arbitrarily. A flat connection on
C
with these holonomies
exists if and only if

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}=0~{\mathrm{mod}}~2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

(82)

This constraint, which is slightly deformed in the case of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, will be important in what follows.

{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, the gauge group relevant to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity,
can be presented
18
18

18

{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{OSp}}(2|2)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, so it is also related to the group of linear transformations of
a vector space of dimension
2|2
that preserves a certain symplectic form. This will be important in section
5
when we derive the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
analog of
Mirzakhani’s recursion relation for volumes. But except in that application, we use the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
description, since it is easier to calculate with
3\times 3
matrices.
as the group of linear transformations of a vector space
V
of dimension
2|1

that preserves a certain hermitian inner product. This inner product can be described by saying that for

a,b|\eta\in V
,

\displaystyle|(a,b|\eta)|^{2}={\mathrm{i}}(a\overline{b}-b\overline{a})+2{\mathrm{i}}\overline{\eta}\eta.

(83)

For bosonic generators of
\mathfrak{su}(1,1|1)
, we can choose

\displaystyle{\sf e}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&1&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf f}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
1&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf h}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}1&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&-1&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf z}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\mathrm{i}}&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&{\mathrm{i}}&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt2{\mathrm{i}}\end{array}\right).

The first three generators satisfy the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
algebra
[{\sf h},{\sf e}]=2{\sf e}
,
[{\sf h},{\sf f}]=-2{\sf f}
and
[{\sf e},{\sf f]}={\sf h}
, while
{\sf z}
is the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
generator and
accordingly commutes with
{\sf e},{\sf f}
, and
{\sf h}
. The
R
-charge generator
J
that was introduced in section
2.3
is represented in this basis by
J=-{\mathrm{i}}{\sf z}
(the
following formulas will be slightly simplified by using
\sf z
rather than
J
). We have chosen
{\sf e},{\sf f},{\sf h},
and
{\sf z}
so that they generate symmetries of the hermitian form
(
83
). Therefore, they are generators of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
; in Lorentz signature, they are represented by anti-hermitian operators, whose exponentials are unitary.

The four fermionic generators of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
are complex linear combinations of

\displaystyle{\sf q}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&1&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf q}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt1\\
0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf q}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
\hline\cr 1&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right),~{\sf q}_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\\
0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt1\\
\hline\cr 0&0&\hskip 1.42262pt0\end{array}\right).

One can work out the commutators of the
{\sf q}_{i}
with the bosonic generators defined in eqn. (
4.2.1
) and their anticommutators with each other, but we omit
these formulas.

In eqn. (
4.2.1
), we have simply picked a convenient basis for the four-dimensional space of fermionic linear transformations of the vector space
V
. Certain complex
linear combinations of the
{\sf q}_{i}
preserve the quadratic form (
83
) and therefore are generators of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
. However, for the Euclidean signature calculation
that we will perform, the existence of such a real basis for the fermionic generators is not important, though the real structure of the maximal bosonic subgroup
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is
important. We will pause to explain this point, which sometimes causes confusion.

First of all, in any quantum field theory with or without gravity,
in Lorentz signature, any group or supergroup
G
of symmetries always has a real structure. The reason for this is that elements of
G
act on the quantum
Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
of the theory as unitary transformations. This statement only makes sense if
G
has a real structure. One can (with some restrictions on the states
a given group element can act on)
analytically continue the action of
G
on
{\mathcal{H}}
to an action of the complexification
G_{\mathbb{C}}
of
G
, but only elements of
G
are unitary; elements of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
that are not
in
G
act on
{\mathcal{H}}
as (densely defined) unbounded and nonunitary operators.

This remark applies to any theory, supersymmetric or not. In the case of a supersymmetric theory, after Wick rotation to Euclidean signature, generically the fermionic
generators do not carry any real structure. For example, consider
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetry in four dimensions. This superalgebra has four fermionic generators, and,
although they carry a real structure in Lorentz signature, they do not admit any real structure in Euclidean structure (they take values in a pseudoreal rather than real
representation of the rotation group
{\mathrm{Spin}}(4)
).

More broadly, even without supersymmetry, in Euclidean signature, fermionic integration variables in general do not carry any real structure. For example, in the standard model of particle physics – because of the chiral nature of the weak interactions – after Wick rotation to Euclidean signature, there is no way to define a real structure for the fermionic
variables. This causes no difficulty, because fermionic integration is a purely algebraic operation. When one says that
\int{\mathrm{d}}\psi\,\psi=1
,
\int{\mathrm{d}}\psi\,1=0
,
there is no need to know whether
\psi
can be considered “real” (and if the answer to that question is “no,” as it is in the standard model or many four-dimensional
models with
{\mathcal{N}}=1

supersymmetry, there is no need to apologize and add a complex conjugate variable
\psi^{*}
).

By contrast, for the bosonic variables, one does need a real structure in Euclidean signature in order to define the appropriate integration cycle. This statement generalizes
the following fact about 1-dimensional integrals: if
F(z)
is a holomorphic function on
{\mathbb{C}}
(possibly with some singularities), then to make sense of an integral

\int_{\gamma}F(z){\mathrm{d}}z
, one needs to pick an integration cycle
\gamma\subset{\mathbb{C}}
. For example, in general, the integral of
F(z){\mathrm{d}}z
on the real
z
-axis gives a different result
from the “same” integral on the imaginary
z
-axis, even if both of these integrals converge.

The measure that we will compute using the relation of
BF
theory to torsion makes sense as a holomorphic measure on the moduli space of flat connections
on
C
with structure group
{\mathrm{SL}}(1,1|1)_{\mathbb{C}}
, the complexification of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.
To integrate this measure, we need a real form for the bosonic integration variables but we do not need a real form for the fermionic integration variables.
When we say that the gauge group we work with is
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, we are really specifying the complex Lie algebra of the gauge group and the real form
that we will use in integrating over the bosons, which will be a maximal bosonic subgroup
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)\cong{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.

At this stage, the reader may wonder why the supergroup
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
that is appropriate for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity in Euclidean signature admits a real structure
for the fermionic generators, given that this does not happen in a generic supersymmetric theory. This fact can be viewed as an accident
of two dimensions. The connected component of the symmetry group of
\mathrm{AdS}_{2}
in Lorentz signature is
{\mathrm{SO}}(2,1)
; Wick rotation converts this
to
\mathrm{SO}(1,2)
, which happens to be isomorphic to
\mathrm{SO}(2,1)
. As a result of this equivalence, after including spin and fermionic generators,
one gets the same supergroup
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
in either Lorentz or Euclidean signature. Since it is always possible to define a real structure in Lorentz signature,
it follows that in this theory one can also do so in Euclidean signature. In dimension
d
, the two groups would be
\mathrm{SO}(2,d-1)
and
\mathrm{SO}(1,d)
,
which are no longer isomorphic. For some values of
d
, a superextension of this symmetry group will not have any real structure in Euclidean signature.

Going back to
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, although this supergroup does admit a real structure for the fermionic generators, it will be more convenient in our calculations to ignore
this fact and make no use of this real structure.

In this discussion, we have implicitly assumed that
\widehat{q}=1
. To incorporate
\widehat{q}
, we want to take a
\widehat{q}
-fold cover of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
and of its maximal
bosonic subgroup
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
. The cover is defined by taking a
\widehat{q}
-fold cover of the second factor
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
. Taking this cover means that the fermionic generators
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
have
R
-charge that is greater than the minimum possible
R
-charge of any operator by a factor
\pm\widehat{q}
.

4.2.2
The Three-Holed Sphere

The ultimate goal of this section is to compute the path integral measure for the component of the moduli of flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connections on an orientable surface
C

associated to hyperbolic surfaces with
{\cal N}=2
supersymmetry. Any orientable surface can be constructed by gluing together three-holed spheres along some of their boundaries, which are circles. So to obtain a general result, we will
need to know the torsion of a flat connection on a three-holed sphere or a circle, and the procedure for gluing.

U
V
W
Y
\to
W
U
V
P
Y

Figure 9:
On the left we show the hyperbolic three-holed sphere
Y
with geodesic boundaries labeled by
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
holonomies
U
,
V
and
W
. On the right we show a topological space given by the interior of the curves labeled by the same three holonomies. The combinatorial torsion is conveniently calculated using the picture on the right.

We denote as
\mathcal{R}
the moduli “space” of flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connections on a three-holed sphere
Y
, with the boundary holonomies required to be hyperbolic
but not otherwise restricted. As we will see,
{\mathcal{R}}
is a rather subtle sort of space, not a supermanifold.
We denote the holonomies of a flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connection on
Y
around the boundaries as
U,V,W
; see fig.
9
.
Actually, to define the holonomies as group elements (not just conjugacy classes), one needs to pick a base-point in the definition of the fundamental group and to choose, for each boundary circle, a corresponding element of the fundamental group. A way to do this is given in the right panel of the figure. From this picture, it is hopefully obvious that, as
UVW
is the holonomy around
a contractible loop in
Y
, any flat connection on
Y
will satisfy

\displaystyle UVW=1.

(110)

We can solve this relation for
W
:
W=V^{-1}U^{-1}
.
U
and
V
are not subject to any constraint, so we can pick an arbitrary pair
U,V\in{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, and this
will determine a flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connection on
Y
. This parametrization is redundant, however:
a conjugation
(U,V,W)\cong(RUR^{-1},RVR^{-1},RWR^{-1})
, which one can think of as resulting from a gauge transformation that equals
R
at the base-point
P

that was used in defining the fundamental group, preserves constraint (
110
) and is associated to a gauge-equivalent flat connection.

We now present the parametrization of
\mathcal{R}
which we use in the torsion computation. Given a pair
(U,V)\in{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, we cannot conjugate them
to be simultaneously diagonal, since they do not commute, but
we can conjugate them to a pair
(U_{0},V_{0})
such that
U_{0}
is upper triangular and
V_{0}
is lower triangular:

U_{0}=\hskip-2.84526pt\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}e^{\frac{a}{2}}&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}\kappa&0\\
0&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}e^{-\frac{a}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right)e^{\psi_{2}{\sf q}_{2}}e^{\psi_{4}{\sf q}_{4}},~V_{0}=\hskip-2.84526pt\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}e^{-\frac{b}{2}}&0&0\\
-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}e^{\frac{b}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right)e^{\psi_{1}{\sf q}_{1}}e^{\psi_{3}{\sf q}_{3}}.

(111)

Every pair
(U,V)
is conjugate to a unique such pair
(U_{0},V_{0})
. The triangular nature of
U_{0},V_{0}
will simplify the calculation considerably.
19
19

19

It is to achieve this simplification
that, as discussed in section
4.2.1
, we ignore the real structure that could be imposed on the fermionic generators.
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
actually has a real Borel
subgroup that could be used to get somewhat similar simplifications in a real basis. (For this, one would define “upper triangular” or “lower triangular” generators to be those with
a positive or negative eigenvalue of
{\sf h}
.) It does not appear that this would lead to simpler calculations.

The boundary parameters
(a,b,\phi_{a},\phi_{b},\kappa)
are the bosonic moduli of
{\mathcal{R}}
.
{\mathcal{R}}
also has four “internal” fermionic moduli
\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{4}
. The torsion
\tau_{Y}
will be holomorphic in all of these
parameters. When ultimately we integrate over moduli (usually after gluing
Y
onto other three-holed spheres and/or external trumpets)
a,b,\phi_{a},\phi_{b},
and
\kappa
are all taken to be real,
20
20

20

It is actually more natural to say that the parameters
a,b,\cdots
are real modulo nilpotents. The reason is that in general the same oriented two-manifold
C

can be decomposed in three-holed spheres in many different ways. The different sets of variables
a,b,\cdots
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},\cdots
associated to these
decompositions are related by a change of variables
that is real modulo nilpotents but not actually real.
So we cannot
in an invariant way say that the bosonic parameters are real, but we can say that they are real modulo nilpotents, which is good enough so that the moduli
space integrals make sense. See for example
Witten:2012bg
for discussion of such issues.
but we do not invoke a real structure for the fermionic parameters
\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{4}
.
As usual, we actually work with a
\widehat{q}
-fold cover of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, so
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{b}
are defined
mod
2\pi
(not
2\pi/\widehat{q}
as the formulas in
3\times 3
matrices suggest).

If two boundary holonomies are
U_{0}
and
V_{0}
, then the third is
W_{0}=V_{0}^{-1}U_{0}^{-1}
.
In the parametrization (
111
) of the moduli space,

a
and
b
are the geodesic lengths of the
U_{0}
and
V_{0}
boundaries while
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{b}
parametrize their
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies. But what is the interpretation of
\kappa
? We would like to replace it instead by the geodesic length of the boundary with holonomy
W_{0}
; we denote this length by
c
. The length and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy of
W_{0}

can be determined by conjugating
W_{0}
to be diagonal. Thus, if there is
D\in{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
such that

\displaystyle DW_{0}D^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{c}}e^{-\frac{c}{2}}&0&0\\
0&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{c}}e^{\frac{c}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{c}}\end{array}\right)

then the
W_{0}
boundary has circumference
c
and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
twist parameter
\phi_{c}
.
The straightforward but tedious calculation to determine
c
and
\phi_{c}
is presented
in appendix
C
. Before quoting the final result, it is instructive to consider the simpler case with vanishing fermionic moduli
\psi_{1}=\cdots=\psi_{4}=0
. In this case the matrix
W_{0}=V_{0}^{-1}U_{0}^{-1}
is

\displaystyle W_{0}\Big|_{\psi=0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{\frac{b-a}{2}}&-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{\frac{b}{2}}\kappa&0\\
-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{-\frac{a}{2}}&e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}(e^{\frac{a-b}{2}}+\kappa)&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{-2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}\end{array}\right).

This case simplifies since
W_{0}
is block diagonal. From the bottom right matrix element of eqn. (
4.2.2
), we can read off that
\phi_{c}=-\phi_{a}-\phi_{b}
. Equivalently,

\displaystyle\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}=0~~~~{\rm for}~\psi_{i}=0,~i=1,\ldots,4.

(120)

Thus the boundary holonomies cannot be specified independently, or the moduli space will be empty.
We essentially already met this constraint in eqn. (
82
); it reflects the fact that when we set the odd moduli to zero, the structure group of a flat

{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connection reduces to the maximal bosonic subgroup
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, in which the second factor is abelian.
The
2\times 2
block in
W_{0}
is

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{\frac{b-a}{2}}&~-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{\frac{b}{2}}\kappa\\
-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}e^{-\frac{a}{2}}&~e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}(e^{\frac{a-b}{2}}+\kappa)\end{array}\right)=-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{c}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-e^{\frac{b-a}{2}}&~e^{\frac{b}{2}}\kappa\\
e^{-\frac{a}{2}}&~-(e^{\frac{a-b}{2}}+\kappa)\end{array}\right).

The matrix on the right, after removing a factor of
-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{c}}
, has eigenvalues
e^{\pm c/2}
, so its trace is
2\cosh\frac{c}{2}
.
Computing the trace, we learn that with all fermionic moduli set to zero,
\kappa=\kappa_{0}
with

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}=-2\cosh\Big(\frac{c}{2}\Big)-2\cosh\Big(\frac{b-a}{2}\Big),~~~{\rm for}~\psi_{i}=0,~i=1,\ldots,4.

(126)

When the odd moduli vanish, these relations determine the parameters
c,\phi_{c}
that parametrize the conjugacy class of
W_{0}
.

Conceptually, with non-vanishing fermionic moduli, one proceeds in the same way. The only modification is that now the right hand sides of (
120
) and (
126
) get nilpotent contributions proportional to the fermionic moduli.
This will modify both the result for
\kappa
and the constraint
\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}=0
.
The most general possible form of this modification is

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}+\sum_{i<j}\kappa_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\kappa_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4},

(127)

\displaystyle\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}f_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+f_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}.

(128)

In appendix
C
, we derive explicit expressions for the coefficients
\kappa_{ij}
and
f_{ij}
,
i,j=1,\ldots,4
, that appear in these relations. The non-zero
f
’s are

\displaystyle f_{12}=-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}}{4\widehat{q}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},~~~f_{14}=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}(e^{-\frac{b}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}-e^{-\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}})}{4\widehat{q}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},

\displaystyle f_{23}=-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}(e^{\frac{b}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}-e^{\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}})}{4\widehat{q}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},~~~f_{34}=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cosh(\frac{a-b}{2}))}{2\widehat{q}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},

\displaystyle f_{4}=-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}e^{-2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}(1+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}\cosh(\frac{c}{2}))}{8\widehat{q}(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))^{2}}.

(129)

and the nonzero
\kappa
’s are

\displaystyle\kappa_{12}=\frac{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}\cosh(\frac{c}{2})}{2(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},

\displaystyle\kappa_{14}=-\frac{e^{-\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+(e^{-\frac{b}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+e^{-\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}})\cosh(\frac{c}{2})}{2(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))}

\displaystyle\kappa_{23}=\frac{e^{\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+(e^{\frac{b}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+e^{\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}})\cosh(\frac{c}{2})}{2(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))},

\displaystyle\kappa_{34}=\frac{(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cosh(\frac{a-b}{2}))(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}})}{\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})}.

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}=\frac{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}(\phi_{b}-\phi_{a})}(1+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b})\cosh(\frac{c}{2}))}{4(\cosh(\frac{c}{2})+\cos\widehat{q}(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}))^{2}}.

(130)

It will be convenient for future reference to define the nilpotent function

\displaystyle K=\sum_{i<j}k_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+k_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4},~~~~k_{ij}=-\frac{\partial_{c}\kappa_{ij}}{\sinh(\frac{c}{2})},~~k_{4}=-\frac{\partial_{c}\kappa_{4}}{\sinh(\frac{c}{2})}.

(131)

The coefficients on the right hand side can be easily extracted from (
130
). We define this function
K
since it will appear in the final expression for the torsion.

At this point, however, we should point out why
{\mathcal{R}}
is not a supermanifold and instead is a rather strange kind of “space.” Because
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is abelian,
a flat
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
connection on
Y
with

\psi_{1}=\cdots\psi_{4}=0
is always invariant under the
R
-symmetry group
{\mathrm{U}}(1)\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
. Accordingly, let
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
be the supermanifold of dimension
5|4

parametrized by
a,b,\phi_{a},\phi_{b},\kappa|\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\psi_{4}
. Then, formally,
{\mathcal{R}}
is the quotient
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}/{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, with
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
acting only on
\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{4}
.
The trouble with this is that dividing a supermanifold, in this case
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, by a continuous group, in this case
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, that acts only on odd variables is not a nice operation,
and the quotient
{\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}/{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is definitely not a supermanifold. We do not want to define directly what should be meant by a volume form on
{\mathcal{R}}
or by integration on
{\mathcal{R}}
.
Pragmatically, we want to define the volume of
{\mathcal{R}}
as
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathcal{R}}^{\prime})/{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathrm{U}}(1))
. For this purpose, we need volume forms on
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, or at least we need the
ratio of a volume-form on
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
and a volume form on
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
. We will see that the torsion gives precisely such a ratio.

4.2.3
Torsion of the Three-Holed Sphere

We are now ready to compute the torsion
\tau_{Y}
of the three-holed sphere
Y
. We will follow the general procedure described in section 3.4.2 of
SW
. We will
review a few steps, emphasizing the points that are different for
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, but we will not give a self-contained explanation.

Since the torsion can be computed using any triangulation (or more generally any cell decomposition) of
a manifold, we can use the very simple description of a three-holed sphere shown in the right panel of fig.
9
, with one two-cell, three boundary one-cells, and
one vertex
P
. Let us discuss
the situation with an arbitrary gauge group
G
before specializing to
G={\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.
If we divide the space of flat connections on
Y

not by all gauge transformations but only by gauge transformations that are trivial at
P
, we get a supermanifold
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}\cong G\times G
that is parametrized
by
U,V
without any constraint or equivalence. Then to get the moduli space
{\mathcal{R}}
, we divide by
G
acting on
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
by conjugation:

{\mathcal{R}}=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}/G.

(132)

The definition of the torsion requires a choice of a left- and right-invariant measure
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}
on the
G
manifold; this is equivalent to a choice of invariant measure
{\mathrm{vol}}_{\mathfrak{g}}

on the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of
G
. Such a choice determines a measure on any copy of the group manifold,
so in particular it determines measures
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(U)
or
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(V)
on the copies of
G
parametrized by
U
or
V
.
Similarly, if we define
U=RU_{0}R^{-1}
,
V=RV_{0}R^{-1}
, with some choices of
U_{0}
,
V_{0}
(which in our application will be given by eqn. (
111
)), one
has a measure
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)
on the copy of
G
parametrized by
R
.
The torsion can be used to define
measures
\tau_{Y}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{Y}
on
{\mathcal{R}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
, respectively. One finds

\widehat{\tau}_{Y}={\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(U){\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(V)

(133)

and

\tau_{Y}=\frac{\widehat{\tau}_{Y}}{{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)}=\frac{{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(U){\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(V)}{{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)}.

(134)

It suffices to evaluate this formula for
\tau_{Y}
at
R=1
, since every flat connection on
Y
can be described by some pair
(U_{0},V_{0})
.

When the quotient
{\mathcal{R}}=\widehat{\mathcal{R}}/G
is sufficiently nice, which happens for the examples
{\mathrm{SL}}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\text{OSp}(1|2)
studied in
SW
, the quotient
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(U){\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(V)/{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)

is a measure on
{\mathcal{R}}
and this is the appropriate moduli space measure. For the example of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
that we aim to understand here, it turns out instead
that
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(U){\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(V)/{{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)}
is the ratio of a measure on
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, defined at the end of section
4.2.2
, and a measure on the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)

R
-symmetry group.
We interpret this to mean that, as essentially stated in section
4.2.2
, integration over
{\mathcal{R}}
should be defined as integration over
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
divided by the volume of
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.

To evaluate the torsion, we first describe the volume form that we will use on
\mathfrak{su}(1,1|1)
.
Consider an arbitrary Lie algebra element
x\in\mathfrak{su}(1,1|1)
with components
x=x_{e}{\sf e}+x_{f}{\sf f}+x_{h}{\sf h}+x_{z}\widehat{q}\,{\sf z}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}x_{j}{\sf q}_{j}
. (Notice the factor of
\widehat{q}
in the normalization in
x_{z}
, such that the range of
x_{z}
is
2\pi
in the
\widehat{q}
-fold of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
we are working with.)
We choose the volume form on the Lie algebra to be

{\mathrm{vol}}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}=4[{\mathrm{d}}x_{e}{\mathrm{d}}x_{f}{\mathrm{d}}x_{h}{\mathrm{d}}x_{z}|{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}{\mathrm{d}}x_{2}{\mathrm{d}}x_{3}{\mathrm{d}}x_{4}],

(135)

and this also determines the volume form on the group. As explained in
SW
, a symbol such as
[{\mathrm{d}}y|{\mathrm{d}}\eta]
(where
y
is bosonic and
\eta
is fermionic)
represents the measure associated to a basis

{\mathrm{d}}y|{\mathrm{d}}\eta
of 1-forms on a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-graded vector space.
A crucial fact is that in contrast to bosons, measures for fermions transform oppositely to differential forms, in the sense that for
21
21

21

The reason
for the absolute value
|\lambda|
in eqn (
136
) in the bosonic case is that for a bosonic variable
y
, the measure
[{\mathrm{d}}y]
, as opposed to the 1-form
{\mathrm{d}}y
, is invariant under

y\to-y
.

\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

[\lambda{\mathrm{d}}y|{\mathrm{d}}\eta]=|\lambda|\,[{\mathrm{d}}y|{\mathrm{d}}\eta],~~~~[{\mathrm{d}}y|\lambda{\mathrm{d}}\eta]=\lambda^{-1}[{\mathrm{d}}y|{\mathrm{d}}\eta].

(136)

Evaluating
{\rm vol}_{G}(R)
is immediate, because it suffices to evaluate this measure at
R=1
. If we write
R=1+r
then, at
R=1
,
R^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}R={\mathrm{d}}r
and

{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)
reduces to

\displaystyle{\rm vol}_{G}(R)=4[{\mathrm{d}}r_{e}{\mathrm{d}}r_{f}{\mathrm{d}}r_{h}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}|{\mathrm{d}}r_{1}{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}{\mathrm{d}}r_{4}].

(137)

Next we evaluate
{\rm vol}_{G}(U)
for
U=RU_{0}R^{-1}
, following the procedure outlined in
SW
.
The quantity
U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U
will get contributions both from the differential of
R
, resulting in terms proportional to components of
{\mathrm{d}}r
which will cancel similar factors in

{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)
when evaluating eqn. (
134
), and contributions from the differential of
U_{0}
. When
we repeat this for
V=RV_{0}R^{-1}
, we will be left finally with
\tau=F[{\mathrm{d}}a{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\kappa{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}|{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{2}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{4}]/[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
for some function
F
.
As anticipated at the end of section

4.2.2
, we interpret the result as the ratio of a volume form on
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
and a volume form on
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.

The identity (
134
) can be greatly simplified by exploiting the fact that
the matrices
U_{0}
and
V_{0}
are respectively upper and lower triangular. Begin by evaluating the
q_{3}
component of
U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U
and the
q_{2}
component of
V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V
:

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{3}=-(e^{\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1){\mathrm{d}}r_{3},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{2}=-(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1){\mathrm{d}}r_{2}.

(138)

Since these expressions are proportional to
{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}
, we can in evaluating the numerator of eqn. (
134
) set to zero any other occurrences
of
{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}
or
{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}
. Then, we can cancel these factors of
{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}
with the factors
of
{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}
that appear in
{\mathrm{vol}}_{G}(R)
in the denominator in eqn. (
134
). After this cancellation, we can forget about
{\mathrm{d}}r_{2}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{3}
and set
them to zero. We are left with a factor
(e^{\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)^{-1}(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)^{-1}
. Note that these factors are inverted because for fermions,
measures transform oppositely to differential forms, as noted in eqn. (
136
).
In the next step, evaluate the bosonic components

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{f}=(e^{a}-1){\mathrm{d}}r_{f},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{e}=(e^{b}-1){\mathrm{d}}r_{e}.

(139)

Because these expressions are proportional to
{\mathrm{d}}r_{f}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{e}
, we can set to zero any other occurrences of
{\mathrm{d}}r_{f}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{e}
in the numerator
of eqn. (
134
). Then we can cancel
{\mathrm{d}}r_{e}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{f}
with similar occurrences in the denominator and set them to zero.
We are left this time with a factor
(e^{a}-1)(e^{b}-1)
. Next evaluate

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{1}=-(e^{-\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1){\mathrm{d}}r_{1},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{4}=-(e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1){\mathrm{d}}r_{4}.

(140)

We can set to zero any other occurrences of
{\mathrm{d}}r_{1}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{4}
in the numerator in eqn. (
134
), and cancel
{\mathrm{d}}r_{1}
and
{\mathrm{d}}r_{4}
with similar factors in the denominator.
We are left with a factor
(e^{-\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)^{-1}(e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)^{-1}
.
Next evaluate

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{h}=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}a}{2},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{h}=-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}b}{2}.

(141)

Since these expressions are proportional to
{\mathrm{d}}a
and
{\mathrm{d}}b
, we can set to zero any occurrences of
{\mathrm{d}}a
and
{\mathrm{d}}b
that we find in the rest of the evaluation. We are left with a factor

-\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{d}}a{\mathrm{d}}b
.
After dropping
{\mathrm{d}}a
and
{\mathrm{d}}b
where they appear elsewhere, we obtain
(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{z}={\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}
and
(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{z}={\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}
, so we can set to zero any further occurrences of
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}
and
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}

that we may encounter, and we remain with a factor
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}
. Then
(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{f}=-2e^{-\frac{b}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}r_{h}
, allowing us to cancel
{\mathrm{d}}r_{h}

with a similar factor in the denominator.
The final bosonic component is
(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{e}=e^{-\frac{a}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\kappa
.
Now we can evaluate the remaining fermionic components

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{4}={\mathrm{d}}\psi_{4}-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{4}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{1}={\mathrm{d}}\psi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{1}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}.

(142)

and

\displaystyle(U^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}U)_{2}={\mathrm{d}}\psi_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{2}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z},~~~~(V^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}V)_{3}={\mathrm{d}}\psi_{3}+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}.

(143)

The measure coming from (
142
) and (
143
) simplifies via
[{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{1}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z},{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{2}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z},{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{3}+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z},{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{4}-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{4}{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]=[{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{2}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{4}]
, since the Berezinian of the indicated change of basis
is 1. We canceled almost all factors in the denominator of (
134
), leaving a leftover factor of
[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
in the denominator.

After all these simplifications, keeping track of all the factors generated along the way, the identity (
134
) becomes

\displaystyle\tau_{Y}=\frac{1}{[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]}\frac{8\sinh\frac{a}{2}\sinh\frac{b}{2}~[{\mathrm{d}}a\,{\mathrm{d}}b\,{\mathrm{d}}\kappa\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}|{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]}{(e^{\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)(e^{-\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)(e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)}

(144)

where to shorten the notation we defined
[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]\equiv[{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{2}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{4}]
.
As promised,
\tau
is the ratio of a measure on
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
and a measure on
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.

The result can be put in a more useful form by eliminating
\kappa
in favor of the other moduli via eqn. (
127
).
We have
{\mathrm{d}}\kappa=\partial_{c}\kappa\,{\mathrm{d}}c+\ldots
, where the omitted terms involve derivatives with respect to other arguments and
do not contribute when this substitution is made for
{\mathrm{d}}\kappa
in eqn. (
144
). So

\displaystyle\tau_{Y}=\frac{1}{[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]}\frac{8(\partial_{c}\kappa)\sinh\frac{a}{2}\sinh\frac{b}{2}~[{\mathrm{d}}a\,{\mathrm{d}}b\,{\mathrm{d}}c\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}|{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]}{(e^{\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)(e^{-\frac{a}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}}+1)(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)(e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}+1)}.

(145)

We are going to make two further adjustments of this formula to put it in its final form.

First, we have to decide what to do with the factor
1/[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
. The most
obvious thing to do, suggested by the discussion at the end of section
4.2.2
, is to interpret the factor
1/[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
to mean that we should divide by
the volume of
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, computed using the measure
[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
. This will
replace
1/[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
with
1/2\pi
. In
appendix
D
, we consider the simpler case of JT gravity coupled to a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge field, described by a
BF
theory of

{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, and verify that this procedure is correct.

Second, the formulas that we have presented so far
have been written in terms of
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{b}
, with no mention of
\phi_{c}
.
We can write the result somewhat more symmetrically by “integrating in”
\phi_{c}
with the identity,

1=\int{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{c}~\delta(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}-F),

(146)

where the coefficients in
F=f_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+f_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}
were presented in eqn. (
129
).
Making use of this, and replacing
1/[{\mathrm{d}}r_{z}]
with
1/2\pi
, the final result for the torsion, which we will use from now on, is

\tau_{Y}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{2e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b}}\sinh\frac{a}{2}\sinh\frac{b}{2}\sinh\frac{c}{2}(1+K)}{(\cosh\frac{a}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{a})(\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{b})}\delta(\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}-F)[{\mathrm{d}}a{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{a}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{b}{\mathrm{d}}c{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{c}|{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi].

(147)

We remind the reader that to derive this expression we singled out a boundary with holonomy
W
. This was used in the definition of the fermionic moduli, which is why the formula
(
147
) is not symmetrical in
a,b,c
.
Nevertheless, we show in sections
4.3
and
5
that the permutation symmetry is restored, as it should be, after integrating over the fermionic moduli.
The formula (
147
) is actually symmetrical in
a
and
b
, up to a charge conjugation operation that reverses the signs of
\phi_{a},\phi_{b}
,
because our definition of the fermionic moduli was symmetrical in
U
and
V
, similarly
to what was explained in eqns. (3.96-7) of
SW
.

4.2.4
The Torsion of a Circle

Let us now discuss what happens when we glue together two manifolds with boundary
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
along a common boundary circle
S
to make a connected manifold
Y^{\prime}
.
In quantum field theory in general, the gluing would be implemented by multiplying the path integrals on
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
and
summing over physical states propagating through
S
. In standard approaches to
BF
theory,
the sum can be carried out by integrating over the conjugacy class of the holonomy of a flat connection around
S
. For gauge group
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, this
would mean an integration over a pair of parameters
a,\phi_{a}
that appear as eigenvalues of the holonomy.

In the approach via the torsion, the appropriate gluing procedure is to multiply the torsions of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
and divide by the torsion of
S
:

\tau_{Y^{\prime}}=\tau_{Y_{1}}\frac{1}{\tau_{S}}\tau_{Y_{2}}.

(148)

This procedure is explained heuristically in
SW
(see eqn. (3.18)) and one can find a more detailed explanation in, for example, section 4 of
Witten:1991we
.
The rough idea is that
\tau_{Y_{1}}
and
\tau_{Y_{2}}
are both measures for the holonomy along
S
(as well as other variables), and to avoid overcounting,
we have to divide by
\tau_{S}
, which is also such a measure.

The general procedure to compute
\tau_{S}
for any gauge group is outlined in section 3.4.3 of
SW
.
For any
G
, let
U
be the holonomy of a flat connection around
S
and let
\widehat{U}
be the linear transformation of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of
G
given
by
\widehat{U}(s)=UsU^{-1}
. Then
\tau_{S}={\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}-1)
, where
{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}
is a sort of superanalog of the determinant. To make this more concrete in the case of
G={\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
,
up to conjugation we can assume that

U={\rm diag}(-e^{\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi},-e^{-\frac{a}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi},e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}).

(149)

\widehat{U}
commutes with a decomposition of
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{su}(1,1|1)
as
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{\perp}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
is the subalgebra of diagonal matrices and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\perp}
is the space
of strictly off-diagonal matrices. The torsion has a corresponding factorization

\tau_{S}=\tau_{S}^{\perp}\cdot\tau_{S}^{0}.

(150)

Let
\widehat{U}^{0}
,
\widehat{U}^{\perp}
be the restrictions of
\widehat{U}
to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\perp}
. Then
\widehat{U}^{\perp}
is invertible and
\tau_{S}^{\perp}={\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}^{\perp}-1)
. The definition
of
{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}
is such that if
M=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}M_{b}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&M_{f}\end{array}\right)
is a linear transformation of a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-graded vector space with invertible bosonic
and fermionic blocks
M_{b}
,
M_{f}
, then
{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(M)=|\det M_{b}|/\det M_{f}
.
In the present case, this leads to

\tau_{S}^{\perp}={\rm Ber}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}^{\perp}-1)=\frac{\sinh^{2}\frac{a}{2}}{(\cosh\frac{a}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)^{2}}.

(151)

On the other hand,
\widehat{U}^{0}-1=0
, which leads to
\tau_{S}^{0}
being a simple quotient of measures,

\tau_{S}^{0}={\mathrm{d}}a\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi\,{\mathrm{d}}\varrho^{-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{-1}.

(152)

Here
a,\phi
are the parameters of
U
introduced in eqn. (
149
), and
\varrho
,
\varphi
are length-twist and
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-twist parameters that we already encountered in
discussing the double trumpet. This formula generalizes eqn. (3.42) of
SW
.

The final answer for the torsion is

\tau_{S}=\frac{\sinh^{2}\frac{a}{2}}{(\cosh\frac{a}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}a\,{\mathrm{d}}\phi\,({\mathrm{d}}\varrho)^{-1}\,({\mathrm{d}}\varphi)^{-1}.

(153)

It is perhaps more to the point to write this as a formula for
1/\tau_{S}
, since that is what appears in the gluing law (
148
):

\frac{1}{\tau_{S}}=\frac{(\cosh\frac{a}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)^{2}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{a}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}a^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\phi^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\varrho\,{\mathrm{d}}\varphi.

(154)

This formula can be interpreted, in part, as follows. Both
\tau_{Y_{1}}
and
\tau_{Y_{2}}
are proportional to
{\mathrm{d}}a{\mathrm{d}}\phi
, and
1/\tau_{S}
removes an unwanted extra factor
of
{\mathrm{d}}a{\mathrm{d}}\phi
that would be present in the product
\tau_{Y_{1}}\tau_{Y_{2}}
. On the other hand, gluing introduces new parameters

\varrho
and
\varphi
, and
1/\tau_{S}
generates a measure for integration over these parameters.

4.2.5
General Case: Measure on Moduli Space

Having computed the torsion of a three-holed sphere and a circle, we can assemble the pieces and compute the moduli space measure of
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity on any oriented
NAdS
2
manifold.

For simplicity, we begin with a discussion of surfaces without boundary. Any closed oriented surface
C
of genus
g
can be assembled by gluing together a set
T
of
2g-2
three-holed spheres
Y_{t}
,
