t\in T
. These three-holed spheres have to be glued along a set
U
of
3g-3
circles
S_{u}
,
u\in U
.
Two three-holed spheres (or two boundaries of the same three-holed sphere) are glued along each
S_{u}
.

We denote as
{\mathcal{T}}_{g}
the moduli space of flat connections modulo gauge transformations on a genus
g
surface
C
without boundary, and we write
\mu

for the corresponding moduli space measure.
The measure
\mu
is simply the tensor product of the
\tau_{Y_{t}}
for all
t\in T
times the tensor product of the
\frac{1}{\tau_{S_{u}}}
for all
u\in U
. This follows inductively
from the gluing formula.
Hence

\mu_{g,n=0}=\prod_{t\in T}\tau_{Y_{t}}\prod_{u\in U}\frac{1}{\tau_{S_{u}}}.

(155)

Looking at eqn. (
147
) for
\tau_{Y_{t}}
and eqn. (
154
) for
1/\tau_{S_{u}}
, we see that significant cancellation occurs in this product between factors
coming from three-holed spheres and factors coming from gluing along circles.

However,
eqn. (
155
) is actually only a formal expression. As seen in eqn. (
147
),
\tau_{Y}
is proportional to a delta function of a sum
\phi_{a}+\phi_{b}+\phi_{c}

of holonomy angles, modulo nilpotent corrections that lead to derivatives of the delta function. If we actually evaluate
\mu_{g,n=0}
using eqn. (
155
), then,
since a closed surface without boundary has no external holonomies, after multiplying together all these delta functions and integrating over the
\phi
’s, we will be left
over with a factor of
\delta(0)
(or possibly a derivative thereof,
\delta^{[n]}(0)
for some
n
). It is quite likely that in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity, the partition function of a closed
surface is not well-defined. What are well-defined, though subtle, and what we need for understanding
holographic duality, are partition functions for surfaces with NAdS external boundaries.
Also well-defined, and closely related, are volumes for surfaces with geodesic boundaries and with specified holonomy parameters
b,\phi
on each boundary.

To describe the appropriate measure in the case of an NAdS boundary, we have to know how to glue a trumpet onto a three-holed sphere.
We have described in section
3

the trumpet path integral
Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
,
with the Schwarzian mode on the asymptotic boundary and standard boundary conditions on the interior geodesic;
we also have computed the path integral on a three-holed sphere using torsion boundary conditions. We need to know how to compare or combine results computed with the two
different types of boundary condition.
It is slightly tricky to do this because a convenient way to combine the Schwarzian path integral and the torsion in one calculation is not known. Instead we proceed indirectly
as in
SW
. In the standard approach, the measure for the double trumpet is
22
22

22

For brevity, we do not show the external parameters
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
characterizing the
trumpets.

Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\,{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi{\mathrm{d}}\varrho{\mathrm{d}}\varphi\,Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi).

(156)

If we knew how to calculate a trumpet path integral with the Schwarzian mode on the outer boundary and torsion boundary conditions on the inner boundary, we would
get a measure
\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\,{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi.
Then to get the appropriate measure for the double trumpet, we would glue together two trumpets using the gluing
law for the torsion, meaning that we would multiply the torsions of the two trumpets and divide by
\tau_{S}
, the torsion of a circle where the gluing occurs. This would give

\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi){\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi\frac{1}{\tau_{S}}\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi){\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi.

(157)

Using eqn. (
154
) for
1/\tau_{S}
, this becomes

\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\frac{(\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)^{2}}{\sinh^{2}\frac{b}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi{\mathrm{d}}\varrho{\mathrm{d}}\varphi\,\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi).

(158)

Comparing these formulas, we get

\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)=Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\frac{\sinh\frac{b}{2}}{\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi}.

(159)

Now consider a Riemann surface
C
of genus
g
with
n
boundaries, all of NAdS type. Thus
C
is made by gluing
n
trumpets onto an internal manifold
C_{\mathrm{int}}
along

n
circles
S_{u}
,
u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}
, where
U_{\mathrm{ext}}
is the set of boundaries of
C_{\mathrm{int}}
. Write
\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}
for the torsion measure of the moduli space
{\mathcal{R}}_{g,n}
of flat connections
on
C_{\mathrm{int}}
. The gluing law for the torsion tells us
that the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity path integral on
C
is the integral over
{\mathcal{R}}_{g,n}
of

\prod_{u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}}\left(\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b_{u},\phi_{u}){\mathrm{d}}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}\frac{1}{\tau_{S_{u}}}\right)\cdot\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}.

(160)

Using eqn. (
159
) for
\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
and also eqn. (
154
) for
1/\tau_{S}
, this becomes

\prod_{u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}}Z_{\rm tr}(b_{u},\phi_{u})\frac{\cosh\frac{b_{u}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{u}}{\sinh\frac{b_{u}}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\varrho_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{u}\cdot\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}.

(161)

In the approach of
SSS
, the expected answer is

\prod_{u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}}Z_{\rm tr}(b_{u},\phi_{u}){\mathrm{d}}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\varrho_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{u}\cdot\mu_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n}),

(162)

where
\mu_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n})
is the Riemannian or symplectic measure on the moduli space
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n})

of flat connections on
C_{\mathrm{int}}
with the holonomy
around
S_{u}
specified to be
b_{u},\phi_{u}
.
Comparing the two formulas, we find a formula for
\mu_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n})
:

\mu_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n})=\prod_{u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}}\frac{\cosh\frac{b_{u}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{u}}{\sinh\frac{b_{u}}{2}}\frac{1}{{\mathrm{d}}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}}\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}.

(163)

Here the factor of
1/{\mathrm{d}}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}
is an instruction to remove the factor
{\mathrm{d}}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}
that is present
23
23

23

This factor is present in
\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}
because

\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}
is a measure on
{\mathcal{R}}_{g,n}
, the moduli space of flat connections on
C_{\mathrm{int}}
with no restriction on the boundary holonomies except that they are hyperbolic;
it is absent in
\mu_{g,n}
, because
\mu_{g,n}
is a measure on the moduli space
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}
of flat connections on
C_{\mathrm{int}}
with specified conjugacy classes of the boundary
holonomies.

in
\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}
.

C_{\mathrm{int}}
can be built by gluing together
2g-2+n
three-holed spheres
Y_{t}
,
t\in T
, along
3g-3+n
circles
S_{u}
,
u\in U_{\mathrm{int}}
. Then we can write a formula
for
\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}
that is precisely analogous to eqn. (
155
):

\tau_{C_{\mathrm{int}}}=\prod_{t\in T}\tau_{Y_{t}}\prod_{u\in U_{\mathrm{int}}}\frac{1}{\tau_{S_{u}}}.

(164)

Finally, using (
147
) for
\tau_{Y_{t}}
and eqn. (
154
) for
1/\tau_{S_{u}}
, and inserting eqn. (
164
) in eqn. (
163
),
we get a more explicit formula for
\mu_{g,n}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{g,n}(b_{1},\phi_{1};\cdots;b_{n},\phi_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{u\in U_{\rm int}}[{\mathrm{d}}a_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}]\cdot[{\mathrm{d}}\varrho_{u}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{u}]\prod_{t\in T}~\frac{-2e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{a_{t}}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{b_{t}}}(\cosh\frac{c_{t}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{c_{t}})}{(2\pi)}

(165)

\displaystyle~~~~\times(1+K_{t})\delta\left(\phi_{a_{t}}+\phi_{b_{t}}+\phi_{c_{t}}-F_{t}\right)[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi_{t}].

In writing this expression we used the same labels for each
t\in Y
that we used in section
4.2.2
. The three lengths are

a_{t}
,
b_{t}
and
c_{t}
, the three
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies are
\phi_{a_{t}}
,
\phi_{b_{t}}
and
\phi_{c_{t}}
and the fermionic moduli are
\psi_{t}
.
The nilpotent terms
K_{t}
and
F_{t}
are implicitly given by the functions appearing in (
129
) and (
130
) involving only the parameters of
t\in Y
.

Since
C
is assumed to be oriented, the measure (
165
) will equal the Weil-Petersson symplectic measure of the moduli space
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}
(assuming that the symplectic
structure is normalized properly).
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}
as we have defined it so far is a version of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Teichmüller space.
24
24

24

For an exploration
of
{\cal N}=2
Teichmüller theory and the associated Weil-Petersson structure, see
Ip:2016ojn
.
To get the corresponding moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
of
{\mathcal{N}}=2

super Riemann surfaces, we have to divide
{\mathcal{T}}_{g,n}
by the mapping class group. Since
BF
theory – and its partition function, the torsion – is diffeomorphism-invariant,

\mu_{g,n}
descends to a measure on
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
.
Finally, we can define formally the volume of
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
:

V_{g,n}=\int_{{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}\mu_{g,n}.

(166)

Although we will be able to compute these integrals and in some sense they certainly deserve to be called volumes, actually their interpretation is subtle.
That is because
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
is not really a supermanifold, partly because of the constraint on the external
R
-symmetry holonomies, and partly because dividing by global

R
-symmetries is not a very nice operation, as
was explained at the end of section
4.2.2
.
We will see in actual computations, for example eqn. (
173
) and generalizations calculated later, that these “volumes” in their dependence on the
R
-symmetry
holonomies are derivatives of a delta function. So for given external holonomies they are either zero or infinite; they cannot be interpreted literally as volumes in any usual sense.
To get actual numbers that would be volumes in a conventional sense, one has to integrate over one or more of the external holonomies to eliminate the delta function.
In fact, we will do just that in a particular way when we use these volumes as inputs to compute the partition function
Z_{g,n}
via eqn. (
167
) below.

Geometrically, integrating over external holonomies is similar to treating them as additional moduli. When one does this, one replaces
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
with a space
{{X}}_{g,n}

that is still not a supermanifold because the operation of dividing by global
R
-symmetries is not nice, but that at least locally can be interpreted as
{{X}}^{\prime}_{g,n}/{\mathrm{U}}(1)
, where
{{X}}^{\prime}_{g,n}
is
a supermanifold. Then the “volume” of
{{X}}_{g,n}
is plausibly a quotient
{\rm vol}({{X}}^{\prime}_{g,n})/{\rm vol}({\mathrm{U}}(1))
.

In this paper, it will not be important to understand the precise sense in which
V_{g,n}
is a volume. More relevant is that we can calculate
V_{g,n}
(after learning in
section
5
how to deal with the mapping class group), and that we can express the JT gravity partition function, which in turn can be compared to the matrix
model predictions, in terms of
V_{g,n}
.
To get the formula for
Z_{g,n}
that is analogous to that of
SSS
in the bosonic case,
we have to integrate the measure (
162
) over
{{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
. This replaces
\mu_{g,n}
by
V_{g,n}
.
In addition, since
V_{g,n}
does not depend on the gluing parameters,
we can explicitly integrate over
\varrho_{u}
and
\varphi_{u}
, giving for each
u\in U_{\mathrm{ext}}
a factor
2\pi b_{u}
.
Finally we get

\displaystyle Z_{g,n}=\left[\prod_{u=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{u}{\mathrm{d}}b_{u}\int_{0}^{2\pi}2\pi{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{u}\,Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{u},\alpha_{u};\overline{{\sf b}}_{u})\right]V_{g,n}({\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{n}),

(167)

where
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
and
\overline{{\sf b}}=(b,-\phi)
.

We
will now do a direct calculation of
V_{0,3}
, enabling us to justify the formula (
74
) that was used in section
3.3
to match with the matrix model prediction for

Z_{0,3}
.
To compute the volumes in general, we will in section
5
use the preceding formulas as input to develop a Mirzakhani-style recursion relation.
This will give general results for the volumes and the partition functions that can be compared to random matrix theory.

4.3
Example: Computation of
V_{0,3}

In bosonic JT gravity,
V_{0,3}=1
, because
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
, the moduli space of geodesic three-holed spheres with specified boundary lengths, consists of a single point. In
{\mathcal{N}}=1
JT supergravity,
V_{0,3}=0
,
because of fermionic moduli. The case of
{\cal N}=2
is more involved and more interesting.

{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
has dimension
0|4
with four fermionic moduli
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{4}
. We denote the three boundary lengths now by
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
and
b_{3}
and the corresponding
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies by
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
. The measure
\mu_{0,3}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
is given by eqns. (
147
) and (
163
). Explicitly

\displaystyle V_{0,3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}}\mu_{0,3}

(168)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi_{2}}(\cosh\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\cos\phi_{3})}{(2\pi)}\int(1+K)\delta\left(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}-F\right)[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi_{t}].

This particular calculation is relatively simple because the mapping class group of a three-holed sphere is trivial. If we did not have the nilpotent contributions
K=\sum_{i<j}k_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+k_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}
and
F=\sum_{i<j}f_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+f_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}
that
were presented in eqns. (
129
) and (
130
),
V_{0,3}
would vanish due to the rule of fermionic integration
\int{\mathrm{d}}\psi=0
. To obtain a non-zero answer requires a term proportional to the product
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}
.

We define the fermionic measure by
\int[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4}=1
, and therefore

\displaystyle\int[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]~\big(1+K\big)~\delta\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i}-F\Big)=

\displaystyle k_{4}\,\delta\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle-(f_{12}k_{34}+f_{14}k_{23}+f_{23}k_{14}+f_{34}k_{12}+f_{4})\delta^{\prime}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle+(f_{12}f_{34}+f_{14}f_{23})\delta^{\prime\prime}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\phi_{i}\Big).

(169)

Using (
129
) and (
130
) for the coefficients, the formula for
V_{0,3}
simplifies considerably:
25
25

25

We would like to thank D. Stanford for finding a mistake in an earlier version of this calculation.

\displaystyle V_{0,3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\Big[G(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\phi_{3})\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3})+2\partial_{\phi_{1}}G(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\phi_{3})\delta^{\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3})

(170)

\displaystyle~~~+\partial^{2}_{\phi_{1}}G(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\phi_{3})\delta(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3})\Big],

where we define

\displaystyle G(\phi,\phi_{3})=\frac{\cosh\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{3}}{\cosh\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi}.

(171)

Surprisingly, the three terms in eqn. (
170
) can be neatly combined to a single delta function:

G(\phi,\phi_{3})\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{3}+\phi)+2\partial_{\phi}G(\phi,\phi_{3})\delta^{\prime}(\phi_{3}+\phi)+\partial^{2}_{\phi}G(\phi,\phi_{3})\delta(\phi_{3}+\phi)=\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{3}+\phi).

(172)

One can prove this identity easily by multiplying by an arbitrary test function and integrating, and then integrating by parts. The only property of
G(\phi,\phi_{3})
that is required
is that
G(\phi_{3},\phi_{3})=1
. By virtue of eqn. (
172
), the Weil-Petersson volume of the
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic three-holed sphere is equal to

V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}).

(173)

This precisely justifies eqn. (
74
) and therefore justifies claims made in section
3.3
that follow from this formula.
It might be possible to define the fermionic moduli in a way that enables one to get this simple result without the complicated intermediate steps, but it is not immediately clear how to do that.

In the next section we will find a recursion relation for
V_{g,n}
, and the formula for
V_{0,3}
will be an initial condition in the recursion. This will extend the match with the random matrix ensemble from the three-holed sphere to an
arbitrary oriented surface.

5
Topological Recursion from Gravity and Random Matrices

In the previous section, we determined the path integral measure of
{\cal N}=2
JT supergravity on an oriented manifold with geodesic boundaries. In order to calculate the partition
function, we need to integrate this measure over the appropriate moduli space.

Even though the measure is extremely simple, how to integrate it over a fundamental domain of the mapping class group is not obvious. For ordinary Riemann surfaces, Mirzakhani
mirzakhani2007simple
;
Mirzakhani:2006eta
solved this problem by developing a recursion relation that can be used
to effectively compute the volumes. This recursion relation was extended to
{\mathcal{N}}=1
super Riemann surfaces in
SW
, see also
norbury2023enumerative
, and here we will extend this story to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann
surfaces. With the help of this result, we will be able to verify the duality between
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity and an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
AZ ensemble, to all orders in
perturbation theory.

5.1
Review of Mirzakhani’s Approach

We begin with a brief
review of Mirzakhani’s method, and then in section
5.2
, we explain the modifications needed to extend this approach to
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic surfaces. The
reader might find it helpful to consult appendix D of
SW
, which contains some more detailed explanations. In this discussion, for brevity we refer to a three-holed
sphere with a hyperbolic metric and geodesic boundaries as a geodesic three-holed sphere.

Let
\Sigma
be a hyperbolic surface of genus
g
with
n+1
geodesic boundaries. We single out one boundary of length
b
,
and denote the other
n
boundary lengths as
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}
. It will be useful to label the boundary
geodesics by their lengths as
\gamma_{b},\gamma_{b_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{b_{n}}
.

Figure 10:
Generically, as explained in the text, a geodesic
\ell_{p}
orthogonal to the boundary of
\Sigma
at
p\in\gamma_{b}
determines a hyperbolic three-holed
sphere
Y\subset\Sigma
that has
\gamma_{b}
as one of its boundaries. Three possibilities are sketched here.

The first step is a simple decomposition of
\gamma_{b}
. Consider a point
p\in\gamma_{b}
, and let
\ell_{p}
be the geodesic orthogonal to
\gamma_{b}
at
p
.
The set of
p
such that
\ell_{p}
continues forever without intersecting itself or returning to the boundary has measure zero, according to a theorem of
Birman and Series.
After throwing away this set of measure zero, we can assume that
\ell_{p}
eventually intersects itself or returns to the boundary of
\Sigma
.
When that happens, we terminate
\ell_{p}
. We take the union of
\ell_{p}
and the boundary component or
components it ends on, and thicken this set
slightly to get what topologically is a three-holed sphere whose boundaries are either boundaries of
\Sigma
and therefore geodesics, or
else embedded circles in
\Sigma
. The lengths of such embedded circles can be minimized in their homotopy classes to get geodesics.
In this way, the choice of a generic point
p\in\gamma_{b}

determines an embedded geodesic three-holed sphere
26
26

26

The moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
of a three-holed sphere is a point, of volume 1. So we can assume
that
\Sigma
is not itself a three-holed sphere, ensuring that

Y
is a proper subset of
\Sigma
and always has at least one boundary in the interior of
\Sigma
.
In the extension to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surfaces, the volume of
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
is not simply 1, but we have already computed it in
section
4.3
, so we will again assume that
\Sigma
is not a three-holed sphere.

Y\subset\Sigma
. All this is pictured in fig.
10
.

We can now decompose
\gamma_{b}
(with a set of measure zero discarded) as the union of three subsets:

•

Set
A
:
\ell_{p}
intersects itself or returns to the original boundary
\gamma_{b}
, and as depicted in figs.
10
(b,c), the three-holed sphere
Y

has one boundary
\gamma_{b}
and two boundaries in the interior of
\Sigma
. The complement of
Y
is
connected and is a surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
of genus
g-1
with
n+2
boundaries, as schematically shown here:

b
b^{\prime}
b^{\prime\prime}
g-1

(174)

We denote the lengths of the new boundaries as
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Set
B
: again
\ell_{p}
intersects itself or returns to
\gamma_{b}
, and
Y
has two interior boundaries,
but now
\Sigma^{\prime}
is disconnected and is the union of surfaces
\Sigma_{1},

\Sigma_{2}
of genera

h_{1},

h_{2}
such that
h_{1}+h_{2}=g
, and with boundary lengths
b^{\prime},B_{1}
and
b^{\prime\prime},B_{2}

such that
B_{1}\cup B_{2}=B
:

b
b^{\prime}
b^{\prime\prime}
h_{1}
h_{2}

(175)

•

Set
C
: The geodesic reaches another boundary
\gamma_{b_{k}}
, or it self-intersects and
\gamma_{b_{k}}
is one of the boundaries of
Y
.
Either way,
Y
has the two boundaries just specified and one internal boundary, say of length
b^{\prime}
.
The remaining surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
is of genus
g
with
n
boundaries:

b
b_{k}
b^{\prime}
g

(176)

Keeping
\Sigma
fixed, the sum of the lengths of the three sets
A,B,C
is
b
, since
A\cup B\cup C=\gamma_{b}
, except for a set of measure 0. If we integrate over all moduli,
including the moduli of
\Sigma
and also the choice of the point
p\in\gamma_{b}
, the result will be simply
bV_{g,n+1}(b,B)
.
However, we can make use of the fact that the choice of
p
generically determines a decomposition of
\Sigma
as the union of a three-holed sphere
Y
with some other
surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
(possibly disconnected). In general,
Y
can be any geodesic three-holed sphere in
\Sigma
one of whose boundaries is
\gamma_{b}
.
So we can organize the integral over the moduli of the pair
\Sigma,p
as a sum over all choices of
Y
, and for each
Y
an integral over
p
and an integral over the moduli
of the remaining surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Since the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma^{\prime}
is always less negative than the Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
, this leads to a recursion
relation that ultimately completely determines the volumes.

To carry out this program, we need to know how to make the decomposition of
\gamma_{b}
in the three sets
A,B,C
if
Y
is given.
Let
Y
be a hyperbolic three-holed sphere
with geodesic boundaries
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of lengths
b
,
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
respectively. In the bosonic case,
Y
has
no moduli apart from these lengths (in the generalization to super Riemann surfaces,
Y
does have fermionic moduli and we will have to integrate over them).
We denote by
{\sf T}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
the length of the set of points
p\in\gamma
such that
\ell_{p}
ends on
\gamma^{\prime}
. Of course the set of points such
that
\ell_{p}
ends on
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
then has length
{\sf T}(b,b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime})
. We also denote by
{\sf D}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
the length of the set of points
p\in\gamma
such that
\ell_{p}
self-intersects or
returns to
\gamma
. Since these are all the options (apart from a set of measure zero), we have

\displaystyle b={\sf D}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})+{\sf T}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})+{\sf T}(b,b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime}).

(177)

In terms of these functions, which we compute shortly, the recursion relation becomes

\displaystyle bV^{\rm bos.}_{g,n+1}(b,B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\,b^{\prime\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime\prime}\,{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})~V^{\rm bos.}_{g-1,n+2}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},B)

(178)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\,b^{\prime\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime\prime}\,{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\sum_{\rm stable}V^{\rm bos.}_{h_{1},|B_{1}|+1}(b^{\prime},B_{1})V^{\rm bos}_{h_{2},|B_{2}|+1}(b^{\prime\prime},B_{2})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{|B|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\,\big(b-{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})\big)V^{\rm bos.}_{g,n}(b^{\prime},B/b_{k})

The three terms on each line of the right hand side correspond to each of the three possible behaviors of
\ell_{p}
. We have denoted the bosonic volumes by
V_{g,n}^{\rm bos.}(b,B)
to distinguish them from their supersymmetric counterpart which we analyze later, and similarly for the kernels appearing in the integrands. The qualification “stable” means that one
excludes the case that one of the components is of genus 0 with less than 3 boundaries, and hence does not have a hyperbolic metric.

In eqn. (
178
), the reason for the coefficient
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
in the first two lines is relatively easy to understand.
The outcomes that correspond to these lines arise if and only if
\ell_{p}
, which is initially perpendicular to
\gamma_{b}
at
p
, self-intersects or returns to
\gamma_{b}

while remaining in a three-holed sphere
Y
whose other boundaries are internal geodesics
\gamma_{b^{\prime}}
,
\gamma_{b^{\prime\prime}}
of
\Sigma
.
In that case, the thickening of
\gamma_{b}\cup\ell_{p}
to a geodesic three-holed sphere will always be
Y
.
The measure of the subset of
\gamma_{b}
for which
\ell_{p}
has this behavior with this particular
Y
is
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
,
so that is the coefficient in the first two lines of eqn. (
178
).
The coefficient in the last line is less obvious. There are two ways that the thickening process can lead to a three-holed sphere
Y
two of whose boundaries are
\gamma_{b}

and another external boundary
\gamma_{b_{k}}
of
\Sigma
. First and perhaps more obvious,
\ell_{p}
might terminate on
\gamma_{b_{k}}
while remaining in
Y
. This
corresponds to a set of measure
{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b_{k},b^{\prime})
. Second and possibly less obvious,
\ell_{p}
might self-intersect or return to
\gamma_{b}
, in such a way that the
thickening process leads to a three-holed sphere with
\gamma_{b_{k}}
as one of its boundaries. This corresponds to a subset of
\gamma_{b}
of measure
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})
.
The combined measure of the two subsets of
\gamma_{b}
is
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})+{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b_{k},b^{\prime})=b-{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})
, which is the
coefficient claimed in (
178
).

To complete the derivation, we need an explicit expression for the functions
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
and
{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
. Thanks to (
177
), we only need to determine
{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, the length of the segment
S
of points along
\gamma
that end up in
\gamma^{\prime}
. We explain this calculation in a way that straightforwardly generalizes
to the
{\cal N}=2
case. It is convenient to do the calculation by first “unwrapping”
Y
in the upper half plane
H
,
which we parametrize by a complex coordinate
z=x+{\mathrm{i}}y
,
y>0
, with metric

{\mathrm{d}}s^{2}=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}y^{2}}{y^{2}}.

(179)

As suggested in the right panel of fig.
9
(b), the fundamental group of
Y
is generated by loops that wind once around
\gamma
or once around
\gamma^{\prime}
, starting and
ending at the base-point
P
. Taking the bosonic limit of the formulas that we used in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supergravity, we can assume that the hyperbolic
flat connection on
Y
has holonomies
U,V
around
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, respectively:

U=\pm\Big(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{b/2}&\kappa\\
0&e^{-b/2}\end{array}\Big),\hskip 56.9055ptV=\pm\Big(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-b^{\prime}/2}&0\\
1&e^{b^{\prime}/2}\end{array}\Big).

(180)

We can view
Y
as
\widehat{Y}/\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is the fundamental group of
Y
, and
\widehat{Y}
is a region in
H
that is bounded by geodesics that are lifts to
H
of the boundaries

\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of
Y
. In fact,
\widehat{Y}
has infinitely many
geodesic boundaries that project in
Y
to
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
, or
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
. For example,
\gamma
has a lift that is invariant under the action of
U
on
H
; its other
lifts in
\widehat{Y}
are invariant under elements of
\Gamma
that are conjugate to
U
. There is a unique
U
-invariant geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}
in
H
; it connects the two fixed points of
U

on the conformal boundary of
H
. The conformal boundary of
H
is at
y=0
; it is a copy of
{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
, which can be parametrized by homogeneous coordinates
u,v

with
x=u/v
.
As a
2\times 2
matrix acting on
\begin{pmatrix}u\\[-4.0pt]
v\end{pmatrix}
,
U
has two eigenvectors, which correspond to the
two fixed points of
U
on
{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
. The eigenvectors of
U
are
\Big(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\[-4.0pt]
0\end{array}\Big)
and
\Big(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{\kappa}{2\sinh\frac{b}{2}}\\[-4.0pt]
1\end{array}\Big)
, so the end-points of the geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}
are located at
x_{0}=-\kappa/(2\sinh\frac{b}{2})
and
x_{3}=\infty
.
Likewise,
\gamma^{\prime}
has a
V
-invariant lift
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
to
\widehat{Y}
. The endpoints of
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
correspond to the eigenvectors of
V
, namely

\Big(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\[-4.0pt]
1\end{array}\Big)
and
\Big(\begin{array}[]{c}-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\\[-4.0pt]
1\end{array}\Big)
, corresponding to
x_{1}=0
and
x_{2}=-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}
.

Let
\ell
be a non-self-intersecting geodesic in
Y
orthogonal to
\gamma
at a point
p
and ending at
p^{\prime}\in\gamma^{\prime}
.
Keeping
p^{\prime}
fixed,
\ell
is the shortest path from
p^{\prime}
to
\gamma
in its homotopy class. A homotopy class of non-self-intersecting paths
from
p^{\prime}
to
\gamma
is unique except for the possibility of adjoining at the beginning of the path a loop that winds around
\gamma^{\prime}
any integer number of times.
As we vary
p^{\prime}
,
\ell
will continue to exist, being always the shortest path from
\gamma^{\prime}
to
\gamma
in its homotopy class;
p
will change, of course. This continues
as
p^{\prime}
winds around
\ell^{\prime}
any number of times, and since in that way we explore all possible homotopy classes of non-intersecting paths from
\gamma^{\prime}
to
\gamma
,
it follows that the space of non-self-intersecting geodesics in
H
that are orthogonal to
\gamma
and end at
\gamma^{\prime}
is connected. Since that is the case, in lifting to
H
,
we can pick a specific
pair of lifts
\widehat{\gamma}
and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
to
H
and consider geodesics in
\widehat{Y}
orthogonal to
\widehat{\gamma}
and ending on
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
. There is no need to consider
different lifts of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. We can simply consider the lifts described in the last paragraph, such that
\widehat{\gamma}
connects boundary points
x_{0}
and
x_{3}
,
while
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
connects
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
.

x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
(x_{0},x_{0}-x_{1})
(x_{0},x_{0}-x_{2})
\mathcal{S}
\widehat{\gamma}
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}

Figure 11:
The picture in the upper half-plane
H
with its conformal boundary shown as a horizontal line.
\widehat{\gamma}
is the solid vertical line and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
is the solid semicircle. The dashed lines, which are portions of circles that are orthogonal to both
\widehat{\gamma}
and the conformal boundary, denote the limiting cases of geodesics that are orthogonal to
\widehat{\gamma}
and reach
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
. The interval between them represents the region
\mathcal{S}
of
\gamma
. Its length can be straightforwardly determined from
the picture.

To work out the length of the desired subset of
\gamma
is now straightforward. We just need to know that geodesics in
H
are either vertical straight lines
or semicircles (in the Euclidean metric
{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}y^{2}
) that are orthogonal to the line
x=0
. So the geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}
from
x_{0}
to
x_{3}
is
the vertical line
x=x_{0}
and the geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
is the semi-circle shown in
fig.
11
. We denote by
x_{1}
and
x_{2}<x_{1}
the end-points of
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
.
Points
p\in\widehat{\gamma}
such that the geodesic
\ell_{p}
orthogonal to
\widehat{\gamma}
at
p
reaches
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
make up an interval
\mathcal{S}
,
and the desired function
{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
is the length of
\mathcal{S}
. The endpoints of
\mathcal{S}
are easily determined as in the figure,
and computing the length of
\mathcal{S}
in the hyperbolic metric (
179
), we get
\log\frac{(x_{0}-x_{2})}{(x_{0}-x_{1})}
. Equivalently (as
x_{3}=\infty
),

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})=\log\frac{(x_{0}-x_{2})(x_{1}-x_{3})}{(x_{0}-x_{1})(x_{2}-x_{3})}.

(181)

In order words, it is the logarithm of a cross-ratio between the four endpoints of the two geodesics
\widehat{\gamma}
and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

The final step is to find an expression for
x_{0},\ldots,x_{3}
in terms of the lengths
b
,
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
. All we need to know is that
\kappa=-2\cosh\frac{b-b^{\prime}}{2}-2\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}

(eqn.
127
). With this information, we can compute

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{\rm bos.}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})=\log\left[\frac{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b+b^{\prime}}{2}}{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b-b^{\prime}}{2}}\right].

(182)

This completes the derivation of Mirzakhani’s recursion relation, since we can easily determine
{\sf D}_{\rm bos.}
from (
177
) and replace it in (
178
).

5.2
Geometry of
{\cal N}=2
Hyperbolic Surfaces

In this section we present some important facts about
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic surfaces, study the geometry of the
{\cal N}=2
three-holed sphere, and derive the kernels that will appear in the recursion, which is written in its final form in the next section
5.3
.

5.2.1
Chiral vs. Non-Chiral Formalism

Up to this point, we have not had to be very specific about what we mean by an
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surface. We only cared about the moduli of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann
surfaces. However, to obtain the recursion relation, we will have to use some geometric facts about
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surfaces themselves, as opposed to their moduli.

There are actually two essentially different kinds of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surface, which share the same moduli space. The difference shows up when one writes
an action for a theory with
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry. There are “
D
-terms,” which are written as an integral
\int{\mathrm{d}}^{2}z{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\theta K
over a supermanifold of complex
dimension
1|2
or real dimension
2|4
, with some generic operator
K
. And there are “
F
-terms,” which are written as an integral
\int{\mathrm{d}}^{2}z{\mathrm{d}}^{2}\theta W
,
over a supermanifold of complex dimension
1|1
or real
dimension
2|2
,
with some chiral operator
W
.

To illustrate the difference between the two, we can describe what is a genus 0 super Riemann surface of one type or the other.
The chiral version of a genus 0 super Riemann surface is
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|1}
, with homogeneous coordinates
u,v|\theta
. We observe that
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|1}
does admit
an action of the supergroup
{\mathrm{SL}}(2|1)
, acting on
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\[-4.0pt]
v\\[-1.0pt]
\hline\cr\theta\end{array}\right)
. We can introduce a hermitian metric
on
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|1}
and reduce to a real form
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2|1)
.
The non-chiral version of a genus 0 super Riemann surface is actually
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|2}
, with homogeneous coordinates
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
, endowed with a symplectic
form that is homogeneous of degree 2:

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}={\mathrm{d}}u{\mathrm{d}}v+\frac{1}{2}({\mathrm{d}}\uptheta^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}\uptheta^{\prime}{}^{2}).

(183)

The group of linear transformations of these homogeneous coordinates that preserves the symplectic form is the complex Lie group

\text{OSp}(2|2)_{\mathbb{C}}
. Again, we can introduce a hermitian metric
and a real form which we will simply call
\text{OSp}(2|2)
.

In fact, the group
\text{OSp}(2|2)_{\mathbb{C}}
is equivalent to a double cover
27
27

27

See footnote
10
for an explanation of this double cover, which however
will not play a major role in the following.
of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2|1)
, and, with the right choice of the real forms,
\text{OSp}(2|2)
is similarly a double cover of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
.
There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between super Riemann surfaces of the two types; see, for example, section 9 of
Witten:2012ga
. So they have the
same moduli spaces and that is why up to this point, there was no need to make a choice.

In this article, we will need very little systematic theory of super Riemann surfaces. For either of the two types of super Riemann surface, we just define

z=u/v
, define the analog
\widehat{H}
of the upper half plane by
{\mathrm{Im}}\,z>0
, and define a hyperbolic super Riemann surface to be a quotient

\widehat{H}/\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a discrete subgroup of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
or
\text{OSp}(2|2)
, with the property that if we reduce modulo the fermionic variables
and forget the
R
-symmetries, then
H/\Gamma
is a hyperbolic Riemann surface in the ordinary sense.

But should we use the chiral or non-chiral version of a super Riemann surface? In fact, it turns out that if we work with non-chiral
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surfaces,
then Mirzakhani’s derivation in the bosonic case and the derivation in
SW
for
{\mathcal{N}}=1
have straightforward generalizations to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
. It appears to be less straightforward
to make similar arguments in terms of chiral
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surfaces. An indication that the generalization to
{\mathcal{N}}=2
is more direct if the arguments are expressed
in terms of non-chiral super Riemann
surfaces is the following. Mirzakhani’s derivation in the bosonic case leads to a cross-ratio of four points (eqn. (
181
)), and similarly, the
{\mathcal{N}}=1
derivation in
SW

leads to an
{\mathcal{N}}=1
version of the cross-ratio. So we can guess that we will need an
{\mathcal{N}}=2
version of the cross ratio.
In the non-chiral formalism, there is no problem with this.
If a point
P_{1}\in{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|2}
has homogeneous coordinates
u_{1},v_{1}|\uptheta_{1},\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}
(where we denote the odd variables as

\uptheta
and
\uptheta^{\prime}
and use the numerical subscript to label the point) and similarly for
P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}
, then one defines

\displaystyle\langle 1,2\rangle=u_{1}v_{2}-u_{2}v_{1}-\uptheta_{1}\uptheta_{2}-\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}\uptheta^{\prime}_{2},

(184)

and the cross ratio is as usual
\langle 1,2\rangle\langle 3,4\rangle/\langle 1,3\rangle\langle 2,4\rangle
. (In addition to this complex cross ratio,
four points in
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|2}
have four odd complex moduli, which will not play an important role.) By contrast, there is no simple definition of a cross ratio for four points in
{\mathbb{CP}}^{1|1}
,
because there is no appropriate invariant in the
2|1
-dimensional representation of
{\mathrm{SL}}(2|1)
.

Other facts about
{\mathcal{N}}=1
super Riemann surfaces that were important in
SW
, appendix D, also have simple analogs for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
in the non-chiral formulation but not in the
chiral formulation. In particular, the notions of geodesics, perpendicular geodesics, intersecting geodesics, and the like will be spelled out in section
5.2.3
.

It is not clear that these notions have equally satisfactory analogs if we work with chiral super Riemann surfaces.
So we will use the non-chiral approach, though we cannot rule out the possibility that a simple proof might somehow be possible in the chiral approach.
A drawback of working with non-chiral super Riemann surfaces is that we will have to convert some
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
formulas into
\text{OSp}(2|2)
formulas.
This gives formulas that are equivalent but generally more complicated. That is actually why we did not choose to write the whole article in terms of
\text{OSp}(2|2)
.

As already explained, in either picture based on
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
or
\text{OSp}(2|2)
, the super upper half plane
\widehat{H}
is defined by
{\mathrm{Im}}\,z>0
, with
z=u/v
.
In section
5.2.3
, we will want to know in the
\text{OSp}(2|2)
picture what should be meant by the conformal boundary of
\widehat{H}
. In fact, the natural conformal boundary is defined
by taking
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
to all be real. Note that the real supergroup
\text{OSp}(2|2)
does act on the locus of real
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
, so the boundary in
this sense is
\text{OSp}(2|2)
-invariant. Equivalently, after scaling to set
v=1
, the boundary is parametrized by odd and even real variables
x|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
.
We will call this boundary
\partial\widehat{H}
. The reader might be slightly surprised that
\partial\widehat{H}
is of codimension
1|2
, not
1|0
, relative to
\widehat{H}
. This
is actually a common state of affairs in supergeometry: the most natural conformal boundary of a supermanifold
is not necessarily of codimension
1|0
. For example, in the AdS/CFT correspondence,
in a superspace description, the conformal boundary will frequently have fewer fermionic coordinates than the bulk.

5.2.2
Converting to
\text{OSp}(2|2)

A first step is to write the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
generators of eqns. (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.1
) as
\text{OSp}(2|2)
generators, that is, as linear transformations
of
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
.
For bosonic generators, we have

{\sf e}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&1&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~~{\sf f}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&0&0\\
1&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~~{\sf h}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}1&0&0&0\\
0&{\scriptsize-}1&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~~{\sf z}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&1\\
0&0&-1&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right)

(185)

and for fermionic generators, we have

\displaystyle{\sf q}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&{\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}}&{\scriptstyle-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\\
0&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}}&0&0\\
0&{\scriptstyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~{\sf q}_{2}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\scriptstyle-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\sqrt{2}}}\\
0&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&{\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}&0&0\\
0&{\scriptstyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\sqrt{2}}}&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~~

\displaystyle{\sf q}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&0&0\\
0&0&{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}}&{\scriptstyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\\
\hline\cr{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}}&0&0&0\\
{\scriptstyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2\sqrt{2}}}&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right),~~~~~{\sf q}_{4}=\left(\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}0&0&0&0\\
0&0&{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\scriptstyle-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\sqrt{2}}}\\
\hline\cr{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}&0&0&0\\
{\scriptstyle-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\sqrt{2}}}&0&0&0\\
\end{array}\hskip-2.84526pt\right).

To obtain the kernel that appears in the recursion relation requires a knowledge of the measure on the space of flat connections on a three-holed sphere
Y
.
We determined this in section
4
in the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
language, and these results can be translated to
\text{OSp}(2|2)
.
As a first step, we can rewrite eqn. (
111
) for the monodromies by which we parametrized a flat connection on
Y
in terms of
algebra generators:

\displaystyle U_{0}=e^{\frac{a}{2}{\sf h}+\frac{a}{2\sinh\frac{a}{2}}\kappa{\sf e}+(\pi+\phi_{1})\widehat{q}{\sf z}}e^{\psi_{2}{\sf q}_{2}+\psi_{4}{\sf q}_{4}},~~~V_{0}=e^{-\frac{b}{2}{\sf h}+\frac{b}{2\sinh\frac{b}{2}}{\sf f}+(\pi+\phi_{2})\widehat{q}{\sf z}}e^{\psi_{1}{\sf q}_{1}+\psi_{3}{\sf q}_{3}}.

(204)

To write the holonomies as operators on
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
, we now just have to substitute into these formulas the expressions
(
185
) and (
5.2.2
) for the generators. The expressions for
\kappa
and the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
constraint in terms of
a,b|\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\psi_{4}

were derived in section
4
.

In the rest of this derivation, we use conventions similar to those that we used in reviewing Mirzakhani’s bosonic derivation. Thus the boundary lengths of a geodesic three-holed
sphere will be
b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}
(not
a,b,c
as hitherto) and the
R
-symmetry twist parameters will be
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}
(not
\phi_{a},\phi_{b},\phi_{c}
).

5.2.3
The Supersymmetric Setup

We now consider the kernel that appears in the Mirzakhani-style recursion relation for the
{\cal N}=2
volumes. We consider an
{\cal N}=2
three-holed sphere

Y
with geodesic boundaries
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}
, and boundary data
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
,
{\sf b}^{\prime}=(b^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
,
{\sf b}^{\prime\prime}=(b^{\prime\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime})
, and
\text{OSp}(2|2)
holonomies
U,V,W
.
We want to find the measure
\widehat{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})
of
the set
\mathcal{S}
consisting of points
p\in\gamma
such that the geodesic
\ell_{p}
orthogonal to
\gamma
at
p
reaches
\gamma^{\prime}
.
The answer is actually the obvious superanalog of eqn. (
181
). If
P_{0},P_{3}
are the endpoints of the
U
-invariant geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}\subset\widehat{H}
,
and
P_{1},P_{2}
are the endpoints of a
V
-invariant geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\subset\widehat{H}
,
then

\widehat{\sf T}=\log\frac{\langle 0,2\rangle\langle 1,3\rangle}{\langle 0,1\rangle\langle 2,3\rangle},

(205)

similarly to eqn. (
181
). The
{\mathcal{N}}=2
version of the symbol
\langle~,~\rangle
was defined in eqn. (
184
).

To justify eqn. (
205
), we first need to explain what is meant by the terms “geodesics,” “orthogonal,” and so on. For
{\mathcal{N}}=1
super Riemann surfaces, such
a discussion was given in appendix D of
SW
, leading to the obvious
{\mathcal{N}}=1
superanalog of eqns. (
181
) and (
205
). A similar discussion applies
for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
, assuming that one works with non-chiral
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super Riemann surfaces, as we will now explain.

First, what is meant by a “geodesic” in
\widehat{H}
? We recall that the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
super upper half plane
\widehat{H}
contains the ordinary upper half plane
H
, embedded
at
\uptheta=\uptheta^{\prime}=0
. We want to regard an ordinary geodesic in
H
as a geodesic in
\widehat{H}
. We also want the notion of a geodesic to be
\text{OSp}(2|2)
-invariant.
So we declare a geodesic in
\widehat{H}
to be any curve – that is, any submanifold of dimension
1|0
– that is equivalent under
\text{OSp}(2|2)
to a geodesic in
H
.
Note that this definition implies that there is a well-defined length element along any geodesic
\gamma\subset\widehat{H}
: we pick an
\text{OSp}(2|2)
transformation that maps

\gamma
to
H
and then we use the ordinary length element for a geodesic in
H
. Another consequence of the definition is that a geodesic in
\widehat{H}
has
endpoints in what we defined in section
5.2.1
as
\partial\widehat{H}
, the conformal boundary of
\widehat{H}
, parametrized by real
x|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
\gamma\subset H
,
then it has endpoints in the conformal boundary of
H
. These are points of the form
x|0,0
, with real
x
, so they are in
\partial\widehat{H}
. Applying an
\text{OSp}(2|2)
transformation
and using the fact that
\partial\widehat{H}
is
\text{OSp}(2|2)
-invariant, we learn that any geodesic in
\widehat{H}
has endpoints in
\partial\widehat{H}
. We can also describe this by saying that
any geodesic in
\widehat{H}
has real endpoints (that is, endpoints whose coordinates
x|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
are all real).

Now, if
\gamma
is a geodesic in
\widehat{H}
and
p\in\gamma
, we want to define the geodesic
\ell_{p}
that is orthogonal to
\gamma
at
p
. If
\gamma\in H
,
we just use the classical definition of the orthogonal geodesic
\ell_{p}
. Any case can be reduced by this one by an
\text{OSp}(2|2)
transformation. We also need to know
what it means to say that a geodesic
\ell_{p}
is self-intersecting or intersects another geodesic
\gamma^{\prime}
. Since a geodesic is a curve of dimension
1|0
,
two geodesics in a super Riemann surface of dimension
2|4
generically do not have a point in common, and indeed asking for two geodesics to have a point in common
is not the right condition.
One way to explain the right condition (slightly different from but equivalent to the explanation in
SW
for
{\mathcal{N}}=1
)
is to use the fact that we are interested in hyperbolic super Riemann surfaces. This means
that in discussing whether two geodesics intersect, we can lift the situation to
\widehat{H}
and give a definition there.
We say that an intersection of two geodesics
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\subset\widehat{H}
consists of a pair of points

P_{1}\in\gamma_{1},

P_{2}\in\gamma_{2}
with
\langle 1,2\rangle=0
. Since there are two
real parameters in the choices of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, the complex condition
\langle 1,2\rangle=0
, equivalent to two real conditions, is a good criterion for intersection of geodesics.

The special case of this that we most need is the condition that two geodesics
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
have an endpoint in common. Suppose that
\gamma_{1}
has an endpoint with coordinates
x_{1}|\uptheta_{1},\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\gamma_{2}
has an endpoint with coordinates
x_{2}|\uptheta_{2},\uptheta^{\prime}_{2}
. These coordinates are all real, since the endpoints of any geodesic are real. The condition under which we say that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}

have a common endpoint is that
\langle 1,2\rangle=0
, again using the pairing (
184
).
Since the coordinates are real, this is a single real condition, just as it would be classically.

Now, going back to the
U
- and
V
-invariant geodesics
\widehat{\gamma},\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}\subset\widehat{U}
, we pick a point

p\in\widehat{\gamma}
and ask if the orthogonal geodesic
\ell_{p}
to
\gamma
at
p
has a common endpoint with
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
. In the bosonic case, the picture corresponding to
this question is drawn
in fig.
11
; two points
p
satisfy the desired condition, one for each endpoint of
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
. We have included the fermionic variables in such a way
that we did not change the number of equations (the condition
\langle 1,2\rangle=0
on the endpoints is a single real condition, reducing to
x_{1}=x_{2}
in the classical limit)
or unknowns (we defined a geodesic to have dimension
1|0
). So also supersymmetrically the condition that
\ell_{p}
and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
have an endpoint in common
determines two points in

\widehat{\gamma}
. These points are the endpoints of the set
\mathcal{S}\subset\widehat{\gamma}
whose length is supposed to be
\widehat{\sf T}
.

At this point, then, to compute
\widehat{\sf T}
we have to analyze the situation of fig.
11
in the supersymmetric case. It might seem that we have to do a genuinely
new supersymmetric calculation, but actually one can justify the obvious answer of eqn. (
205
) without any really new calculation.
First, we can make an
\text{OSp}(2|2)
transformation that maps
\widehat{\gamma}
to
H
and, of course, also acts on
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
. (We can even pick the
\text{OSp}(2|2)
transformation
so that
\widehat{\gamma}
is a vertical geodesic in
H
, as pictured in fig.
11
. But this is not necessary for the argument.) Then the endpoints
P_{0}
,
P_{3}

of
\widehat{\gamma}
are at
x_{0}|0,0
and
x_{3}|0,0
. Since
\widehat{\gamma}\subset H
, by definition all geodesics orthogonal to
\widehat{\gamma}
are in
H
also, so for any
p\in\widehat{\gamma}
,
an endpoint
Q
of
\ell_{p}
is in
H
, say at
\widetilde{x}|0,0
. The endpoints
P_{1},P_{2}
of
\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}
are generically not in
H
; they are at, say,
x_{1}|\uptheta_{1},\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}

and
x_{2}|\uptheta_{2},\uptheta_{2}^{\prime}
. But since
Q
is at
\uptheta=\uptheta^{\prime}=0
, the conditions
\langle 1,Q\rangle=0
and
\langle 2,Q\rangle=0
do not depend on the fermionic coordinates of

P_{1},P_{2}
; those conditions reduce to
y=x_{1}
and
y=x_{2}
. At this point, the calculation of the length of the set
\mathcal{S}
reduces precisely to the calculation that we already
explained in the purely bosonic case, leading to

\widehat{\sf T}=\log\frac{(x_{0}-x_{2})(x_{1}-x_{3})}{(x_{0}-x_{1})(x_{2}-x_{3})},

(206)

as in eqn. (
181
).

This is the answer in a coordinate system with
\widehat{\gamma}\in H
, implying that the points
P_{0}
,
P_{3}
are at
x_{0}|0,0
and
x_{3}|0,0
. We would like a formula
for
\widehat{\sf T}
that does not depend on this condition. To find such a formula, we just note that the fact that
P_{0},P_{3}
have vanishing fermionic coordinates implies
that
x_{0}-x_{2}=\langle 0,2\rangle
,
x_{1}-x_{3}=\langle 1,3\rangle
, etc. So the covariant version of the answer is just eqn. (
205
).

One point that might puzzle the reader is that in analyzing the function
\widehat{\sf T}
, we made use of the real structure of
\text{OSp}(2|2)\cong{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, but
in computing the measure on moduli space
we ignored this
real structure (at least as regards the fermionic moduli). There is actually no contradiction.
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
does have a real structure,
and we are free to use it, if convenient, in computing the function
\widehat{\sf T}
. On the other hand, the measure can be analytically continued to complex values of the moduli,
and in computing the measure, we can disregard the real structure if that is convenient.

5.2.4
Evaluation of the Kernel Appearing in the Recursion

It remains to reduce eqn. (
205
) to an explicit formula. Unfortunately, this will lead us through some rather complicated formulas, though the simplicity of the final
results suggests that there might be a simpler route to those results.

The first step is to find explicit formulas for the endpoints
P_{0},\cdots,P_{3}
that appear in this derivation, In doing so, we will describe these points by homogeneous coordinates

u_{0},v_{0}|\uptheta_{0},\uptheta_{0}^{\prime}
,
u_{1},v_{1}|\uptheta_{1},\uptheta_{1}^{\prime}
,
u_{2},v_{2}|\uptheta_{2},\uptheta_{2}^{\prime}

and
u_{3},v_{3}|\uptheta_{3},\uptheta_{3}^{\prime}
.
We view the holonomies
U,V
as
2|2\times 2|2
matrices
acting on
u,v|\uptheta,\uptheta^{\prime}
.
These matrices have two bosonic and two fermionic eigenvectors. The bosonic eigenvectors of
U
are the points
P_{0},P_{3}
, and the bosonic eigenvectors of
V
are the points

P_{1},P_{2}
.
After a detailed calculation, we find the explicit eigenvectors:

\displaystyle u_{0},v_{0}|\uptheta_{0},\uptheta^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa}{2\sinh\frac{b}{2}},1\Big|\frac{\psi_{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2\sinh\frac{b}{2}}\psi_{4}}{\sqrt{2}(1-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi-\frac{b}{2}})},-{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\psi_{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2\sinh\frac{b}{2}}\psi_{4}}{\sqrt{2}(1-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi-\frac{b}{2}})},

\displaystyle u_{1},v_{1}|\uptheta_{1},\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,1\Big|\frac{\psi_{1}}{2\sqrt{2}(e^{\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}-1)},{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\psi_{1}}{2\sqrt{2}(e^{\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}-1)},

\displaystyle u_{2},v_{2}|\uptheta_{2},\uptheta^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2},1\Big|\frac{\psi_{1}-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\psi_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}(e^{-\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}-1)},{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\psi_{1}-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\psi_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}(e^{-\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}-1)},

\displaystyle u_{3},v_{3}|\uptheta_{3},\uptheta^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,0\Big|\frac{\psi_{4}}{\sqrt{2}(-1+e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi})},\frac{-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{4}}{\sqrt{2}(-1+e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi})}.

(207)

As explained in section
5.2.3
, we now have to evaluate

\widehat{{\sf T}}=\log\frac{{\langle}0,2{\rangle}{\langle}1,3{\rangle}}{{\langle}0,1{\rangle}{\langle}2,3{\rangle}}=\log\left[\frac{u_{0}v_{2}-u_{2}v_{0}-\uptheta_{0}\uptheta_{2}-\uptheta^{\prime}_{0}\uptheta^{\prime}_{2}}{u_{0}v_{1}-u_{1}v_{0}-\uptheta_{0}\uptheta_{1}-\uptheta^{\prime}_{0}\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}}\frac{u_{1}v_{3}-u_{3}v_{1}-\uptheta_{1}\uptheta_{3}-\uptheta^{\prime}_{1}\uptheta^{\prime}_{3}}{u_{2}v_{3}-u_{3}v_{2}-\uptheta_{2}\uptheta_{3}-\uptheta^{\prime}_{2}\uptheta^{\prime}_{3}}\right].

(208)

This function depends on the three geodesic lengths, the two independent
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies, and the four fermionic moduli. An explicit formula, using eqn. (
207
)
for the eigenvectors, is

\displaystyle\widehat{{\sf T}}=\log\left[\frac{-\kappa+2\sinh\frac{b}{2}\,2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{(\kappa\psi_{4}+2\sinh\frac{b}{2}\psi_{2})(\psi_{1}-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\psi_{3})}{2\big(1-e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\big)\big(1-e^{-\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}\big)}}{-\kappa+\frac{(\kappa\psi_{4}+2\sinh\frac{b}{2}\psi_{2})\psi_{1}}{2\big(1-e^{-\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\big)\big(1-e^{\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}\big)}}\frac{1-\frac{\psi_{1}\psi_{4}}{2\big(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}-1\big)\big(1-e^{\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}\big)}}{1+\frac{(\psi_{1}-2\sinh\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}\psi_{3})\psi_{4}}{2\big(e^{\frac{b}{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}-1\big)\big(e^{-\frac{b^{\prime}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}-1\big)}}\right]

To make the dependence on fermionic moduli explicit, we can use the expansion
\kappa=\kappa_{0}+\sum_{i<j}\kappa_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\kappa_{4}\psi_{1}\ldots\psi_{4}
presented in (
130
). Then we expand
\widehat{{\sf T}}

\widehat{{\sf T}}=\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}+\sum_{i<j}\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}+\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\psi_{4},

(209)

Finding the explicit formulas for
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}
and
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}
is straightforward but tedious. The function
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}
is simpler and, importantly, is equal to the bosonic length appearing in the original Mirzakhani recursion

\displaystyle\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}={\sf T}_{\rm bos.}=\log\left[\frac{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b+b^{\prime}}{2}}{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b-b^{\prime}}{2}}\right].

(210)

We will not attempt to write down the answers for
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}
and
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}
, partly because they are long, and also because we ultimately only need the simpler functions that arise after fermionic integration.

We are now ready to complete the calculation, and integrate
\widehat{\sf T}
over
\mathcal{M}_{0,3}
, the
0|4
dimensional moduli space of the three-holed sphere. We define

{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})=2\pi\,\int_{\mathcal{M}_{0,3}}\mu_{0,3}\cdot\widehat{{\sf T}}.

(211)

We included a factor of
2\pi
on the right hand side to simplify the final form of the recursion. The measure obtained in the previous section is

\mu_{0,3}=-\frac{2e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}}(\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime\prime})}{(2\pi)}(1+K)\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}-F)[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]

(212)

When integrating
\mu_{0,3}\cdot\widehat{\sf T}
over fermionic moduli there are three terms, depending on whether we pick
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}
,
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}
or
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}
to absorb the fermionic integrations. The three terms are given schematically by

\displaystyle\int[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]~\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}-F)\,(1+K)\,\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}

\displaystyle\propto\delta^{\prime\prime}(\sum\phi)\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0},

\displaystyle\int[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}-F)\,(1+K)\,\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\prime}(\sum\phi)\,f_{ij}\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}+\delta(\sum\phi)~k_{ij}\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij},

\displaystyle\int[{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\psi]\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}-F)\,(1+K)\,\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}

\displaystyle=\delta(\sum\phi)\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}.

(213)

The first term involving
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}
was essentially already computed in section
4
. Since
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{0}
is already independent of fermionic moduli, the answer is proportional to the quantity
V_{0,3}
, computed in that section. The second term involves the new function
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}
. Surprisingly, we find that
f_{ij}\widehat{\sf T}_{ij}=0
. Finally, we are left with two contributions proportional to
\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})
, from
k_{ij}\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}
and from
\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}
. The sum of these is surprisingly simple:

k_{ij}\widehat{{\sf T}}_{ij}+\widehat{{\sf T}}_{4}=\frac{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime}-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}}{2(\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi^{\prime\prime})}\Big[\frac{1}{16\cosh^{2}\frac{b+b^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime}}{4}}-\frac{1}{16\cosh^{2}\frac{b-b^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime}}{4}}+(b^{\prime\prime}\to-b^{\prime\prime})\Big].

There might be a more direct route to this relatively simple formula, but this is not obvious. The final result for
\sf T
is presented in eqn. (
215
) below.

To formulate the recursion relation, we need one more function
{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})
.
This is the integral over the fermionic moduli of the segment of
\gamma_{b}
from which orthogonal geodesics reach neither
\gamma_{b^{\prime}}
nor
\gamma_{b^{\prime\prime}}
. It
can be computed from the following sum rule:

2\pi\int_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}}\mu_{0,3}\cdot b={\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})+{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})+{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime}).

(214)

It is important to formulate this sum rule after integration over moduli, since the way we parametrize the fermionic moduli spoils the symmetry between the
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
boundaries. The extra factor of
2\pi
was introduced for future convenience, like the rescaling of
{\sf T}
. As opposed to the case of
{\cal N}=1
super Riemann surfaces, the left hand side of
eqn. (
214
) does not vanish and produces an answer proportional to
V_{0,3}
. The result for
{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})
is stated in eqn. (
216
).

We are now ready to write down the final version of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
recursion relation.

5.3
Recursion Relation for
{\cal N}=2
Volumes

5.3.1
Final Evaluation

In this section we will summarize the results so far and write down the recursion relation for the Weil-Petersson volumes for
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic
surfaces in a way that is self-contained and can be read independently from the details of the derivation outlined so far.

The computation in section
5.2.4
leads to explicit formulas for the two kernels
{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})
and
{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})
that appear in the recursion relation.
Here we remind the reader that
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
. The two functions can be expanded

\displaystyle{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}){\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})-\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}){\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

(215)

\displaystyle{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}){\sf D}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})-\delta(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime}){\sf D}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}).

(216)

The functions
{\sf T}_{0}
,
{\sf T}_{2}
and
{\sf D}_{0}
,
{\sf D}_{2}
appearing on the right hand side depend only on the lengths; the subscript indicates the number of derivatives acting on the delta function they multiply.
{\sf T}_{0}
,
{\sf T}_{2}
are

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left[\frac{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b+b^{\prime}}{2}}{\cosh\frac{b^{\prime\prime}}{2}+\cosh\frac{b-b^{\prime}}{2}}\right],

(217)

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16\cosh^{2}\frac{b+b^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime}}{4}}-\frac{1}{16\cosh^{2}\frac{b-b^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime}}{4}}+(b^{\prime\prime}\to-b^{\prime\prime}).

(218)

{\sf D}_{0}
,
{\sf D}_{2}
are obtained through the sum rule

\displaystyle{\sf D}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b-{\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})-{\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime})

\displaystyle{\sf D}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})-{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime})

(219)

that comes from eqn. (
214
) by integration over
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,3}
.
One can check explicitly that

\displaystyle{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\partial_{b^{\prime}}^{2}{\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

(220)

\displaystyle{\sf D}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\partial_{b^{\prime}}^{2}{\sf D}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}).

(221)

These relations are not accidents and will be crucial to match the
{\cal N}=2
generalization of the Mirzakhani recursion relation with the matrix ensemble.

We have now all the ingredients we need to obtain the final form of the recursion relation that computes the volumes of moduli spaces of
\mathcal{N}=2
hyperbolic surfaces

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptbV_{g,n+1}({\sf b},{\sf B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(2\pi)\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}b^{\prime}~\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime\prime}{\mathrm{d}}b^{\prime\prime}~\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi^{\prime\prime}\,{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}^{\prime\prime})

(222)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\times\left(V_{g-1,n+2}(\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime},\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime\prime},{\sf B})+\sum_{\rm stable}V_{h_{1},|{\sf B}_{1}|+1}(\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime},{\sf B}_{1})V_{h_{2},|{\sf B}_{2}|+1}(\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime\prime},{\sf B}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+\sum_{k=1}^{|{\sf B}|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}b^{\prime}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi^{\prime}\,\Big[-\frac{b}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi_{k})-{\sf T}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},{\sf b}_{k})\Big]V_{g,n}(\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime},{\sf B}/{\sf b}_{k}).

Here
{\sf B}=\{{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{n}\}
is the set of boundary parameters, and following our notation an overline denote orientation reversal for a given boundary, so that if
{\sf b}=(b,\phi)
then
\overline{{\sf b}}=(b,-\phi)
. The coefficients in this relation have precisely the same interpretation
as in the bosonic recursion relation (
178
), except that they have been integrated over the fermionic moduli of a three-holed sphere.
The qualification “stable” under the sum has the same meaning it had in the bosonic case reviewed earlier. Integration over the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
twist parameters will produce a factor of
(2\pi)^{2}=\int{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\prime\prime}
in the first line, and of
(2\pi)=\int{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{\prime}
in the last. We will explain below the relevance of these seemingly innocent factors and the role they play. One of those factors cancels with a similar one in the normalization of
\mu_{0,3}
and the normalization of the functions
{\sf T}
and
{\sf D}
.

As a final ingredient, we need two initial conditions for this recursion relation. One initial condition is familiar:

V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},{\sf b}_{3})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}+\phi_{3}).

(223)

We also need
V_{1,1}
, which can be easily derived by imitating the derivation of the recursion with a slight modification:
28
28

28

A Riemann surface of genus 1 with one
hole or puncture has a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry. As in
SW
, by
V_{1,1}
, we mean the volume of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
in the orbifold sense, including a factor of
1/2
because
every point in
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,1}
has this
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry. If we do not do this, the statement of the recursion relation (
222
) would be more awkward.

\displaystyle V_{1,1}({\sf b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2b}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}b^{\prime}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi^{\prime}\,{\sf D}({\sf b},{\sf b}^{\prime},\overline{{\sf b}}^{\prime}),

(224)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(2\pi)\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\frac{b^{2}+4\pi^{2}}{48}-(2\pi)\delta(\phi)\frac{1}{8}

The recursion relation determines all
V_{g,n}
in terms of these two.

Eqn. (
222
) is the main result of this section. As we show in section
5.4
, it implies the duality between
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity and the
random matrix ensemble. First, we will present some general properties that follow from the recursion relation.

5.3.2
{\cal N}=2
Volumes at “Fixed Charge”

The volume of the three-holed sphere with geodesic boundaries of fixed
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}
is proportional to
\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi+\phi^{\prime}+\phi^{\prime\prime})
. Similarly the kernels appearing in the recursion are sums of delta functions, possibly with derivatives, of a sum of
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomies. This implies, by induction, that the same is true for the volume of every
{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
. More precisely, from the recursion relation, one can deduce an expansion

V_{g,n}({\sf B})=(2\pi)^{2g-1}\sum^{2g-2+n}_{m=0}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(4\widehat{q}^{\,2})^{m}}\delta^{(2m)}(\phi_{{\sf B}})\,v_{g,n,m}(B),

(225)

where we introduced some notation to avoid clutter here and later. On the right hand side,
2m
is the number of derivatives acting on the delta function.
For
{\sf B}=\{{\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{n}\}
,
we define
\phi_{\sf B}=\phi_{1}+\ldots+\phi_{n}
. The function
v_{g,n,m}(B)
depends only on the lengths
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}
. The choice of overall normalization in (
225
) is for later convenience.

It is convenient to introduce “fixed charge” volumes.
The simple dependence of
V_{g,n}(\sf B)
on the holonomies implies the following condition for the Fourier transforms:

\displaystyle\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{n}\phi}{(2\pi)^{n}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\phi_{i}}\,V_{g,n}({\sf B})=0,~~~{\rm unless}~~q_{1}=q_{2}\ldots=q_{n}.

(226)

Note that because
V_{g,n}({\sf B})
is supported at
\phi_{1}+\cdots+\phi_{n}=0
, this integral makes sense and has the stated property as long as all differences

q_{i}-q_{j}
are integers; it is not necessary for the individual
q_{i}
to be integers.
Vanishing of eqn. (
226
) when the
q_{i}
are not all equal is equivalent to the statement that different supermultiplets are statistically independent, to all orders
in the topological expansion.
To study any one supermultiplet, we can set
q_{1}=\ldots=q_{n}=q
and define the “fixed charge” volumes

V^{(q)}_{g,n}(B)=(2\pi)^{2-2g}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{n}\phi}{(2\pi)^{n}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{\sf B}}\,V_{g,n}({\sf B}).

(227)

The normalization will simplify the recursion relation that these volumes satisfy. Here
q
is not the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)

R
-charge of a state, but a label of a supermultiplet.
One can verify (see eqn. (
238
)) that when these volumes are glued to trumpets in order to compute the path integral with NAdS boundary conditions, the
R
-charges of the supermultiplet states are
q\pm\widehat{q}/2
; therefore
q
has the same interpretation as in section
3
.

In Fourier space, the “fixed charge” volumes are simply polynomials in
q^{2}
of the form

V^{(q)}_{g,n}(B)=\sum^{2g-2+n}_{m=1}\Big(\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\Big)^{m}v_{g,n,m}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}).

(228)

One can easily prove this property by induction on the recursion relation, without going through (
225
), since the Fourier transforms of the kernels have
this property. One can also prove, imitating the proof by Mirzakhani, that the coefficient of a given power of
q^{2}
in the fixed charge volumes is a polynomial in the variables
b_{i}^{2}
, of a degree we will specify below.

For future reference in proving the match with the matrix model, it is useful to translate the recursion for the volumes
V_{g,n}({\sf B})
into a recursion for the “fixed charge” volumes
V_{g,n}^{(q)}(B)
. One can simply apply a Fourier transform on (
222
). The result is

\displaystyle bV^{(q)}_{g,n+1}(b,B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}~b^{\prime\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime\prime}~\Big(\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\,{\sf D}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})-{\sf D}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\Big)

(229)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\left(V^{(q)}_{g-1,n+2}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},B)+\sum_{\rm stable}V^{(q)}_{h_{1},|B_{1}|+1}(b^{\prime},B_{1})V^{(q)}_{h_{2},|B_{2}|+1}(b^{\prime\prime},B_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+\sum_{k=1}^{|B|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\Big[\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}(b-{\sf T}_{2}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k}))+{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})\Big]V^{(q)}_{g,n}(b^{\prime},B/b_{k})

This way of presenting the recursion also avoids the cluttering associated to keeping track of the holonomies. The purpose of normalizing the “fixed charge” volumes with a factor of
(2\pi)^{2-2g}
in (
227
) was to obtain (
229
) in this form. The initial conditions for this recursion can be read off from the previous results:

\displaystyle V_{0,3}^{(q)}=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\underbrace{1}_{v_{0,3,1}},~~~~V_{1,1}^{(q)}=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\underbrace{\frac{b^{2}+4\pi^{2}}{48}}_{v_{1,1,1}}+\underbrace{(-\frac{1}{8})}_{v_{1,1,0}}.

(230)

5.3.3
Relation to Bosonic Volumes

There is a simple property we can notice easily starting from (
229
). Consider the coefficient
v_{g,n,m}
with the highest number of derivatives, or equivalently the highest power of
q
, with
m=2g-2+n
. This coefficient satisfies a simplified version of the recursion (
229
) in which we keep only the terms proportional to
q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}
in the recursion kernels. Up to a simple rescaling, this
is precisely the same as the bosonic volume recursion of Mirzakhani, given in (
178
). A careful analysis of the normalization therefore yields the relation

\displaystyle v_{g,n,2g-2+n}(B)=V^{\rm bos.}_{g,n}(B).

(231)

This property holds for the initial conditions given in eqn. (
230
), and the recursion relation implies that it is true for all
g,n
.
We can rewrite the same property in terms of the volumes at fixed holonomies as

\displaystyle V_{g,n}({\sf B})=(2\pi)^{2g-2}\,V^{\rm bos}_{g,n}(B)\,(-1)^{n}\frac{2\pi\delta^{(4g-4+2n)}(\phi_{\sf B})}{(4\widehat{q}^{\,2})^{2g-2+n}}+\ldots.

(232)

In bosonic JT gravity coupled to a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
gauge theory, one would get a similar result, but without the factors
(-1)^{n}/(4\widehat{q}^{\,2})^{2g-2+n}

and without the derivatives acting on the delta function. The derivatives of the delta function have their origin in the fermionic moduli, and
the other extra factors are due to choices of normalization in
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity.

This relation makes it clear that
v_{g,n,2g-2+n}
is a polynomial in
b_{i}^{2}
of degree
3g-3+n
, since it follows from Mirzakhani’s results. Moreover, one can show using similar techniques that
v_{g,n,2g-2+n-s}
is a polynomial in
b_{i}^{2}
of degree
3g-3+n-s
. This implies that
v_{g,n,0}
is a polynomial of degree
g-1
in
b_{i}^{2}
. This is true even for
g=0
, as
v_{0,n,0}=0
.

5.3.4
Some Simple Examples

Next we give some concrete simple examples of
{\cal N}=2
volumes obtained from the recursion relation.

For any genus
g\geq 1
, there can be terms in the volumes with no derivatives acting on the delta function, since this is true for the seed
V_{1,1}({\sf b})
. That is not so for the case of genus zero, since the seed
V_{0,3}
has two derivatives. Therefore all volumes at genus zero are sums of expressions with at least two derivatives. We can analytically compute the two-derivative coefficients and verify that they are non-zero. After performing the integrals over holonomies, the recursion for
v_{0,n,1}
reduces to

bv_{0,n+1,1}(b,B)=\sum_{k=1}^{|B|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}b^{\prime}~{\sf T}_{0}(b,b^{\prime},b_{k})~v_{0,n,1}(b^{\prime},B/b_{k}).

(233)

One can prove by direct evaluation that the following ansatz for the term with two derivatives solves this recursion:

V_{0,n}({\sf B})=(2\pi)^{-1}(-1)^{n}\frac{(n-1)!}{2}\frac{1}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\ldots+\phi_{n})+({\rm higher~derivatives}).

(234)

or equivalently
v_{0,n,1}=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{(n-1)!}{2}
.

To illustrate some of the preceding statements, we can compute
V_{0,4}^{(q)}
and
V_{0,5}^{(q)}
:

\displaystyle V_{0,4}^{(q)}(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q^{4}}{4^{2}\widehat{q}^{\,4}}\underbrace{\frac{4\pi^{2}+b_{1}^{2}+b_{2}^{2}+b_{3}^{2}+b_{4}^{2}}{2}}_{v_{0,4,2}=V^{\rm bos.}_{0,4}(B)}+\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}\underbrace{(-3)}_{v_{0,4,1}},

(235)

\displaystyle V_{0,5}^{(q)}(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4},b_{5})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q^{6}}{4^{3}\widehat{q}^{\,3}}V^{\rm bos.}_{0,5}(B)-\frac{q^{4}}{4^{2}\widehat{q}^{\,4}}\frac{52\pi^{2}+9\sum_{j=1}^{5}b_{j}^{2}}{2}+\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}12.

(236)

From these expressions, we can recover
the bosonic volumes and the two derivative term predicted in eqn. (
233
). We see as well that in these examples, the volumes
are polynomials in the variables
b_{i}^{2}
of the expected degree.

5.4
Equivalence with Random Matrix Recursion

Now we will finally complete the circle of ideas and show that the
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity path integral is equal to the result from the random matrix ensemble that we have described.
We recall eqn. (
167
) expressing partition functions in terms of volumes:

\displaystyle Z_{g,n}=\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}(b_{j}{\mathrm{d}}b_{j})\int_{0}^{2\pi}(2\pi{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{j})Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{j},\alpha_{j};\overline{{\sf b}}_{j})\right]V_{g,n}({\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{n}).

We need a more explicit version of this formula. For this, we use the explicit form (
66
) of the
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity trumpet to rewrite this as

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptZ_{g,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{n}\sum_{q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{i}(q_{i}-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{i}(q_{i}+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)e^{-\beta_{i}E_{0}(q_{i})}\right]

(237)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}{\mathrm{d}}b_{j}\,Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta,b)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{j}}{2\pi}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{j}q_{j}}\,V_{g,n}({\sf b}_{1},\ldots,{\sf b}_{n}),

where the bosonic trumpet is
Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta,b)=e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}}/(2\sqrt{\pi\beta})
.
As in the discussion of eqn. (
226
), the integration over the
\phi_{i}
has simple properties because the integrand is supported at
\phi_{1}+\cdots+\phi_{n}=0
and
the differences of the exponents
q_{i}-q_{j}
are integers.
Then we obtain a partition function consistent, to all orders in
e^{-S_{0}}
