, with statistically independent supermultiplets

Z_{g,n}=(2\pi)^{2g+n-2}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{i}(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha_{i}(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\Big)Z_{g,n}^{(q)}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}),

(238)

where the contribution from each supermultiplet
Z_{g,n}^{(q)}
is given by
29
29

29

We can now complete the proof that the number of BPS states does not fluctuate in gravity. Since, as we argued above,
V^{(q)}_{g,n}
are polynomials in
b_{i}^{2}
, if
E_{0}(q)>0
, the integral over the
b_{i}
cannot produce a dependence with
\beta
that cancels the exponential decay
e^{-\beta E_{0}(q)}
at large
\beta
. For the multiplet with
E_{0}(0)=0
it would require a pole at
b=0
to modify the number of BPS states, also ruled out by the fact that
V_{g,n}^{(q)}
are polynomials.

Z_{g,n}^{(q)}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})=\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{j}{\mathrm{d}}b_{j}\,Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta_{i},b_{i})e^{-\beta_{i}E_{0}(q)}\right]V^{(q)}_{g,n}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}).

(239)

The factor of
(2\pi)^{2g+n-2}
can be absorbed by a shift of
S_{0}
, since the exponent is minus the Euler characteristic of the surface.
For this reason, we start by focusing on
Z_{g,n}^{(q)}
.

To show the equivalence with the matrix model recursion, we will proceed in the following way. Let
Z_{g,n,{\mathrm{M}}}^{(q)}
be the solution of the matrix model recursion of an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
AZ ensemble with spectral curve
y_{q}(x)
and threshold energy
E_{0}(q)
, both for now generic.
30
30

30

{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity has a number of BPS states proportional to
e^{S_{0}}
. We show in appendix
B
that the loop equations of an AZ ensemble with measure
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1+2\nu,2)
are independent of
\nu
when
\nu
is proportional to
L
(apart for allowing a pole in the spectral curve at
x=0
). Therefore the loop equations only depend on
\upalpha_{0}=\upalpha-\upbeta\nu
which we take to be one.

Similarly, define matrix model “volumes”:

Z_{g,n,{\mathrm{M}}}^{(q)}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})=\left[\prod_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{j}{\mathrm{d}}b_{j}Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta_{i},b_{i})e^{-\beta_{i}E_{0}(q)}\right]V^{(q)}_{g,n,{\mathrm{M}}}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}).

(240)

We want to show
that
Z_{g,n,{\mathrm{M}}}^{(q)}
, with the right choice of spectral curve, obeys the same recursion relation as
Z_{g,n}^{(q)}
.
A small generalization of a calculation in
Eynard:2007fi
, which we explain in appendix
B
, shows that one can rewrite the matrix model loop equations (presented in eqn. (
393
)) in terms of the matrix model volumes, as

\displaystyle bV^{(q)}_{g,n+1,{\mathrm{M}}}(b,B)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime\prime}~D_{q}(b^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime},b)

(241)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\times\left(V^{(q)}_{g-1,n+2,{\mathrm{M}}}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},B)+\sum_{\rm stable}V^{(q)}_{h_{1},|B_{1}|+1,{\mathrm{M}}}(b^{\prime},B_{1})V^{(q)}_{h_{2},|B_{2}|+1,{\mathrm{M}}}(b^{\prime\prime},B_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{|B|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b^{\prime}\text{d}b^{\prime}\Big(D_{q}(b^{\prime}+b_{k},b)+D_{q}(b^{\prime}-b_{k},b)\Big)V^{(q)}_{g,n,{\mathrm{M}}}(b^{\prime},B/b_{k})

where the function
D_{q}(x,y)
depends on the spectral curve
y_{q}(x)
through the following integral transform

D_{q}(x,y)=-\int_{\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{e^{-xz}\sinh(yz)}{zy_{q}(E_{0}(q)-z^{2})}.

(242)

The contour of integration is along the imaginary axis with a small positive real part
\varepsilon>0
. This result was deduced in
Eynard:2007fi
for a Dyson ensemble with
\upbeta=2
, but is also valid for an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
AZ ensemble,
since the matrix model recursion is the same.
31
31

31

The matrix model recursion is different for ensembles describing theories with time-reversal symmetries.

The first obvious observation is that the threshold energy has to match the one computed in section
3
for a supermultiplet with average charge
q
, which is
E_{0}(q)=q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}
. We can derive the following identities by summing over residues

\displaystyle-\int_{\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{4\pi}{z\sin 2\pi z}e^{-xz}\sinh(yz)=y+\log\Big(\frac{\cosh^{2}\frac{x-y}{4}}{\cosh^{2}\frac{x+y}{4}}\Big),

(243)

\displaystyle-\int_{\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{4\pi z^{2}}{z\sin 2\pi z}e^{-xz}\sinh(yz)=-\frac{\sinh\frac{x}{2}\sinh\frac{y}{2}}{2(\cosh\frac{x}{2}+\cosh\frac{y}{2})^{2}}

(244)

The first and second lines are also equal to

\displaystyle-\int_{\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{4\pi}{z\sin 2\pi z}e^{-xz}\sinh(yz)=y-{\sf T}_{2}(y,b^{\prime},x-b^{\prime})-{\sf T}_{2}(y,x-b^{\prime},b^{\prime}),

(245)

\displaystyle-\int_{\varepsilon+{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{4\pi z^{2}}{z\sin 2\pi z}e^{-xz}\sinh(yz)=-{\sf T}_{0}(y,b^{\prime},x-b^{\prime})-{\sf T}_{0}(y,x-b^{\prime},b^{\prime})

(246)

These relations imply that the recursion relation we derived for the volumes of moduli spaces of
{\cal N}=2
hyperbolic surfaces, at “fixed charge”
q
, is equivalent to the matrix model loop equation for spectral curve
1/y_{q}(q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}-z^{2})\sim(q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}-z^{2})/\sin(2\pi z)
. To verify the claim, insert this spectral curve in (
242
), then write (
241
) explicitly and compare with (
229
). Working out the prefactors, and including the contribution from the overall
(2\pi)^{2g+n-2}
in (
238
), which further multiplies the spectral curve by a power of
1/2\pi
, we obtain the final version of the spectral curve associated to the matrix model dual to pure
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity

y_{q}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\sin\Big(2\pi\sqrt{-x+\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}\Big)}{4\pi x}.

(247)

This is precisely the spectral curve we obtained in section
3
by performing the gravitational path integral on the disk topology. If we write
x=q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2}-z^{2}
and multiply the spectral curve by
2\pi
, which produces an overall factor of
(2\pi)^{2g+n-2}
, then we obtain
y_{q}(z)\sim\frac{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}{q^{2}}\frac{\sin 2\pi z}{4\pi}
up to a correction of
relative order
1/q
. This is the spectral curve of bosonic JT gravity, up to a rescaling, giving another explanation of the fact that the “fixed charge” volumes are proportional to the bosonic ones at large
q
:
V_{g,n}^{(q)}(B)\sim(q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{\,2})^{2g+n-2}V^{\rm bos}_{g,n}(B)
.

5.5
Deformations of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity

We have successfully proven, to all orders in the topological expansion, that
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity without additional symmetries is dual to the type of random
matrix ensemble proposed in section
2
for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
quantum mechanics, namely the supermultiplets of different
R
-charge are statistically independent, and each is
governed by a
(1,2)
AZ ensemble.

Such ensembles depend on two choices. The first is the spectrum of BPS states for each
R
-charge. This can be extracted from the disk partition function and, for the ensembles relevant to black holes, the BPS spectrum does not fluctuate (this is explained in section
3
). The second is the choice of matrix potential that appears in (
23
), or
equivalently the choice of spectral curve. This is specified by the leading order density of states of non-BPS multiplets in the topological expansion, also computed in section
3
for pure
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity. In this section, we want to address the question of how to give a gravity interpretation to other choices of BPS spectrum and other matrix potentials in the random matrix ensemble.

The recipe in gravity to modify the matrix potential was provided in
Maxfield:2020ale
and
Witten:2020wvy
for bosonic JT gravity, and generalized to
{\mathcal{N}}=1
JT gravity in
Rosso:2021orf
. Introduce a gas of defects in the gravitational path integral of pure JT gravity. In the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
case, the defect species are parametrized by the opening angle
\gamma
, and also a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
R
-symmetry holonomy
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}
around the defect.
32
32

32

This was referred to as
\alpha
in
Maxfield:2020ale
and
Witten:2020wvy
, but we avoid this notation here to avoid confusion with the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
R
-symmetry chemical potential at the NAdS boundaries.
These two parameters can be combined into the following
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
holonomy matrix around the defect

\displaystyle U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\cos\frac{\gamma}{2}&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\sin\frac{\gamma}{2}&0\\
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\sin\frac{\gamma}{2}&e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\cos\frac{\gamma}{2}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\end{array}\right).

In the upper left block, we have simply written the product of a rotation and an
R
-symmetry transformation.
This deformation of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity involves an integral over the location of the defect and a sum over the possible number of them, besides the usual degrees of freedom of pure gravity. For each defect insertion, we add a weighting factor
w
, which is another parameter of the deformation. The monodromy
U
is conjugate to a diagonal matrix of the form (
3.2
) with imaginary geodesic length
b={\mathrm{i}}\gamma
. Therefore the result for the path integral with such defects can be obtained by analytic continuation of the path integral with geodesic boundaries. More precisely, this is true when
0<\gamma\leq\pi
; for attempts to extend the results beyond this range, see
Turiaci:2020fjj
;
Eberhardt:2023rzz
.

For generic values of
\gamma
and
\phi
, the one-defect correction to the disk partition function is given by

\displaystyle Z_{\rm 1-def}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}e^{-\beta E_{0}(q)}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4\beta}}}{2\sqrt{\pi\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\int_{E_{0}(q)}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}Ee^{-\beta E}\frac{we^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}\cosh\big(\gamma\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}\big)}{8\pi^{2}\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}}.

The first line is the analytic continuation of (
66
) upon
b\to{\mathrm{i}}\gamma
. In the second line we wrote the defect partition function in a way that makes evident what is the correction to the non-BPS density of states. A first observation is that the threshold energy continues to be
E=E_{0}(q)
, for each
q
, in the presence of the defect. To be more exact,
this is true order by order in the defect expansion, but the
1/\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}
singularity indicates that there is actually a shift of order
w
in the threshold energy, assuming that
the theory can be described by a matrix integral. A second observation is that the result is complex. It is therefore natural to consider
w
to be a complex parameter as well, and to introduce for each defect species a conjugate defect with holonomy
U^{-1}
and complex conjugate weight. Therefore we include
defect species parametrized by
(\gamma,\phi)
in pairs, such that we preserve unitarity, and to linear order the correction to
\rho_{q}(E)
for
E>E_{0}(q)
is

\rho_{q}(E)=\frac{\sinh\big(2\pi\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}\big)}{8\pi^{3}E}+{\rm Re}\left[w\,e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}\frac{\cosh\big(\gamma\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}\big)}{4\pi^{2}\sqrt{E-E_{0}(q)}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(w^{2}).

(252)

We conclude that adding such generic defects modifies the spectral curve, and therefore the matrix potential, without modifying the number of BPS states. We leave for future work to derive the finite
w
expression for the non-BPS density of states, analogous to the ones derived in
Maxfield:2020ale
and
Witten:2020wvy
. In particular it is important to verify that the resummation over the number of defects can be interpreted in terms of a shift of the threshold energy
E_{0}(q)
, since otherwise the square root singularity of (
252
) near threshold is not consistent with a matrix integral.

Instead of considering further the non-BPS sector, in this article we focus on the new element not present in the previous literature: how to modify the number of BPS
states, or in other words the choice of component of solutions to the constraint
Q^{2}=0
. It turns out this can also be done with a gas of defects. Even though a generic defect does not contribute to the number of BPS states, supersymmetric defects do.
33
33

33

An analog for
{\mathcal{N}}=1
is that the number of supersymmetric states, which vanishes in the absence
of defects, becomes nonzero in the presence of a gas of Ramond punctures
SW
.
Locally, a defect preserves supersymmetry whenever the deficit angle is related to the
R
-symmetry holonomy by

\gamma=2\pi-2\widehat{q}\,|\phi|.

(253)

To write this expression we use that
\phi
can always be defined to be in the range
-\pi\leq\widehat{q}\phi\leq\pi
. When this relation is satisfied, the holonomy (
5.5
) commutes with two out of the four fermionic generators. The sign of
\phi
determines
precisely which fermionic symmetry is preserved by the defect. The partition function on the disk with such a defect inserted is analyzed in appendix
A
. It is not a simple analytic continuation of the usual trumpet partition function and is instead

\displaystyle Z_{\rm susy-defect}(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}n\delta}\widehat{q}\,\cos\big(\frac{\widehat{q}(\alpha+2\pi n)}{2}\big)}{2\pi^{3}\big(1-{\rm sgn}(\phi)\frac{\widehat{q}(\alpha+2\pi n)}{\pi}\big)}e^{\frac{1}{4\beta}(2\pi-2\widehat{q}|\phi|)^{2}-\frac{\widehat{q}^{\,2}}{\beta}(\alpha-\phi+2\pi n)^{2}}

(254)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}n\delta}\widehat{q}\,\cos\big(\frac{\widehat{q}(\alpha+2\pi n)}{2}\big)}{2\pi^{3}\big(1-{\rm sgn}(\phi)\frac{\widehat{q}(\alpha+2\pi n)}{\pi}\big)}~~(\beta\to\infty)

In the second line we take
\beta\to\infty
and therefore project onto BPS states. Using Poisson resummation one can derive

Z_{\rm susy-defect}(\beta\to\infty,\alpha)=w\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha k}\frac{e^{{\rm sgn}(\phi){\mathrm{i}}\pi\frac{k}{\widehat{q}}}}{4\pi^{2}}\Theta(k),

(255)

where we defined the function

\Theta(k)=\begin{cases}1&|k|<\frac{\widehat{q}}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&|k|=\frac{\widehat{q}}{2}\\
0&|k|>\frac{\widehat{q}}{2}.\end{cases}

(256)

Again we see that this correction to the spectrum is complex and therefore for each defect we need to insert an orientation reversed one. The correction to the BPS spectrum is then

N_{\rm BPS}(k)=e^{S_{0}}\frac{\cos(\frac{\pi k}{\widehat{q}})}{4\pi^{2}}+{\rm Re}\left[\frac{w\,e^{{\rm sgn}(\phi){\mathrm{i}}\pi\frac{k}{\widehat{q}}}}{2\pi^{2}}\right]\Theta(k)

(257)

This equation is exact in both
S_{0}
and
w
as long as
0<\gamma\leq\pi
. For
\gamma
in this range, configurations either with handles or with more than one defect do not contribute to the BPS spectrum. The reason is that for
\gamma
in this range, as soon as we have two or more defects or any number of handles there will be a geodesic homotopic to each NAdS boundary. The form of the trumpet partition function guarantees that there will be no contribution to the number of BPS states.

We can consider multiple supersymmetric defect species, with different values of
\phi
. The contribution to the number of BPS states comes only from either the disk, or a disk with one defect (of any species). Since the one defect contribution to the number of BPS states computed in eqn. (
257
) has such a mild dependence on
\phi
, a sum over species will only modify the effective value of
w
. The answer will still take the form in (
257
), at least as long as all defects satisfy
0<\gamma\leq\pi
.

For the reasons given above, the most general shift in the spectrum of BPS states is a linear combination of
\cos(\frac{\pi k}{\widehat{q}})
, associated to the real part of
w
, or
\sin(\frac{\pi k}{\widehat{q}})
, associated to the imaginary part of
w
. Only for
w\in\mathbb{R}
can we preserve charge conjugation invariance, since
\sin(\frac{\pi k}{\widehat{q}})
changes sign under
k\to-k
. Moreover, a contribution
\sin(\frac{\pi k}{\widehat{q}})
produces BPS states at charges
k=\pm\widehat{q}/2
. Since the disk predicts a vanishing number of BPS states at these charges, a model with non-real
w
would seemingly predict a negative number of BPS states for either
k=+\widehat{q}/2
or
-\widehat{q}/2
. Such a theory of gravity would then be apparently non-unitary if
\delta=1/2
, so that the spectrum of charges is half-integral.
34
34

34

A similar issue arises in other examples. For example, adding a gas of defects to bosonic JT gravity can lead to a negative density of states. On the matrix model side, it has been proposed in
Rosso:2021orf
that this signals a phase transition.

Similar to their non-supersymmetric counterpart, the spectrum of non-BPS states is affected by contributions with an arbitrary number of supersymmetric defects, but we will not analyze this problem in this article.

The spectrum of BPS states that we can achieve with a gas of supersymmetric defects is very restrictive. For example if
w\in\mathbb{R}
, then the correction from a defect has the same dependence on
k
as the leading answer from the disk. Interestingly, this change in the spectrum does not allow us to construct a theory of gravity with a number of BPS states reproducing the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SYK answer (with the only exception being
\widehat{q}=3
.) It would be nice to find a gravitational calculation that reproduces the BPS spectrum of SYK for any
35
35

35

We
thank J. Maldacena for raising this question.

\widehat{q}
.

Finally, it is possible to interpret the deformations by a gas of defects as a modification of the dilaton potential of two-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=2
dilaton-gravity, with a modification that
depends on both the dilaton and the scalar field coming from the
R
-symmetry
{\mathrm{U}}(1)

BF
theory. This approach actually explains why we need to consider defects coming in pairs with opposite orientation: in this way the gravity action is real. Since we did not analyze the explicit form of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity action, it is outside of the scope of this article to describe this perspective.

6
Incorporating Time-Reversal Symmetry

6.1
Boundary Viewpoint

In this section, we will incorporate time-reversal symmetry in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
matrix models and JT supergravity.

Time-reversal is an anti-unitary transformation that commutes with the Hamiltonian. But we have to choose whether it will commute or anticommute with the
R
-charge generator

J
. The standard convention in particle physics
36
36

36

The
reason for this convention is that historically,
{\sf T}
was defined to be an approximate symmetry of ordinary matter (originally, it was believed to be an exact symmetry). For this
to be the case, additive conserved charges such as baryon and lepton number must be
{\sf T}
-invariant.

is that an anti-unitary symmetry that commutes with additive conserved charges as well as with the Hamiltonian is called
{\sf T}
, while an anti-unitary symmetry that commutes with the Hamiltonian but anticommutes with additive conserved charges is called
{\sf{CT}}
.
So we will consider two cases:

(A) The boundary quantum mechanics system (or ensemble) may have an antiunitary symmetry
{\sf T}
that commutes with both
H
and
J
. To be a symmetry,
{\sf T}
will have to conjugate

Q
and
Q^{\dagger}
to linear combinations of themselves. Since
[J,Q]=\widehat{q}\,Q
,
[J,Q^{\dagger}]=-\widehat{q}\,Q^{\dagger}
,
{\sf T}
must conjugate
Q
to a multiple of itself and likewise

Q^{\dagger}
. After possibly replacing
{\sf T}
with
{\sf T}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\gamma J}
for some
\gamma
, we can assume that
{\sf T}
commutes with
Q
and
Q^{\dagger}
.
So
{\sf T}
simply commutes with the whole supersymmetry algebra of the boundary theory.

In theories with fermions, one can often define a time-reversal symmetry such that
{\sf T}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
, the operator that distinguishes bosons and fermions. In the present context, however,
we have a fermionic operator
Q
that commutes with
{\sf T}
, and therefore with
{\sf T}^{2}
. So necessarily
{\sf T}^{2}
commutes with fermionic operators, rather than anticommuting with them.
(This leads in Euclidean signature to what is called a
{\mathrm{pin}}^{-}
structure, rather than
{\mathrm{pin}}^{+}
.)

The operator
{\sf T}^{2}
is a unitary operator that commutes with the whole supersymmetry algebra.
The unitary operators that we know about that commute with the supersymmetry algebra are
c
-numbers
e^{{\mathrm{i}}a}
,
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and also operators
e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}nJ/\widehat{q}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We could consider a boundary theory with an additional unitary symmetry
beyond the supersymmetry algebra; then there would also be an additional gauge symmetry in bulk. However, we will avoid introducing additional symmetries.
Given this,
{\sf T}^{2}
must be of the form
e^{{\mathrm{i}}a}e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}nJ/\widehat{q}}
for some
a,n
.
However,
{\sf T}^{2}
is further constrained by the fact that it commutes with
{\sf T}
. The only operators
e^{{\mathrm{i}}a}e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}nJ/\widehat{q}}
that commute with
{\sf T}
are
\pm 1
.
So the only options are
{\sf T}^{2}=\pm 1
.

Since
{\sf T}
commutes with
J
,
{\sf T}
symmetry places no constraint on the charge defect defined by
J=\delta~{\mathrm{mod}}~{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(B) The boundary system may have an antiunitary symmetry
{\sf{CT}}
that commutes with
H
but anticommutes with
J
. In this case,
{\sf{CT}}
must conjugate
Q
to a multiple of

Q^{\dagger}
, and vice-versa. So
{\sf{CT}}Q{\sf{CT}}^{-1}=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha}Q^{\dagger}
,
{\sf{CT}}Q^{\dagger}{\sf{CT}}^{-1}=e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\alpha}Q
,
for some real
\alpha
. By replacing
{\sf{CT}}
with
{\sf{CT}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\alpha J/\widehat{q}}
, we can reduce to
\alpha=0
:

{\sf{CT}}Q{\sf{CT}}^{-1}=Q^{\dagger},~~{\sf{CT}}Q^{\dagger}{\sf{CT}}^{-1}=Q.

(258)

Again
{\sf{CT}}^{2}
commutes with the whole supersymmetry algebra, so if we do not wish to introduce additional symmetries, we must have
{\sf{CT}}^{2}=e^{{\mathrm{i}}a}e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}nJ/\widehat{q}}

for some
a,n
. The condition that
{\sf{CT}}^{2}
commutes with
{\sf{CT}}
implies that actually
{\sf{CT}}^{2}=\pm e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}nJ/\widehat{q}}
for some
n
. But we are still free to substitute

{\sf{CT}}\to{\sf{CT}}e^{-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}kJ/\widehat{q}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, without disturbing eqn. (
258
). By choosing
k
so that either
2k=n
or
2k=n+\widehat{q}
, we can reduce to
{\sf{CT}}^{2}=\pm 1
.

Since
J
is odd under
{\sf{CT}}
, the possible values of
\delta
in a
{\sf{CT}}
-invariant theory are
0,1/2
. (In the reduction in the last paragraph, one has to take into account
that if
\delta=1/2
, then
\exp(2\pi{\mathrm{i}}J)=-1
.)

When the boundary theory has a
{\sf T}
or
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry, we expect the two-dimensional bulk supergravity theory to have such a symmetry as well. In the present article,
we study the bulk theory primarily in Euclidean signature. In that context, it is more natural to consider an ordinary (linear) reflection symmetry rather than an antilinear time-reversal
symmetry. So we make use of the
{\sf{CPT}}
theorem, which tells us that a relativistic theory has a spatial reflection symmetry
{\sf R}
that commutes with additive conserved
charges such as
J
if and only if it has a
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry, and it has a spatial reflection symmetry
{\sf{CR}}
that anticommutes with additive conserved charges such as
J

if and only if it has a
{\sf T}
symmetry.

We will next discuss the random matrix ensembles that are natural in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry extended by
{\sf T}
or
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry.
The bulk picture with
{\sf{CR}}
or
{\sf R}
symmetry is discussed in section
6.2
.

(A) Since
{\sf T}
commutes with
J
, it maps the space of BPS states for any given
J
to itself and places no constraint on the dimensions of these spaces.
37
37

37

There is one
small exception:
if
{\sf T}^{2}=-1
, the dimensions of spaces of BPS states must be even, by what is usually called Kramers doubling. However, this factor of 2
is essentially invisible in a double-scaled model, in which dimensions are proportional to
e^{S_{0}}
. There is a similar doubling for the non-BPS supermultiplets. A random matrix
description of a theory with
{\sf T}^{2}=-1
uses an ensemble in which this doubling is built in.
Likewise, the space of
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
representations is
{\sf T}
-invariant,
for each
k
.

It is straightforward to repeat the analysis of sections
2.2
and
2.3
to incorporate the
{\sf T}
symmetry.
38
38

38

The following amounts mostly to repeating the analysis
in
SW
for the cases that the anomaly coefficient vanishes (no anomaly,
{\sf T}^{2}=1
) or equals 4 (the anomaly is captured by saying
{\sf T}^{2}=-1
).

First, consider a single
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
multiplet
assuming that the others do not exist. We have to modify the analysis of section
2.2
by assuming that the matrix
{\sf Q}
is
{\sf T}
-invariant and similarly
by restricting the symmetry group
G={\mathrm{U}}(L_{k})\times{\mathrm{U}}(L_{k+\widehat{q}})
to its
{\sf T}
-invariant subgroup. For
{\sf T}^{2}=1
, this means that we should assume
{\sf Q}
to be a real
matrix and take
G={\rm O}(L_{k})\times{\rm O}(L_{k+\widehat{q}})
. In other words,
{\sf Q}
is now a bifundamental of a product of orthogonal, rather than unitary, groups.
The derivation of eqn. (
15
) is modified as follows. There is no continuous symmetry rotating the
phase of a given eigenvalue, so
\upalpha=0
in eqn. (
15
); and
w
is real in eqn. (
10
), as a result of which the factors
(\lambda_{i}\pm\lambda_{j})^{2}

in eqn. (
14
) are replaced by
|\lambda_{i}\pm\lambda_{j}|
. The resulting measure is
\prod_{i}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda_{i}\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2}|
,
an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(0,1)
. In the subsequent derivation
assuming the presence of
\nu
zero-modes of charge
k
or
k+\widehat{q}
, there are now
\nu
instead of
2\nu
additional
symmetry generators for each
\lambda_{i}
, so the factor
\lambda_{i}^{2\nu}
in eqn. (
17
) is replaced by
\lambda_{i}^{\nu}
. The final result is then

\prod_{i}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda_{i}\,\lambda_{i}^{\nu}\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_{i}^{2}-\lambda_{j}^{2}|
, an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(\nu,1)
.
We can write this as in eqn. (
33
), but now with
\upbeta=1
:

\upalpha=\upalpha_{0}+\upbeta\nu,\hskip 28.45274pt(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(0,1).

(259)

The derivation in section
2.3
to allow
for potential couplings between the different multiplets requires similar small corrections.
The argument
\widetilde{\lambda}u+\lambda v
of the delta function
in eqn. (
26
) is now real, so the delta function is an ordinary delta function of a real variable, and also
w
is
now real in eqn. (
27
). These effects cancel as before and the multiplets remain statistically independent.
Upon allowing for the presence of BPS states, there are now half as many symmetries mapping a non-vanishing row
or column to a row or column of zeros, so the contribution to
\upalpha
is half as big as before. The final prediction is that the
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
multiplets are statistically independent
and each one is described by an AZ multiplet with parameters satisfying (
259
).

In case
{\sf T}^{2}=-1
,
{\sf Q}
is a matrix of quaternions and the unitary groups
{\mathrm{U}}(L_{k})
and
{\mathrm{U}}(L_{k+\widehat{q}})
are replaced by symplectic groups. In other words,

{\sf Q}
is now a bifundamental of a product of symplectic groups. The group that rotates a single eigenvalue is now
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)\cong{\mathrm{Sp}}(1)
, with dimension 3, leading to
\upalpha_{0}=3
,
and the parameter
w
in eqn. (
10
) is now quaternionic, depending on four real parameters and leading to
\upbeta=4
. After also taking into account that all
parameters are quaternionic and there
are now four generators mapping a nonzero row or column of a bifundamental to a vanishing row or column, one finds that the
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
multiplets are statistically
independent and described by an AZ multiplet with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(3+4\nu,4)
.
So

\upalpha=\upalpha_{0}+\upbeta\nu,\hskip 28.45274pt(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(3,4).

(260)

In a bulk description, the difference between a bifundamental of a pair of orthogonal group and a bifundamental of a pair of symplectic groups is a factor of
(-1)^{\chi}
, that
is a factor of
-1
for each crosscap. This is proved in section 4.2.4 of
SW
using the loop equations.

(B) Since
{\sf{CT}}
anticommutes with
J
, it maps the space of BPS states with
J=k
to the corresponding space with
J=-k
, placing no constraint on the dimensions
L_{k}^{0}

of the spaces of BPS states except that
L_{k}^{0}=L_{-k}^{0}
(and in case
\delta=0
and
{\sf T}^{2}=-1
,
L_{0}^{0}
must be even by Kramers doubling). Similarly, the space
of
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
representations is mapped by
{\sf{CT}}
to the space of
(-k-\widehat{q},-k)
representations. Thus for generic values of
k
,
{\sf{CT}}
merely implies that the random matrix
description of the
(k,k+\widehat{q}\,)
states is the same as the random matrix description of the
(-k-\widehat{q},-k)
states. In terms of the bulk charge
q=k+\widehat{q}/2
introduced in section
3
,
{\sf{CT}}
acts as
q\to-q
.

We learn more only in the case of a supermultiplet with
R
-charges
(-\widehat{q}/2,\widehat{q}/2)
, or equivalently
q=0
. As
\widehat{q}
is odd, this
multiplet only exists if
\delta=1/2
. For
\delta=1/2
,
{\sf{CT}}
maps the
(-\widehat{q}/2,\widehat{q}/2)

states to themselves, and therefore for this multiplet, the random matrix description will be different.
Since
J
has eigenvalues
\pm\widehat{q}/2
for these states, it can be put in a block diagonal form

J=\frac{\widehat{q}}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}1&0\\
\hline\cr 0&-1\end{array}\right).

(261)

The blocks are of equal size, as they are exchanged by
{\sf{CT}}
. Since
{\sf{CT}}
anticommutes with
J
, and
{\sf{CT}}^{2}=\varepsilon
where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, we can assume
{\sf{CT}}
to take the form

{\sf{CT}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&1\\
\hline\cr\varepsilon&0\end{array}\right)\star.

(262)

The symmetry group is reduced to the group of unitary transformations that commute with both
J
and
{\sf{CT}}
. These are unitary
transformations of the form

{\mathcal{U}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}U&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\overline{U}\end{array}\right),

(263)

with
U
unitary (the adoint, complex conjugate, and transpose of a matrix
M
will be denoted
M^{\dagger},\,\overline{M},\,M^{\mathrm{tr}}
).
Thus the symmetry group is
{\mathrm{U}}(L_{\widehat{q}/2})
.
Because
Q
and
Q^{\dagger}
carry
R
-charges
\widehat{q}
and
-\widehat{q}
, they take the general form

Q=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&M\\
\hline\cr 0&0\end{array}\right),\hskip 28.45274ptQ^{\dagger}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&0\\
\hline\cr M^{\dagger}&0\end{array}\right).

(264)

The condition
{\sf{CT}}Q^{\dagger}{\sf{CT}}^{-1}=Q
tells us that
M=\varepsilon M^{\mathrm{tr}}
is symmetric or antisymmetric depending on whether
\varepsilon=+1
or
\varepsilon=-1
,
and the symmetry
Q\to{\mathcal{U}}Q{\mathcal{U}}^{-1}
becomes

M\to UMU^{\mathrm{tr}}
. In other words,
M
is a complex second rank tensor of the unitary group
{\mathrm{U}}(L_{\widehat{q}/2})
, a symmetric tensor if
\varepsilon=1
and an antisymmetric
one if
\varepsilon=-1
.

Ensembles of such symmetric or antisymmetric matrices with unitary symmetry also arise in
{\mathcal{N}}=1
JT supergravity with anomaly coefficient 2 or 6 mod 8
SW
.
The symmetric tensors correspond to an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,1)
and the antisymmetric tensors correspond to an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,4)
.
This can be shown by arguments similar to those in section
2
. We consider in turn the two cases:

(1) The canonical form of a symmetric second rank tensor
M
is
{\mathrm{diag}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots)
,
with all
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
. For each
j
, the symmetry group
{\rm U}(L_{\widehat{q}/2})
has one generator rotating the phase of
\lambda_{j}
; this leads to
\upalpha=1
.
For each pair
\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}
, the symmetry group has two generators rotating the corresponding eigenvectors of
M
among themselves, as opposed to the four
in eqns. (
10
), (
12
). This leads to
\upbeta=1
rather than 2. So the ensemble is an AZ multiplet with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,1)
.

(2) The canonical form of an antisymmetric tensor
M
is a direct sum of

2\times 2
blocks of the form
\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{j}\cr-\lambda_{j}&0\end{pmatrix}
, with all
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
. The unitary group
{\mathrm{U}}(L_{\widehat{q}/2})
has for each
j

a
{\mathrm{U}}(2)
subgroup acting on this block, of which an
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
subgroup leaves
M
invariant and the one remaining generator rotates the phase of
\lambda_{j}
, leading to
\upalpha=1
. By looking at generators that act between a pair of these
2\times 2
blocks of
M
, one finds

\upbeta=4
. So
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,4)
for antisymmetric tensors.

There are no BPS states of charge
\pm\widehat{q}/2
, so for these multiplets, there is no
distinction betweeen
\upalpha
and
\upalpha_{0}
.

In a bulk description, the difference between a random ensemble of symmetric complex tensors with unitary symmetry and
a random ensemble of antisymmetric complex tensors with that symmetry consists of a factor
(-1)^{\chi}
, just like the difference between the two cases in (A). This is proved using the
loop equations in section 4.2.4 of
SW
, and it also has a simple proof based on Feynman diagrams explained in appendix B of that paper.

6.2
The Cross-Cap With
{\sf R}
or
{\sf{CR}}
Symmetry

In Lorentz signature with coordinates
t=x^{0},x=x^{1}
, consider gamma matrices
\gamma_{\mu}
,
\mu=0,1
satisfying
\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=\pm 2\eta_{\mu\nu}
with

\eta_{\mu\nu}={\mathrm{diag}}(-1,1)
. The massless Dirac equation
(\gamma^{0}\partial_{0}+\gamma^{1}\partial_{1})\psi=0
has a time-reversal symmetry

{\sf T}\psi(t,x)=\gamma_{0}\psi(-t,x)
and a reflection symmetry
{\sf R}\psi(t,x)=\gamma_{1}\psi(t,-x)
. We observe that
{\sf T}^{2}=\pm 1
acting on
\psi
if and only if
{\sf R}^{2}=\mp 1
acting on

\psi
; the
signs are determined by the sign in the Clifford algebra. An operator version of this statement is that
39
39

39

Here we are considering
{\sf T}^{2}
and
{\sf R}^{2}
as automorphisms of
the operator algebra. Even if
{\sf T}^{2}
or
{\sf R}^{2}
is the identity as an automorphism of the operator algebra, it may still act as
-1
on a quantum Hilbert space, under some
conditions.

{\sf T}^{2}=1
or
(-1)^{\sf F}
if and only if
{\sf R}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
or 1.
This relationship is actually a general prediction of the
{\sf{CPT}}
theorem.

Here we did not incorporate any additive conserved charge. If one does so, for example by taking
\psi
to be a complex fermion with a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
symmetry, one
finds that because time-reversal acts by an antilinear operator while a spatial reflection acts by a linear one, time-reversal will commute with the conserved charges if and only if the
reflection anticommutes with them,
and vice-versa. To see this, expand
\psi
in Majorana fermions by
\psi=\psi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{2}
. A time-reversal symmetry can be defined to act by, say,

\psi_{1}(t,x)\to\gamma_{0}\psi_{1}(-t,x)
,
\psi_{2}(t,x)\to\varepsilon\gamma_{0}\psi_{2}(-t,x)
, with
\varepsilon=\pm 1
. Regardless of what signs we pick in the definition of time-reversal,
the reflection symmetry that is related to it by the
{\sf{CPT}}
theorem will act with the same signs:
\psi_{1}(t,x)\to\gamma_{1}\psi(t,-x)
,
\psi_{2}(t,x)\to\varepsilon\gamma_{1}\psi_{2}(t,-x)
.
Then, as time-reversal is anti-unitary, we see that it maps
\psi
to
\psi^{\dagger}=\psi_{1}-{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{2}
if
\varepsilon=1
, but to
\psi
if
\varepsilon=-1
. For a unitary reflection
symmetry, these statements are reversed.

So a
{\sf T}
symmetry satisfying
{\sf T}^{2}=1
(or
(-1)^{\sf F}
) is always accompanied by a
{\sf{CR}}
symmetry satisfying
{\sf{CR}}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
(or 1), and a
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry satisfying

{\sf{CT}}^{2}=1
(or
(-1)^{\sf F}
) is always accompanied by an
{\sf R}
symmetry satisfying
{\sf R}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
(or 1).
The boundary ensembles discussed in section
6.1
have a
{\sf T}
or
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry satisfying
{\sf T}^{2}=1
or
{\sf{CT}}^{2}=1
, so they will have a
{\sf{CR}}
or
{\sf R}
symmetry
satisfying
{\sf{CR}}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
or
{\sf R}^{2}=(-1)^{\sf F}
.

One more important detail that can be understood from the preceding derivation is the following. To get
{\sf T}^{2}=1
acting on
\psi
, the sign in the Clifford algebra was

\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=-2\eta_{\mu\nu}
. This Clifford algebra has a two-dimensional real representation, so Majorana fermions with
{\sf T}^{2}=1
can exist in two
spacetime dimensions. However, when we Wick rotate to Euclidean signature, where we will perform computations, the Clifford
algebra becomes
\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=-2\delta_{\mu\nu}
. This algebra does not have a two-dimensional real representation,
but it does have a representation by imaginary
2\times 2
matrices. The formula

{\sf R}\psi(t,x)=\gamma_{1}\psi(t,-x)
shows that in acting on fermions
{\sf R}
(or
{\sf{CR}}
if conserved charges are incorporated) is proportional to a gamma matrix, so we have to expect
that as a
2\times 2
matrix acting on fermions,
{\sf R}
or
{\sf{CR}}
can be chosen to be imaginary but cannot chosen to be real.
Since
\gamma_{1}^{2}=-1
,
\gamma_{1}
has eigenvalues
\pm{\mathrm{i}}

and determinant 1; hence we expect
{\sf R}
to have determinant 1 as an operator on fermions.

Figure 12:
(a)
C
is a cylinder with opposite points on the right boundary identified (as schematically indicated by the
\Large\times
) to make
an unorientable “crosscap” manifold. After this identification, the boundary on the right becomes a circle of half the original circumference,
and it is no longer a boundary but an interior circle, ultimately an interior geodesic.
A flat connection on
C
with holonomy
U
around the boundary and holonomy
V
around the interior geodesic can exist
if and only if
V^{2}=U
. (b) To calculate the torsion, one can use the very simple cell decomposition of
C
(or more precisely of a topological space that for these
purposes is equivalent to
C
)
indicated here, with a single vertex
P
, two one-cells labeled
V
and
U
(the two one-cells labeled
V
that appear in the diagram are identified to make the crosscap), and
one two-cell.

With these preliminaries out of the way, our goal now is to compute the “torsion” (or moduli space integration measure) for a basic unorientable two-manifold
from which all others can be built.
This is the “crosscap” manifold (fig.
12
(a)), which is obtained from a cylinder by identifying opposite points on one boundary, to build an unorientable manifold
C

with a single boundary circle.
Gluing this manifold onto a trumpet gives an unorientable asymptotically NAdS spacetime
C^{\prime}
with one asymptotic boundary; the path integral
on
C^{\prime}
is expected to reproduce the leading correction to the random matrix partition function due to time-reversal symmetry. The main novel step
in computing this correction is to compute the torsion of a flat connection on
C
. Once this is known, relatively standard steps similar to those that we have already explained
lead to the prediction for the correction to the partition function.

In computing the torsion, we consider a flat connection on
C
in which the holonomy around the boundary is
U
and the holonomy around the “interior” circle
\gamma
where the
identification is made is
V
. (
\gamma
will ultimately be a geodesic in a hyperbolic metric.)
Flatness of the connection implies that
U=V^{2}
, because the boundary on the left of fig.
12
(a) is homotopic to a double cover of
\gamma
.
We can satisfy these conditions with

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}e^{\frac{b}{2}}&0&0\\
0&-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}e^{-\frac{b}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{2{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\end{array}\right),\hskip 28.45274ptV=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\mathrm{i}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{\frac{b}{4}}&0&0\\
0&-{\mathrm{i}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{-\frac{b}{4}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}\end{array}\right).

(265)

Not coincidentally, this is the same
U
that we used in eqn. (
3.2
) to describe a trumpet with an inner geodesic of
length
b
and an
R
-symmetry parameter
\phi
. Clearly,
V^{2}=U
,
and if we set
b=\phi=0
so that
U
reduces to
{\mathrm{diag}}(-1,-1|1)
, then
V
has the properties described earlier for a reflection
{\sf R}
(acting on the fermions, it is imaginary
with determinant 1). Replacing
{\mathrm{i}}
by
-{\mathrm{i}}
in the formula for
V
would give an equivalent formula, up to a shift of
\phi
by
2\pi
.

In eqn. (
265
),
V
is an element of the supergroup
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, or more exactly its complexification,
as is appropriate for a model with reflection symmetry
{\sf R}
. To get
{\sf{CR}}
symmetry, we have to combine the expression for
V
with a charge conjugation operation;
to postpone this slight complication, we consider
{\sf R}
symmetry first, case (B) in the language of section
6.1
.

In section 3.4.5 of
SW
, using the existence of a very simple cell decomposition of
the crosscap manifold (fig.
12
(b)), a simple general recipe was described to compute
\tau_{C}
, the torsion of a flat bundle on
C
. Here we will just use this
recipe and refer to the paper for an explanation.

For
x\in\mathfrak{g}
, let
\widehat{V}(x)=VxV^{-1}
. Then the torsion of the crosscap manifold
C
is
{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{V}-1)
. Decompose the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of

{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, as
\mathfrak{g}^{0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
is the kernel of
\widehat{V}-1
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\perp}
is its orthocomplement. For the case of
{\sf R}
symmetry,
\mathfrak{g}^{0}
consists of the subalgebra of diagonal matrices
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}
consists of the strictly off-diagonal matrices. Because this decomposition is
\widehat{V}
-invariant, one has a factorization

\tau_{C}=\tau_{C}^{0}\tau_{C}^{\perp},

(266)

with

\tau_{C}^{\perp}={\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{V}^{\perp}-1),\hskip 28.45274pt\tau_{C}^{0}={\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{V}^{0}-1).

(267)

Here
\widehat{V}^{0}
and
\widehat{V}^{\perp}
are the restrictions of
\widehat{V}
to to
\mathfrak{g}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}
.
For the case of
{\sf{CR}}
symmetry that we consider later, the decomposition
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{\perp}
is slightly different, but it is still
\widehat{V}
-invariant, so eqn. (
266
) still holds.

\widehat{V}^{\perp}-1
is invertible and preserves the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading of the Lie algebra, with separate blocks
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{+}-1
and
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{-}-1

acting on the bosonic and fermionic parts of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\perp}
. So

{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{V}^{\perp}-1)=\frac{|\det(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{+}-1)|}{\det(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{-}-1)}.

(268)

(See the paragraph after (
150
) for a definition of
{\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}
.)
In the present example, the eigenvalues of
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{+}-1
are
-e^{\pm b/2}-1
, leading to
|\det\,(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{+}-1)|=(1+e^{b/2})(1+e^{-b/2})=4\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}.
The action on the fermionic generators is

\widehat{V}:\begin{pmatrix}{\sf q}_{1}\cr{\sf q}_{2}\cr{\sf q}_{3}\cr{\sf q}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}{\mathrm{i}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{\frac{b}{4}}{\sf q}_{1}\cr{\mathrm{i}}\,e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{\frac{b}{4}}{\sf q}_{2}\cr-{\mathrm{i}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{-\frac{b}{4}}{\sf q}_{3}\cr-{\mathrm{i}}\,e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}}e^{-\frac{b}{4}}{\sf q}_{4}\end{pmatrix}.

(269)

Therefore the eigenvalues of
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{-}-1
are
-1\pm{\mathrm{i}}e^{\pm b/4}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi/2}
and
-1\pm{\mathrm{i}}e^{\pm b/4}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi/2}
, so

\det\,(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{-}-1)=4(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}
. Combining these results, we get

\tau_{C}^{\perp}=\frac{\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}}{(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}}.

(270)

\tau_{C}^{0}
can be computed precisely as in the derivation of
eqn. (3.49) of
SW
. It is a simple ratio of measures for parameters
b,\phi
that appear in
U
and
V
and potential gluing parameters
\varrho,\varphi

for gluing the left boundary in fig.
12
(a) onto something else:

\tau_{C}^{0}=\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi({\mathrm{d}}\varrho{\mathrm{d}}\varphi)^{-1}.

(271)

The factor of
1/4
here has the same origin as the
1/2
in eqn. (3.49) of
SW
: one can write
\tau_{C}^{0}={\mathrm{d}}\overline{b}{\mathrm{d}}\overline{\phi}({\mathrm{d}}\varrho{\mathrm{d}}\varphi)^{-1}
, where

\overline{b}
is the length of the interior geodesic in the figure and
\overline{\phi}
is the
R
-symmetry monodromy around this geodesic; as
b=2\overline{b}
,
\phi=2\overline{\phi}
,
this leads to eqn. (
271
).
So the crosscap torsion is

\tau_{C}=\tau_{C}^{\perp}\tau_{C}^{0}=\frac{\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}}{4(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi({\mathrm{d}}\varrho{\mathrm{d}}\varphi)^{-1}.

(272)

To turn this into a prediction that can be compared to random matrix theory, we have to combine this result with the boundary path integral of the Schwarzian mode.
As in eqn. (
159
), the trumpet path integral with boundary conditions natural for the torsion calculation is given by

\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)=Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\frac{\sinh\frac{b}{2}}{\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi},

(273)

where
Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
was computed in (
66
). A trumpet that ends on a crosscap can be obtained by gluing an ordinary trumpet onto the crosscap manifold of fig.
12
(a). The gluing law for the torsion
gives a formula for the path integral that results from this gluing:

Z_{\rm xcap}(b,\phi)=\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi){\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi\frac{1}{\tau_{S}}\tau_{C},

(274)

Using eqn. (
273
) for
\widetilde{Z}_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
, eqn. (
153
) for
\tau_{S}
, and eqn. (
272
) for
\tau_{C}
, we get finally the measure
for a trumpet that ends on a crosscap:

Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf{CT}}}(b,\phi)=Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\frac{(\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)\coth\frac{b}{4}}{8(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\phi,

(275)

where we make explicit the fact that this is the prediction in a model
with
{\sf{CT}}
or
{\sf R}
symmetry.
Since we have already computed
Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
, this result can be compared to the matrix model prediction, as we will do in section
6.3
.

Now we consider the case of
{\sf T}
or
{\sf{CR}}
symmetry, case (A) in the language of section
6.1
. This means that we have to include charge conjugation, which we will
call
{\sf C}
, as part of the monodromy. As we explain in detail momentarily,
{\sf C}
is an outer automorphism of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, satisfying
{\sf C}^{2}=1
. It acts trivially on
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)
but reverses the
sign of the
R
-charge generator, which
was called
\sf z
in eqn. (
4.2.1
). Roughly, including
{\sf C}
means replacing the monodromy
V
by
V_{\sf C}={\sf C}V
. However, because the
R
-charge is odd under
{\sf C}
, the conjugacy
class of
V_{\sf C}
is independent of
\phi
. To see this, write more explicitly
V_{\phi}
for the
\phi
-dependent matrix
V
defined in eqn. (
265
), and observe that,
because
{\sf z}
is odd under
{\sf C}
, we have

e^{\lambda{\sf z}}{\sf C}V_{\phi}e^{-\lambda{\sf z}}={\sf C}e^{-2\lambda{\sf z}}V_{\phi}={\sf C}V_{\phi-\lambda/\widehat{q}}
.
Hence by conjugation we can set
\phi=0
in
V
, in which case also
\phi=0
in
U=V^{2}
(concretely, this happens because as the
R
-charge is odd under
{\sf C}
,

U={\sf C}V_{\phi}{\sf C}V_{\phi}
is actually independent of
\phi
). So we consider the monodromies

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}-e^{\frac{b}{2}}&0&0\\
0&-e^{-\frac{b}{2}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1\end{array}\right),\hskip 28.45274ptV_{\sf C}={\sf C}V={\sf C}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}{\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{b}{4}}&0&0\\
0&-{\mathrm{i}}e^{-\frac{b}{4}}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1\end{array}\right).

(276)

{\sf C}
cannot be written as a
3\times 3
matrix, because it is an outer automorphism of the Lie algebra of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
that
conjugates the
2|1
-dimensional representation that we use in writing
most formulas
into an inequivalent representation, with reversed
R
-charges. (The formula
V_{\sf C}={\sf C}V
should really be interpreted as a formula in the group or in a
{\sf C}
-invariant
representation such as the adjoint representation, not a formula in
3\times 3
matrices as written in eqn. (
276
).)

{\sf C}
acts on the Lie algebra of
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1|1)
, in the notation of eqn. (
4.2.1
), by

\displaystyle{\sf q}_{1}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow-{\sf q}_{2}

(277)

\displaystyle{\sf q}_{3}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow{\sf q}_{4}

(278)

\displaystyle{\sf z}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow-{\sf z},

(279)

with
{\sf e},{\sf f},{\sf h}
invariant. This defines an automorphism of the Lie algebra, and clearly
{\sf C}^{2}=1
.

To compute the torsion, we have to replace
\widehat{V}(x)=VxV^{-1}
by
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}(x)={\sf C}(VxV^{-1})
with
V
as defined in eqn. (
276
).
One now has
\tau_{C}^{\perp}={\mathrm{Ber}}^{\prime}(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{{\sf C}}-1)
.
In the previous example,
\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}_{+}
was spanned by the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)
generators
{\sf e,f}
. These generators are
{\sf C}
-invariant,
so acting on them,
there is no difference between
\widehat{V}
and
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}
, and they contribute a factor
4\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}
to
|\det(\widehat{V}_{{\sf C}\perp,+}-1)|
, as before.
However, as the
R
-charge generator
\sf z
is
{\sf C}
-odd, it is not in the kernel of
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}-1
and instead has eigenvalue
-2
.
Hence it contributes a factor of 2 to
|\det\,(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{{\sf C},+}-1)|
, which now equals

8\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}
.
Acting on
\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}_{-}
,
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}
is quite different from
\widehat{V}
. It acts by

\widehat{V}_{\sf C}:\begin{pmatrix}{\sf q}_{1}\cr{\sf q}_{2}\cr{\sf q}_{3}\cr{\sf q}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}-{\mathrm{i}}e^{b/4}{\sf q}_{2}\cr-{\mathrm{i}}e^{b/4}{\sf q}_{1}\cr-{\mathrm{i}}e^{-b/4}{\sf q}_{4}\cr-{\mathrm{i}}e^{-b/4}{\sf q}_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(280)

From this, one finds
\det(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{{\sf C},-}-1)=4\cosh^{2}\frac{b}{4}=\frac{1}{2}\det(\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{{\sf C},+}-1)
. So eqn. (
270
) is replaced by

\tau_{C}^{\perp}=2.

(281)

What about
\tau_{C}^{0}
? The kernel of
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}
is generated by
\sf h
, and the same derivation as in eqn. (3.49) of
SW
leads to

\tau_{C}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\varrho^{-1}
. Putting these factors together,

\tau_{C}=\tau_{C}^{\perp}\tau_{C}^{0}={\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\varrho^{-1}.

(282)

This is a measure for a flat connection on the crosscap manifold, where the monodromy
U
around the outer circle automatically has
\phi=0
.

The prediction for the path integral measure for a trumpet ending on a crosscap in a theory with
{\sf T}
symmetry can be deduced from eqn. (
274
) using
this formula for
\tau_{C}
:

Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}(b)=Z_{\rm tr}(b,0)\frac{\cosh\frac{b}{4}}{\sinh\frac{b}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}b{\mathrm{d}}\varphi.

(283)

We have written the left hand side only as a function of
b
, with no
\phi
; and on the right hand side, we have set
\phi=0
in the trumpet partition function
Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)
.

Compared to eqn. (
275
), we see a notable difference: there is no
{\mathrm{d}}\phi
, and instead there is a
{\mathrm{d}}\varphi
. Technically, the reason that this happened
is that because the
R
-charge generator
\sf z
is not annihilated by
\widehat{V}_{\sf C}
,
\tau_{C}^{0}
did not contain a factor
{\mathrm{d}}\phi({\mathrm{d}}\varphi)^{-1}
that was present in the previous
analysis. Eqn. (
283
) is telling us that instead of integrating over an
R
-symmetry angle
\phi
in the monodromy
V
, we have to integrate over an
R
-symmetry
parameter
\varphi
that appears in gluing the crosscap to the trumpet.

The fact that there is no
R
-symmetry angle
\phi
to integrate over is indeed something we already know, since the monodromies
(
276
) do not depend on any such parameter.
But why are we supposed to integrate over an
R
-symmetry gluing parameter
\varphi
? The point is that, at the asymptotic boundary of the trumpet (or of any NAdS
2

spacetime), gauge transformations are not allowed. So if
A
is the
R
-symmetry gauge field, we can define a gauge-invariant parameter
\vartheta=\int_{\ell}A
, where
the integration path
\ell
starts on the asymptotic boundary of the trumpet, heads in to the geodesic
\gamma
, winds once around
\gamma
, and then heads back out to
the asymptotic boundary. In our previous analysis with
{\sf R}
symmetry rather than
{\sf{CR}}
, the parameter
\vartheta
would have no gauge-invariant content (beyond what is contained in
the monodromy
V
around
\gamma
) because the parts of the integral from the asymptotic boundary to
\gamma
and back again would cancel. However, with
{\sf{CR}}
symmetry,
because the monodromy around
\gamma
involves the action of
{\sf C}
, under which
A
is odd, the “incoming” and “outgoing” parts of the integral add, instead of canceling.
Since these integrals do add, one can identify
\vartheta
as
2\varphi
, where
\varphi
is the
R
-symmetry gluing parameter that we have to integrate over, according to
eqn. (
283
). The range of the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy parameter
\vartheta
is
0\leq\vartheta\leq 2\pi
, so the range of
\varphi=\vartheta/2
is
0\leq\varphi\leq\pi
.
Integration over
\varphi
with measure
{\mathrm{d}}\varphi
just gives a constant factor
\pi
, since the integrand does not depend on
\varphi
.

Finally, we discuss some topological details that will be important in understanding and completing the calculations in section
6.3
.
In the case of
{\sf T}
symmetry,
U
and
V
are unique, up to conjugation. However, with
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry,
U
has an arbitrary
R
-symmetry monodromy
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}
and
for each choice of
\phi
, there are two possible choices of
V
. For given
\phi
, each choice of
V
corresponds to a flat
{\mathrm{U}}(1)_{R}
bundle
{\mathcal{L}}\to C

with holonomy
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\widehat{q}\phi}
around the boundary of
C
. The two flat bundles differ by the value of the first Chern class
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})
. This is a relative
first Chern class, because
C
has a boundary; one cannot say which choice of
V
corresponds to
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})=0
and which corresponds to
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})\not=0
,
but one can say that they are different.
40
40

40

The topology is easier to describe if we turn
C
into a compact manifold without boundary by gluing on a disc. In that
way we make a closed, unorientable manifold which is a copy of
{\mathbb{RP}}^{2}
. A flat
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
bundle over this manifold is either trivial, or else has holonomy
-1
around the
generator of
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{RP}}^{2})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. The first Chern class of this nontrivial flat bundle is the nonzero element of
H^{2}({\mathbb{RP}}^{2},{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. This
is a torsion element, so it cannot be detected by curvature. In general the first Chern class of a line bundle with connection is determined by the curvature of the connection
only modulo torsion.

{\sf{CT}}
invariance implies that
the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)_{R}
theta-angle has the possible values
0
and
\pi
. According to eqn. (
39
),
\theta=\pi
if and only if
\delta=0
. In the case of a Riemann
surface
C
without boundary, the meaning of
\theta=\pi
is that the path integral with a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
should be weighted by a factor
(-1)^{c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})}
.
In the present case, because
C
has a boundary and
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})
is a relative first Chern class, it is subtle to explain what is meant by
(-1)^{c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})}.

A simple statement, however, is that when we multiply
Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf{CT}}}
by the trumpet path integral,
which for
\delta=0
is odd under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
, the two choices of
V
will contribute with opposite signs. This is a manifestation of the fact that
\theta=\pi
if
\delta=0
.
If
\delta=1/2
, the trumpet is even under
\phi\to\phi+2\pi
, so the two choices of
V
will contribute with the same sign, in accord with the fact that
\theta=0
.

However,
Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf{CT}}}(b,\phi)-Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf{CT}}}(b,\phi+2\pi)
is imaginary, so if the two choices of
V
contribute with opposite signs, then
it seems that the path integral will be imaginary. Euclidean path integrals can be complex-valued in general, but an imaginary result here will not agree with expectations
from the boundary matrix ensemble. In fact, a rather subtle additional factor of

\pm{\mathrm{i}}
appears precisely when
\delta=0
. A
{\sf{CT}}
-invariant (or
{\sf T}
-invariant) theory with
n
Majorana fermions all transforming in the same way under
{\sf{CT}}
(or
{\sf T}
)
has an anomaly that depends on the value of
n
mod 8. The
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian theory has two Majorana fermions, which transform oppositely under
{\sf T}
, leading to no anomaly,
but transform the same way under
{\sf{CT}}
, so that the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian theory with
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry
has an anomaly coefficient
41
41

41

In
{\mathcal{N}}=1
JT supergravity, the Schwarzian theory has a single
Majorana fermion, and is anomalous even in the absence of time-reversal symmetry. This was important in
SW
. The
\eta
-invariant entered in that analysis
for reasons similar to what we explain here.
of 2. If
\delta=1/2
, the boundary theory is anomalous in the same
way and the anomalous Schwarzian theory correctly reproduces the anomaly in the microscopic boundary description,
42
42

42

We can realize
\delta=1/2
with an
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SYK model
with an odd number
N=2r+1
of complex fermions. This is equivalent to
4r+2
Majorana fermions, so the anomaly coefficient is
4r+2
, or
\pm 2
mod 8. Thus the boundary
anomaly either equals the anomaly of the Schwarzian theory or differs from it by 4.
If the two coefficients are equal, the Schwarzian theory simply reproduces the anomaly of the boundary theory. If they differ by 4, we can compensate
with a bulk factor
(-1)^{\chi}
, which carries an anomaly coefficient of 4. Including this factor
is a familiar option and does not affect the fact that the path integral is real.
but if
\delta=0
, the boundary theory
is anomaly-free and we need to modify the bulk theory to compensate for the anomaly of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian theory. In appendix
A
, a factor in computing
the partition function of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian theory was
{\mathrm{Pf}}(D)
, where
{\mathrm{Pf}}
is the Pfaffian (the square root of the determinant) and
D
, the rotation generator, can be viewed
as the 1-dimensional Dirac operator
of the two Majorana fermions that are described by the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Schwarzian theory. This Dirac operator is defined on
S
, the boundary of the spacetime
C^{\prime}
obtained
by gluing the crosscap to the trumpet. A general recipe
43
43

43

This recipe is suggested by
the Dai-Freed theorem
DF
and was most fully justified from a physical point of view in
WY
.
to define a potentially anomalous
fermion path integral on a manifold
S=\partial C^{\prime}
is to replace
{\mathrm{Pf}}(D)
with
|{\mathrm{Pf}}(D)|\exp(-{\mathrm{i}}\pi\eta(D_{C^{\prime}})/2)
, where
\eta(D_{C^{\prime}})
is the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer
eta-invariant of a bulk Dirac operator

D_{C^{\prime}}
that extends
D
. In the present case, we can define
D_{C^{\prime}}
as the Dirac operator (for some
{\rm pin}^{-}
structure)
coupled to a flat gauge field on
C^{\prime}
defined by the monodromy
V
of eqn. (
265
) with
\phi
replaced by
\alpha
. Any other choice of
D_{C^{\prime}}
is equivalent topologically to this, possibly with a different
{\rm pin}^{-}
structure (or equivalently with
\phi=\alpha+2\pi
). To see the consequences, first note that if
\alpha=0
, then

D_{C^{\prime}}
is the direct sum of two copies of a Dirac operator coupled to a
{\rm pin}^{-}
bundle. Its
\eta
-invariant is then
-1
or

1
, depending on the
{\rm pin}^{-}
structure, so
\exp(-{\mathrm{i}}\pi\eta/2)=\pm{\mathrm{i}}
. When one varies
\alpha
, because
\eta
is a topological invariant on a closed manifold of even dimension,

\eta(D_{C^{\prime}})
is constant except for jumps that precisely ensure that
|{\rm Pf}(D)|e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\pi\eta(D_{C^{\prime}})/2}
varies smoothly with
\alpha
. So
|{\rm Pf}(D)|e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\pi\eta(D_{C^{\prime}})/2}=\pm{\mathrm{i}}\,{\rm Pf}(D)
(with a sign that does not depend on
\alpha
), and the effect of including the eta-invariant and canceling the anomaly is just to multiply the path integral of the Schwarzian modes, naively

{\rm Pf}(D)
, by an overall factor
\pm{\mathrm{i}}
. The choice of
sign leads to two bulk theories that differ by a factor of
(-1)^{\chi}
, dual as usual to two options in the boundary theory.

6.3
The Matrix Model Prediction

We will now compare to matrix model predictions in the two cases of
{\sf T}
and
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry.

(A) We begin with
{\sf T}
symmetry, and consider statistically independent multiplets with
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(0,1)
or
(3,4)
. The spectral curve of a supermultiplet with charges
(q-\widehat{q}/2,q+\widehat{q}/2)
is given by eqn. (
52
). We remind the reader that in gravity it is more convenient to label a multiplet by the bulk charge
q
, which is also equal to the average microscopic
R
-charge of the multiplet. Let us first write down the prediction from
{\cal N}=2
JT gravity for the partition function of the crosscap more explicitly

\displaystyle Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b\,Z_{\rm tr}(b,0)\pi\coth\Big(\frac{b}{4}\Big)

(284)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}+\delta-\frac{1}{2}}\big(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q-\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}+e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha(q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2})}\big)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b\,Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta,b)V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}(b),

where we introduced the “fixed charge” volume for the crosscap

V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}(b)=\frac{\coth(\frac{b}{4})}{2}.\hskip 28.45274pt{\rm Case~A}

(285)

A factor of
1/(2\pi)
comes from the normalization of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
trumpet in terms of its bosonic counterpart
Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos}
. This definition of the “fixed charge” crosscap volume in terms of the partition function is consistent with eqns. (
238
) and (
239
) specialized to
g=1/2
and
n=1
. We used a subscript
{\sf T}
in
V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}

to distinguish this crosscap volume from the one in case (B) below. The goal of this section is to reproduce eqn. (
285
) from the matrix model.

The genus
g=1/2
correction
R^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}(x)
to the one boundary resolvent
R(x)
of a supermultiplet
q
can be derived from the matrix model loop equation, which we review in appendix
B
, and is given by

R^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}(x)=\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{1}{x^{\prime}-x}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma(x^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{\sigma(x)}}\left(\frac{(1-\frac{2}{\upbeta})y^{\prime}(x^{\prime})}{2y(x^{\prime})}+\frac{\upalpha_{0}-1}{2\upbeta x^{\prime}}\right),

(286)

and we can use
\sigma(x)=(x-\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}})
(this function is introduced in the appendix), since there is a single edge in the double-scaling limit. The integration contour for
x^{\prime}
starts as a small circle around
x
and can be deformed to compute the discontinuity along the cut. The answer for an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(0,1)
AZ ensemble with spectral curve (
52
) is given by

R^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}(x)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{-x+\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}}\int_{\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{x^{\prime}-\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}-x}\left[-\frac{\coth\big(2\pi\sqrt{x^{\prime}-\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}\big)}{\sqrt{x^{\prime}-\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}}}\right]

(287)

Shifting the integrand
x^{\prime}\to\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}+x^{\prime}
and writing
x
in terms of a new variable
z
as
x=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}-z^{2}
, we can put the matrix model prediction for the crosscap in the form

R^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}\Big(x=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}-z^{2}\Big)=-\frac{1}{2z}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}+z^{2}}\frac{\coth\big(2\pi\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)}{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}}

(288)

The matrix model prediction of the crosscap volume is given in terms of the resolvent by the integral transform

\displaystyle V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap,M}}(b)=\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}e^{bz}(-2z)R^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}\Big(x=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{\,2}}-z^{2}\Big),

(289)

where
z
is integrated in the imaginary direction. To derive this relation, at the level of an individual multiplet, one can combine
Z_{\rm xcap}(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}bZ_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta,b)e^{-\beta E_{0}}V_{\rm xcap,M}(b)
with
R(x)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\beta\,e^{\beta x}Z(\beta)
. Apply first the integral transform to go from the partition function to the resolvent and use that
-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\beta\,e^{\beta x}Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos}=e^{-bz}/(-2z)
, with
x=E_{0}-z^{2}
, to write the resolvent in terms of the volume. Applying an inverse Laplace transform, and using that
E_{0}=q^{2}/4\widehat{q}^{2}
, one arrives at the expression (
289
) given above.

We can insert (
288
) into the right hand side of (
289
), to derive
V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap,M}}(b)
. Even though the integral appearing in (
288
) diverges for large
x^{\prime}
, we can obtain a finite answer for the volume by first integrating over
z
and second over
x^{\prime}
. We get

\displaystyle V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap,M}}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\,\sin\big(b\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)\frac{\coth\big(2\pi\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)}{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}}=\frac{\coth(\frac{b}{4})}{2}=V^{(q)}_{{\rm xcap},{\sf T}}(b).

(290)

This matches precisely the prediction from gravity in (
285
), as indicated in the right hand side of eqn. (
290
). The result for the
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(3,4)
ensemble is the same up to an overall minus sign, which can be reproduced in gravity by including a factor of
(-1)^{n_{c}}
in the path integral, where
n_{c}
is the number of crosscaps.

(B) We now analyze
{\sf CT}
symmetry. The gravity calculation performed in the previous section gives the following answer for the crosscap partition function

\displaystyle Z_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b\,\int_{0}^{4\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi\,Z_{\rm tr}(b,\phi)\frac{(\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)\coth\frac{b}{4}}{8(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}},

(291)

The integrand is precisely (
275
) and the range over the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
holonomy
\phi
is from
0
to
4\pi
since we need to integrate over all possible holonomies around the crosscap, given in eqn. (
265
).
The integral is not absolutely convergent because of singularities when
b=0
and
\widehat{q}\phi
is an odd multiple of
\pi
; we will integrate over
\phi
before integrating over
b
, a procedure that seems to give sensible results.
Comparing with (
239
), the crosscap volume at “fixed charge” is given by

V_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}^{(q)}(b)=\int_{0}^{4\pi}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\phi}{2\pi}\,e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}\,\frac{(\cosh\frac{b}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi)\coth\frac{b}{4}}{8(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b}{4})^{2}}

(292)

A factor of
1/(2\pi)
comes, again, from the normalization of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
trumpet in terms of
Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}
. This integral seems complicated but can be computed using residues in a straightforward way. In order to do this, change variables from
\phi
to
w=e^{{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\phi}{2}}
and write the integrand as a meromorphic function in
w
implementing the following replacements
2\cos\widehat{q}\phi\to w^{2\widehat{q}}+w^{-2\widehat{q}}
,
2\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi}{2}\to w^{\widehat{q}}+w^{-\widehat{q}}
and
e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi q}\to w^{-2q}
. The corresponding integration contour in the complex
w
-plane is over the unit circle
|w|^{2}=1
. We can evaluate the integral as the sum over the residues at the poles inside the unit disk in the complex
w
-plane. The integrand has potentially a pole at
w=0
and
\widehat{q}
poles at
w^{\widehat{q}}=-{\mathrm{i}}e^{-b/4}
(the poles at
w^{\widehat{q}}={\mathrm{i}}e^{+b/4}
are outside of the unit disk and do not contribute to this sum). The final answer is

V_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}^{(q)}(b)=\begin{cases}\frac{\coth\frac{b}{4}}{2}\delta_{q,0}-e^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\pi|q|}{\widehat{q}}}e^{-\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}b},&\text{if $2q=0~{\rm mod}~\widehat{q}$,}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(293)

As a preliminary observation, charge conjugation acts as
q\to-q
and we see indeed that the crosscap volume depends only on
|q|
. We remind the reader that a
{\sf CT}
symmetry is only possible if
\delta=0
or
1/2
. We separately analyze each case below.

\delta=1/2
:

Let us consider first the anomalous theory. In this case the spectrum of
R
-charges
k
is half-integral and therefore the bulk charge of a supermultiplet
q=k\pm\widehat{q}/2
is an integer. The random matrix ensemble analysis implies that for all
q>0
we should study an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
while for
q=0
we should study the
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,1)
or
(1,4)
ensembles, depending on the sign of
{\sf CT}^{2}
. Let us therefore begin with
q=0
, corresponding to a supermultiplet with
R
-charges
(-\widehat{q}/2,\widehat{q}/2)
. In this case the crosscap volume given in eqn. (
293
) becomes

V_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}^{(0)}(b)=\frac{\coth\frac{b}{4}}{2}-1

(294)

Next compare this with the matrix model crosscap in the
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,1)
AZ ensemble. The answer for
(1,4)
is the same up to an overall minus sign that in gravity can be interpreted as adding a factor
(-1)^{n_{c}}
in the path integral, where
n_{c}
is the number of crosscaps. The matrix model prediction to the crosscap contribution to the resolvent, using the spectral curve given in eqn. (
52
) and using the loop equation (
286
), is given by

\displaystyle R^{(0)}_{\rm xcap}(x=-z^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{1}{x^{\prime}-x}\frac{\sqrt{\sigma(x^{\prime})}}{\sqrt{\sigma(x)}}\left(\frac{(1-\frac{2}{\upbeta})y^{\prime}(x^{\prime})}{2y(x^{\prime})}+\frac{\upalpha_{0}-1}{2\upbeta x^{\prime}}\right)

(295)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2z}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}+z^{2}}\left[-\frac{\coth\big(2\pi\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)}{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}}+\frac{1}{\pi x^{\prime}}\right],

where
\sigma(x)=x
in this case. We remind the reader in deriving this equation that for the
q=0
supermultiplet, the gap vanishes
E_{0}(q=0)=0
and there are no BPS states with charges
\pm\widehat{q}/2
. Since there is no pole in the spectral curve for this multiplet at
x=0
we also do not need to add any logarithmic correction to the matrix potential. We can use the relation between the matrix model prediction for the volume and the matrix model resolvent to verify

\displaystyle V^{(0)}_{{\rm xcap,M}}(b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}e^{bz}(-2z)R^{(0)}_{\rm xcap}(-z^{2})

(296)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}~\sin\big(b\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)\left[\frac{\coth\big(2\pi\sqrt{x^{\prime}}\big)}{\sqrt{x^{\prime}}}-\frac{1}{\pi x^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\coth\frac{b}{4}}{2}-1=V_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}^{(0)}(b).

We therefore obtain a match between gravity and the matrix ensemble proposed at the beginning of this section for the
q=0
sector.

Next we analyze the other multiplets with
q\neq 0
. An
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
AZ ensemble predicts a vanishing crosscap contribution to the resolvent. In gravity we obtained that the crosscap volume vanishes for most charges except when
2q=0~{\rm mod}~\widehat{q}
, which in the
\delta=1/2
theory reduces to
q=0~{\rm mod}~\widehat{q}
. The crosscap volume in this case is

V_{{\rm xcap},{\sf CT}}^{(q)}(b)=-(-1)^{q}e^{-\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}b}.

(297)

How can we make sense of this non-zero result? It is customarily assumed that, when taking the large
L
limit of a matrix integral, we keep fixed the matrix potential or equivalently the spectral curve
y(x)
. Nevertheless, one is free to include small corrections to the spectral curve (or the matrix potential) of order
1/L
. This will contribute to the resolvent
at the same order in
e^{-S_{0}}
as a crosscap. This possibility has
not been necessary in previous discussions of two-dimensional gravity and matrix models, but it does seem to be necessary in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity with
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry.

Let us write
y(x)=y_{0}(x)+e^{-S_{0}}y_{1/2}(x)+\ldots
and compute the correction
y_{1/2}(x)
needed to reproduce (
297
). We can use the crosscap volume from gravity to evaluate the change in the partition function

Z^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b\,\frac{e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}}}{2\sqrt{\pi\beta}}e^{-\beta\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{2}}}\,(-1)^{q+1}e^{-\frac{|q|b}{2\widehat{q}}}.

(298)

The correction to the resolvent is given by

R_{\rm xcap}^{(q)}(x)=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\beta\,e^{\beta x}\,Z^{(q)}_{\rm xcap}(\beta)=\frac{(-1)^{q}}{2z(z+\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}})},~~~{\rm where}~~x=\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{2}}-z^{2}.

(299)

The difference between
R(x)
and
y(x)
is
V^{\prime}(x)/2
, which can be an arbitrary analytic function of
x
up to the possible presence of a simple pole at
x=0
. But

y
is odd under the hyperelliptic involution
z\leftrightarrow-z
. Therefore we can read off the change in

y
from the odd piece of (
299
):

y_{1/2}(x)=(-1)^{q}\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}\,\frac{1}{2xz}.

(300)

The first check of our proposal is that
y_{1/2}(x)
has the right analytic behavior to be interpreted as a correction to the spectral curve. Following
Maxfield:2020ale
and
Witten:2020wvy
, the pole in
z
at
z=0
indicates this is the first term in a series that resums into a shift of the ground state energy of the supermultiplet

E_{0}(q)\to E_{0}(q)+e^{-S_{0}}(-1)^{q}\frac{2\pi|q|}{\widehat{q}}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2S_{0}}).

(301)

This statement, and the precise prefactors, can be verified easily by expanding the spectral curve (
52
) under a small variation of
E_{0}
near
z=0
. In addition,
the correction to the spectral curve (
300
) has a pole at
x=0
indicating the need for the correction to the matrix potential to have a
\log x
term as well.
A singularity of
y
other than the

1/z
and
1/x
singularities that we have actually found would not be consistent with interpreting
y_{1/2}(x)
as a correction to the spectral curve.

This interpretation of
y_{1/2}
has non-trivial implications for higher order corrections to the resolvent, and we will not attempt a full analysis here. But we will verify our proposal in the first nontrivial case. The leading order, connected, two-boundary partition function of the matrix integral of an AZ ensemble in the double scaling limit is universal and depends solely on
E_{0}
, the ground state energy,

Z_{0,2}=\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{2\pi(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})E_{0}}.

(302)

Therefore a change in
y
will influence
Z_{0,2}
only through the shift in
E_{0}
.
With this in mind, consider the topological expansion of the two-boundary partition function as
Z_{2}=Z_{0,2}+e^{-S_{0}}Z_{\frac{1}{2},2}+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-2S_{0}})
. If the
e^{-S_{0}}Z_{\frac{1}{2},2}
contribution
reflects a correction to
y
, then we can predict
Z_{\frac{1}{2},2}
in terms of
the shift of the ground state energy computed in (
301
). In terms of the fixed charge partition function, the prediction is

Z^{(q)}_{\frac{1}{2},2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=-(-1)^{q}\frac{2\pi|q|}{\widehat{q}}\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{2\pi}e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})E_{0}(q)}.

(303)

We can reproduce this prediction directly from gravity. For this purpose, we consider the path integral on a hyperbolic cylinder with a crosscap in the interior, see fig.
13
. We first introduce
V_{\frac{1}{2},2}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2})
as the path integral of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT gravity on the sphere with two geodesic boundaries and one crosscap. This quantity is easy to compute combining the rules given in section
4
and section
6.2
, since it does not involve a non-trivial action of the mapping class group. The answer is

\displaystyle V_{\frac{1}{2},2}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{4\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{3}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b_{3}V_{0,3}({\sf b}_{1},{\sf b}_{2},\overline{{\sf b}}_{3})\frac{(\cosh\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{3})\coth\frac{b_{3}}{4}}{8(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi_{3}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b_{3}}{4})^{2}}

(304)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{2}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b_{3}\int_{0}^{4\pi}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{3}}{2\pi}\,e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{3}q}\,\frac{(\cosh\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\cos\widehat{q}\phi_{3})\coth\frac{b_{3}}{4}}{8(\cos\frac{\widehat{q}\phi_{3}}{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\sinh\frac{b_{3}}{4})^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{q}\frac{(-1)^{q}}{2\pi}\frac{q^{2}}{4\widehat{q}^{2}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}b_{3}e^{-\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{q}\frac{(-1)^{q}}{2\pi}\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}\,e^{{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{2}}

In the first line, we start with a three-holed sphere and glue a crosscap to one hole, labeled
3
. We included an orientation reversal of the crosscap
R
-symmetry holonomy with respect to the three-holed sphere as indicated by
\overline{{\sf b}}_{3}=(b_{3},-\phi_{3})
. In the second line we used (
173
) and wrote the delta function on holonomies as a sum over charges
q

-\delta^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}-\phi_{3})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{2}e^{{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}q\phi_{3}}.

(305)

which allowed us to combine a factor of
e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\phi_{3}q}
with the crosscap contribution into the same integral we already computed above in (
293
). Finally we performed the
\phi_{3}
integral. In the last two lines we did not include the contribution of (
293
) with
q=0
since, even though the
b_{3}
integral is divergent, it is multiplied by a factor of
q^{2}
and therefore automatically vanishes. (This is consistent with the fact that the
q=0
multiplet does not require any correction to the spectral curve.) Finally, we can glue this volume to the two external trumpets and obtain the correction to the two-boundary partition function in a sector of “fixed charge”
q

\displaystyle Z^{(q)}_{\frac{1}{2},2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(-1)^{q}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}{\mathrm{d}}b_{1}Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(b_{1},\beta_{1})\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{2}{\mathrm{d}}b_{2}Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(b_{2},\beta_{2})(2\pi)\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}

(306)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(-1)^{q}\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}{\pi}(2\pi)\frac{|q|}{2\widehat{q}}=-(-1)^{q}\frac{|q|}{\widehat{q}}\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}

(307)
