This answer obtained from the gravitational path integral, in the second line, matches precisely the matrix model prediction (
303
) in the presence of a correction to the spectral curve, verifying our proposal.

\beta_{1},\alpha_{1}
\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}

Figure 13:
Contribution to the connected two-boundary path integral with a crosscap in the interior. The two NAdS boundaries are labeled by their inverse temperature and
R
-charge chemical potential, while the crosscap is indicated with a cross. The geodesic dividing the NAdS boundaries from the internal surface are denoted by dashed lines.

Even though we have found a matrix integral that reproduces the gravitational path integral to this order in the topological expansion, we should stress that we do not have an understanding of why in the presence of
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry, and not otherwise, such a modification of the spectral curve is necessary.

\delta=0
:

Finally we analyze the non-anomalous theory with
\delta=0
. Since the spectrum of
R
-charges
k
is integral, the spectrum of bulk charges
q=k\pm\widehat{q}/2
will be half-integral. Again we find from (
293
) that most multiplets have a vanishing contribution to the crosscap except when
2q=0~{\rm mod}~\widehat{q}
. In those cases, the crosscap volume has the same
b
-dependence as considered above and we can again interpret it as a shift of the spectral curve. The check for the cylinder with a crosscap works as well. The only new feature in this case is that the “fixed charge” crosscap volume is imaginary since it is proportional to
e^{-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\pi|q|}{\widehat{q}}}={\mathrm{i}}(-1)^{q+\frac{\widehat{q}}{2}}
with half-integer
q
. To get a real answer, we
need the factor of
\pm{\mathrm{i}}
, coming from the eta-invariant, that was explained at the end of section
6.2
.

There is a subtlety here concerning the supermultiplets
(-\widehat{q},0)
and
(0,\widehat{q}\,)
that contain states of
R
-charge zero. While a generic supermultiplet in the presence of

{\sf{CT}}
symmetry is described by an AZ
multiplet with
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(1,2)
, the
(-\widehat{q},0)
and
(0,\widehat{q}\,)
multiplets are different because they both contain states of vanishing charge, and states of vanishing charge
are mapped to themselves
by
{\sf{CT}}
. An analysis similar to what we have described before for in other examples shows that these multiplets are naturally described by ensembles with

(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(-1,2)
. With an analytic potential, such an ensemble would produce a non-vanishing crosscap correction. As discussed in appendix
B
, we
can add a
\log x
term to the matrix potential that shifts the effective value of
\upalpha_{0}
. After such a shift, we can make the measure for the singular values of
Q_{0}
and
Q_{-\widehat{q}}
take the same form as an
(\upalpha_{0},\upbeta)=(+1,2)
ensemble, therefore removing the contribution to the crosscap coming from the loop equations. After doing this, we can again add a small correction to the spectral curve in a way that reproduces the non-vanishing crosscap volume computed from gravity.

Finally, we note that in both the
{\sf T}
-invariant case and the
{\sf{CT}}
-invariant case, the
b
integral is divergent at
b=0
, which means that it is probably difficult to
extend the comparison between the matrix model and the crosscap to higher orders
44
44

44

In the bosonic case, the divergence can be regulated in gravity by working with the
(2,p)
minimal string which becomes JT gravity only in the large
p
limit
SSS
;
SW
;
Mertens:2020hbs
. It is reasonable to expect that if a similar duality between matrix integrals and the non-critical
{\mathcal{N}}=2
string exists, the crosscap would again be finite.
. In the case of
{\sf{CT}}
symmetry, the divergence is only at
q=0
(assuming that one
integrates over
\phi
before integrating over
b
, as we have done), so for other values
of
q
, there appears to be no difficulty in computing in higher orders. However, this is precisely the case in which we have found that the matrix potential has higher order
corrections. We do not know if further corrections are needed in going to higher orders, so we do not have clear predictions about the higher order amplitudes.

7
\mathcal{N}=4
JT Gravity Black Hole

In this section we present some partial results regarding the study of pure
{\cal N}=4
JT gravity including a sum over topologies.

7.1
The
{\cal N}=4
JT Black Hole Spectrum

The general
structure of
{\mathcal{N}}=4
JT supergravity is similar to what we have already discussed for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
.
First, the action has, besides a topological term proportional to
S_{0}
, a bulk action that can be written as a topological
BF
theory with gauge supergroup
G=\text{PSU}(1,1|2)
. A
maximal bosonic subgroup of
G
is
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
. The first factor describes gravity while the second is the gauge
group of an
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)

BF
theory. We denote generators of this group as
J^{1},J^{2},J^{3}
. For asymptotically NAdS boundary conditions, boundary terms are required; these are simpler to write in a second order formulation. The bosonic fields in bulk are a graviton and
dilaton along with the
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
gauge fields. The gravitino is now in the fundamental of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
.
Because the group
\text{PSU}(1,1|2)
has an
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
group of outer automorphisms, the
BF
theory of
\text{PSU}(1,1|2)
has
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
as a global symmetry group.
45
45

45

If one gauges
this global
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, one gets a
BF
theory of the supergroup
D(2,1;\alpha)
, potentially related to a near extremal limit of black holes that have AdS
3
throats with large
{\cal N}=4

superconformal symmetry.
To distinguish the two
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
’s, we refer to the one that is gauged as the
R
-symmetry group
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, and the other one we call

{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{{\rm gl}}
.

The disk partition function for
{\mathcal{N}}=4
again reduces to the path integral of a boundary reparametrization mode, the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
Schwarzian mode. We work at inverse temperature
\beta
and we turn on a chemical potential for the Cartan of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, parametrizing the fugacity by
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha J^{3}}
. This problem was studied in
Heydeman:2020hhw
. The boundary theory governs reparametrizations of the
{\cal N}=4
supercircle
(t,\theta,\overline{\theta})
where
\theta
is a complex
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
doublet and
\overline{\theta}
its complex conjugate. The grand canonical partition function is found by imposing the following twisted conditions

\displaystyle(t,\theta,\overline{\theta})\to\big(t+\beta,-e^{{\mathrm{i}}\alpha J^{3}}\theta,-e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\alpha J^{3}}\overline{\theta}\big).

(308)

Since
\theta
is in the fundamental representation, we can write
J^{3}=Z/2
where
Z={\rm diag}(1,-1)
is a Pauli matrix. Thus
\alpha
has periodicity
\alpha\cong\alpha+4\pi

as well as the
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
Weyl invariance, which acts by
\alpha\to-\alpha
. The final result for the partition function is

Z_{\rm disk}=e^{S_{0}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\beta\frac{\cot(\frac{\alpha}{4})(\alpha+4\pi n)}{\pi^{4}(1-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}(\alpha+4\pi n)^{2})^{2}}\,e^{\frac{\pi^{2}}{\beta}\big(1-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}(\alpha+4\pi n)^{2}\big)}\,.

(309)

The derivation is outlined in appendix
A
.

With NAdS boundary conditions, the boundary quantum mechanics has four supercharges
Q_{A\dot{A}}
,
A,\dot{A}=1,2
transforming as a bifundamental
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
(acting on the
A
index) and
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
(acting on
\dot{A}
) and satisfying

\{Q_{A\dot{A}},Q_{B\dot{B}}\}=\varepsilon_{AB}\varepsilon_{\dot{A}\dot{B}}H,

(310)

with
H
the Hamiltonian. Here
\varepsilon_{AB}
and
\varepsilon_{\dot{A}\dot{B}}
are antisymmetric tensors with
\varepsilon_{12}=\varepsilon_{\dot{1}\dot{2}}=1
.
Alternatively, since
({\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\cong{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
(where here
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is the product of the centers of the two
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
’s), and a bifundamental
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
is a vector of
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
, the supersymmetry algebra contains four hermitian supercharges
Q_{I}
,
I=1,\cdots,4
transforming as a vector of
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
,
and satisfying

\{Q_{I},Q_{J}\}=2\delta_{IJ}H.

(311)

In quantum mechanics with
{\mathcal{N}}=4
supersymmetry, a BPS multiplet consists of a single irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
of any spin
J
, while an irreducible
non-BPS multiplets decomposes under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
as
J\oplus(J-1/2)\oplus(J-1/2)\oplus(J-1)
with some
J\geq 1/2

(for
J=1/2
, the multiplet instead reduces to
0\oplus 0\oplus 1/2
). The partition function (
309
) can be expanded in such multiplets as

\hskip-5.69046ptZ_{\rm disk}=e^{S_{0}}+\sum_{J\geq\frac{1}{2}}\big(\chi_{J}(\alpha)+2\chi_{J-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha)+\chi_{J-1}(\alpha)\big)\int_{J^{2}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}Ee^{-\beta E}\frac{e^{S_{0}}J\,\sinh\big(2\pi\sqrt{E-J^{2}}\big)}{\pi^{2}E^{2}}.

(312)

where
\chi_{J}(\alpha)=\frac{\sin{((2J+1)\alpha/2)}}{\sin(\alpha/2)}
is the character of a spin
J
representation of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, and the gap in the spectrum is given by
E_{0}(J)=J^{2}
for a supermultiplet with maximum spin
J
.
(For
J=1/2
, since formally
\chi_{-1/2}(\alpha)=0
, the expression
\chi_{J}(\alpha)+2\chi_{J-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha)+\chi_{J-1}(\alpha)
for the character of a non-BPS multiplet
reduces to
\chi_{1/2}(\alpha)+2\chi_{0}(\alpha)
, which is the character of the exceptional multiplet
J=1/2
.) Since
J\geq 1/2
in all cases,
the energy gap
J^{2}
is non-vanishing for all non-BPS multiplets.
In contrast to the case of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
, there is no multiplet with a vanishing gap.
The BPS states are described by the term
e^{S_{0}}
in eqn. (
312
), and since this term is independent of
\alpha
, they all have spin 0.

The spectrum extracted from this partition function is very simple.
In addition to
e^{S_{0}}
BPS states, all of spin 0, there is for each
J\geq 1/2
a continuous spectrum of non-BPS supermultiplets with highest spin
J
.
The density of states is

\displaystyle\rho_{J}(E)=\begin{cases}e^{S_{0}}\frac{J\sinh\big(2\pi\sqrt{E-J^{2}}\big)}{\pi^{2}E^{2}}&E\geq J^{2}\cr 0&E<J^{2}.\end{cases}

(313)

To obtain this answer from a matrix model, the spectral curve should be

\displaystyle y_{J}(x)=\frac{J\sin\big(2\pi\sqrt{-x+J^{2}}\big)}{\pi x^{2}}.

(314)

The behavior of the spectrum near threshold is the typical square root behavior
y_{J}(J^{2}+\varepsilon)\sim\sqrt{\varepsilon}
of a Dyson ensemble in random matrix theory,
or an AZ ensemble in which the threshold energy is positive. The behavior at
x=0
merits discussion. Naively
y_{J}(x)
has a double pole at
x=0
:

\displaystyle y_{J}(x)=\frac{J\sin(2\pi J)}{\pi x^{2}}-\frac{\cos(2\pi J)}{x}+({\rm analytic~in~}x).

(315)

The double-pole would be problematic since it would imply a highly divergent term in the matrix potential at
x=0
that would spoil the derivation of the loop equations in their usual form. This is explained in appendix
B
. Fortunately, since
2J
is an integer the coefficient of the double-pole vanishes:
\sin(2\pi J)=0
. The coefficient of the simple pole is non-zero and equal to
(-1)^{2J+1}
. Thus the residue of the simple pole is positive or negative for half-integer or integer
J
.
A simple pole is consistent with the loop equations in the presence of a logarithmic term in the potential, as discussed in the appendix.

{\mathcal{N}}=4
JT supergravity does not have some of the generalizations that exist for
{\mathcal{N}}=2
. There is no analog of the parameter
\widehat{q}
,
since the
R
-symmetry group
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
is simply-connected. For essentially the same reason, there is no analog of the parameter
\delta
.

7.2
The Two-Boundary Wormhole

Having obtained the spectrum and the multiplet structure, the next question is whether different multiplets are statistically independent or not.
The most basic test of this question comes from the amplitude for a cylinder, the leading connected amplitude with two NAdS boundaries.
The path integral on the cylinder reduces to an integral over the moduli space of flat
\text{PSU}(1,1|2)
connections, modulo gauge transformations. These are labeled by the supergroup holonomy around the non-contractible circle. In order to restrict to gauge connections that have an interpretation in hyperbolic geometry we restrict to holonomies with the property that the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)
block is in the hyperbolic conjugacy class. We assume no other restriction. This holonomy is parametrized by a geodesic length
b
, describing the conjugacy class of the
{\mathrm{SU}}(1,1)
block, and an
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
holonomy conjugate to
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\phi J^{3}}
, with parameter
\phi
,
where in the fundamental representation
J^{3}=Z/2
with
Z
the diagonal Pauli matrix. Like
\alpha
,
\phi
has a periodicity
\phi\to\phi+4\pi
and a Weyl symmetry

\phi\to-\phi
. For concreteness we take
\phi>0
. There is also a Schwarzian reparametrization mode on each boundary. The path integral of the reparametrization mode along each boundary is given by

Z_{\rm tr}(\beta,\alpha;b,\phi)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\cot(\frac{\alpha}{4})}{4\pi\beta}e^{-\frac{1}{4\beta}b^{2}}\Big(e^{-\frac{1}{4\beta}(\alpha-\phi+4\pi n)^{2}}-e^{-\frac{1}{4\beta}(\alpha+\phi+4\pi n)^{2}}\Big)\,.

(316)

We review the derivation in appendix
A
. Using Poisson resummation, this expression can be written as a sum over representations:

Z_{\rm tr}(\beta,\alpha;b,\phi)=\sum_{J\geq\frac{1}{2}}\left(\chi_{J}(\alpha)+2\chi_{J-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha)+\chi_{J-1}(\alpha)\right)2\sin(J\phi)\,\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{e^{-\beta J^{2}-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}}}{2\sqrt{\pi\beta}}.

(317)

The
\beta
dependence is the same as that of the trumpet in bosonic JT (
Z_{\rm tr}^{\rm bos.}(\beta,b)=e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta}}/(2\sqrt{\pi\beta})
) up to a shift of the group state energy to
E_{0}(J)=J^{2}
.

We can now compute the cylinder partition function by gluing two of these trumpets along a common geodesic boundary. The final answer is

Z_{0,2}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}b{\mathrm{d}}b\int_{0}^{4\pi}{\mathrm{d}}\phi\,Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};b,\phi)Z_{\rm tr}(\beta_{2},\alpha_{2};b,-\phi).

(318)

The factor of
2\pi
comes from the integral over the twist parametrizing the maximal torus of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
, including a factor of
1/2
because of the Weyl symmetry. (In any case, we should emphasize we do not claim to properly understand the absolute normalization of the trumpet.) After gluing the two trumpets we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{0,2}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{J_{1},J_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}}\left(\chi_{J_{1}}(\alpha_{1})+2\chi_{J_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha_{1})+\chi_{J_{1}-1}(\alpha_{1})\right)

(319)

\displaystyle\times\left(\chi_{J_{2}}(\alpha_{2})+2\chi_{J_{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha_{2})+\chi_{J_{2}-1}(\alpha_{2})\right)\int_{0}^{4\pi}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\phi}{8\pi}(2\sin J_{1}\phi)(2\sin J_{2}\phi)

\displaystyle\times e^{-\beta_{1}J_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}J_{2}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}b{\mathrm{d}}b\,\frac{e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta_{1}}-\frac{b^{2}}{4\beta_{2}}}}{4\pi\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}}.

The integral over the geodesic length
b
gives the same as before, since the trumpet is again proportional to the bosonic one. Integration over
\phi
imposes that
J_{1}=J_{2}
. Therefore again we can only have the same multiplet propagating on each boundary to get a non-zero answer. The final result is then

Z_{0,2}(\beta_{1},\alpha_{1};\beta_{2},\alpha_{2})=\sum_{J\geq\frac{1}{2}}\Big[\prod_{i=1,2}\big(\chi_{J}(\alpha_{i})+2\chi_{J-\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha_{i})+\chi_{J-1}(\alpha_{i})\big)\Big]\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\,e^{-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})J^{2}}}{2\pi(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})}.

(320)

The multiplets are statistically independent, and the temperature dependence matches the expectations from a double-scaled matrix integral with a gap
E_{0}(J)=J^{2}
. This is
encouraging for a possible random matrix description.

7.3
{\mathcal{N}}=4
Random Matrix Theory

Finally, in this section we will explore aspects of
{\mathcal{N}}=4
random matrix theory. We will consider the case that only
J=1/2
multiplets (multiplets that decompose under

{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
as
0\oplus 0\oplus 1/2
) are present.
By similar logic to that of section
2.3
, we will argue that it is natural for random
{\mathcal{N}}=4
supersymmetry with all multiplets of
J=1/2

to be described by an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,2)
, the familiar ensemble that is important in
{\mathcal{N}}=1
and
{\mathcal{N}}=2
JT supergravity. We expect the same to be true
for multiplets of any
J
, but it would be technically more complicated to demonstrate this.

The
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra in the
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
language of eqn. (
311
) has an obvious resemblance to a Clifford algebra.
That fact enables an easy construction of the
J=1/2
representation, from which all other representations are easily constructed. Consider four gamma matrices

\gamma_{I}
,
I=1,\cdots,4
, satisfying
\{\gamma_{I},\gamma_{J}\}=2\delta_{IJ}
, transforming as a vector of
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
(or equivalently a
bifundamental of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
) and acting in the usual way on a four-dimensional
Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
. As usual we let
\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}\gamma_{4}
be the product of the four
\gamma_{I}
.
Then, we get an irreducible representation of
{\mathcal{N}}=4
global supersymmetry by taking

Q_{I}=\lambda\gamma_{I},~~~~H=\lambda^{2},

(321)

with a nonzero constant
\lambda
. To make the
Q_{I}
hermitian,
\lambda
must be real, and by possibly conjugating with
\gamma_{5}
, we can reduce to
\lambda>0
.
A familiar fact about spinors is that
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
transforms under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
as
(1/2,0)\oplus(0,1/2)
. So we can describe
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
as
{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}
,
where
{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}
is a two-dimensional vector space in the spin 1/2 representation of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}
is a two-dimensional vector space in the spin 1/2 representation
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
. However, since
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
is not part of the supersymmetry
algebra, we often want to describe
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
just as a representation of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
. From that point of view,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is just a two-dimensional vector space on which

{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
acts trivially, so
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}
.

We can now also easily describe an arbitrary irreducible representation of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra. For
J
a positive integer or half-integer, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
be a Hilbert space of dimension

2J+1
on which
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
acts as spin
J
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{J-1/2}
. Setting
Q_{I}=\lambda\gamma_{I}\otimes 1
, we get an irreducible representation of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4

algebra.
{\mathcal{H}}
transforms under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
as
(J-1)\oplus(J-1/2)\oplus(J-1/2)\oplus J
, and we label this representation by the highest spin it contains,
namely
J
. We note that
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
actually acts on
this representation via its action on
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
, transforming the supersymmetry generators in the usual way. So even though it is not part of the
supersymmetry algebra,
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
acts on every irreducible representation of this algebra, and therefore on every representation.
46
46

46

In general, an outer automorphism of
a group
G
will either act in a given representation
W
, or conjugate
W
to some inequivalent representation. In the present case, since the group
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
is connected,
there is no way for it to act nontrivially on the discrete label
J\in\{1/2,1,3/2,\cdots\}
of a representation and instead it
acts in each representation.
This fact turns out to be helpful in understanding the random matrix theory.

Here, however, as already noted,
we will restrict ourselves to considering a random realization of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra that is isomorphic to the direct sum of some number
L
of copies of the basic
J=1/2

representation. For this, we consider a bosonic Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2L}
and a fermionic Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}={\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{f}^{\prime}
with
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}^{\prime}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
.
Thus,
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
acts trivially on
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
transforms as
the direct sum of
L
copies of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}
.
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
also has an expected action on
Q_{I}
coming from
the fact that they transform as
(1/2,1/2)
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
.
Then we look for a set of four operators
Q_{I}:{\mathcal{H}}_{b}\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
that obey the algebra (
310
) and intertwine with the action
of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, acting on the four
Q_{I}
and on the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{b}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
in the expected fashion.

The symmetry group of this problem is simply the group of unitary transformations of
{\mathcal{H}}
that commute with
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
. This is
{\mathrm{U}}(2L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
, where the first factor
acts on
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
and the second on
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}^{\prime}
.

Given our knowledge of the classification of representations of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra, we can use the group
{\mathrm{U}}(2L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
to put
any set of
Q_{I}
that generate such a representation in a canonical form. A representation of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{f}={\mathbb{C}}^{2L}\oplus S_{1/2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
is
generically
47
47

47

The reason that this is only generically the case is that a
J=1/2
representation with
H=\lambda^{2}

degenerates to a collection of BPS states if
\lambda=0
.
the direct sum of
L
copies of the
J=1/2
representation. In a single copy of that representation, we have as before

{\mathcal{H}}_{f}={\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}

and
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}
, and we can assume that
Q_{I}=\lambda\gamma_{I}
, with the
\gamma_{I}
being generators of a Clifford
algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
. To get
L
copies of this representation, we introduce an
L
-dimensional vector space
V\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
with trivial action of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, and then take

{\mathcal{H}}_{b}=S^{\prime}_{1/2}\otimes V
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}=S_{1/2}\otimes V
, and

Q_{I}=\gamma_{I}\otimes M,

(322)

where
M
is a hermitian matrix acting on
V
. By a unitary transformation, we can put
M
in a diagonal form
M={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{L})
, and,
since it is possible to make a
\gamma_{5}
transformation separately for each “eigenvalue”
\lambda_{i}
, we can assume that
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
.

We have already determined that the symmetry group in this problem, taking into account the
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
symmetry,
is
K={\mathrm{U}}(2L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
, with real dimension
5L^{2}
. We will now show that, given the constraint of

{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
-invariance, to specify
Q_{I}
requires
8L^{2}
real parameters. And we will show that the supersymmetry algebra imposes
3L^{2}
real constraints on the
Q_{I}
.
One might therefore expect that the moduli space of representations of the supersymmetry algebra with the assumed dimension and action of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
would have
dimension
8L^{2}-5L^{2}-3L^{2}=0
. Instead, by explicit construction in eqn. (
322
), we see that this moduli space has dimension
L
, parametrized by
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{L}
. What is happening is that the
5L^{2}
generators of
K
are not all effective at reducing the moduli space dimension, because an
L
-dimensional subalgebra,
consisting of the matrices on
V
that commute with
M
, leaves fixed
Q_{I}
in eqn. (
322
). Hence the dimension of the moduli space
is actually
8L^{2}-(5L^{2}-L)-3L^{2}=L
.

This counting shows that the
3L^{2}
constraints that come from the supersymmetry algebra are all effective at reducing the dimension of the moduli space.
So they are all independent; there are no relations between these constraints.
In section
2.3
, we found that in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
random matrix theory, there are relations between the constraints as soon as
at least four consecutive values of the
R
-charge are present.
For
{\mathcal{N}}=4
, it turns out that there are relations between the constraints
for all non-BPS representations with
J>1/2
, even if only one type of representation is present.
48
48

48

In fact, there are not only relations between the constraints;
there are relations between the relations, and higher order relations between those relations, and so on, with a number of steps of order
J
.
That is the main reason
that here we will analyze the random matrix theory only for
J=1/2
; the case of higher
J
is technically more complicated.

It remains to verify that after taking into account the action of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, there are
8L^{2}
parameters in the
Q_{I}
, and the supersymmetry algebra places

3L^{2}
relations among those parameters.

To count parameters in
Q_{I}
, we note first that we can write
Q_{I}={\mathcal{Q}}_{I}+{\mathcal{Q}}_{I}^{\dagger}
, where
{\mathcal{Q}}_{I}
maps
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{I}^{\dagger}
is the adjoint map
from
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
.
{\mathcal{Q}}_{I}
is constrained by
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
invariance, but it does not satisfy any condition of hermiticity (and there is no constraint associated to
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
,
since
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
invariance is not assumed). At this point, it is convenient to think of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{I}
as a bifundamental of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
rather than a vector
of
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
. Four operators
{\mathcal{Q}}_{A\dot{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{L}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{2L}
that transform with spin 1/2 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
are equivalent to a pair
of operators
{{X}}_{\dot{A}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{L}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{2L}
that obey no particular constraint.
The two operators
{{X}}_{\dot{A}}
depend on a total of
2\cdot L\cdot 2L=4L^{2}
complex parameters, or
8L^{2}
real parameters. This is the number of real parameters
involved in the choice of the
Q_{I}
, after imposing the action of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
, but without imposing the constraint that the
Q_{I}
satisfy the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra.

The number of parameters involved in imposing the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra can be determined similarly.

A priori
,
\{Q_{I},Q_{J}\}
transforms as
(0,0)\oplus(1,1)
under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
. The
(0,0)
part is the Hamiltonian, and the supersymmetry constraint says
that the
(1,1)
part vanishes. Of course,
\{Q_{I},Q_{J}\}
maps
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
. An operator that transforms as spin 1 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
cannot
map
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
nontrivially to itself, since
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
transforms with spin 0 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
. Since
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
transforms under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
with spin 1/2, and spin 1/2 arises in
the decomposition
1/2\otimes 1=1/2\oplus 3/2
, it is possible for a spin 1 operator
to map
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
to itself. A set of three hermitian operators mapping
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}={\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
to itself and transforming with spin 1 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
are determined by a single hermitian operator
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
, and thus depends on
L^{2}
real parameters. The
(1,1)
part of
\{Q_{I},Q_{J}\}
consists of 9 hermitian operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
, making up three vectors of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
.
This is equivalent to three hermitian operators on
{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
, so the number of real parameters is
3L^{2}
, as claimed earlier. These parameters transform under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
as
L^{2}
copies
of the spin 1 representation.

We still have to determine the measure for integration over the “eigenvalues”
\lambda_{i}
. By definition, when we do this, we are trying to compute the measure on the
space of
Q_{I}
’s that do satisfy the constraints of
{\mathcal{N}}=4
supersymmetry (modulo equivalence generated by the group
K
). As noted earlier, every solution of the constraints
is invariant under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
, acting via a suitable subgroup of
K
. In computing the measure, we have to consider (in first order) perturbations of the
Q_{I}
that do
not necessarily preserve the
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
symmetry, but
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
is still very useful in organizing and constraining the calculation.

As in eqn. (
322
), when the constraints are satisfied,
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
have decompositions
{\mathcal{H}}_{b}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}={\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}\otimes\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
,
where
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
acts on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}
(in the spin 1/2 representation) and not on anything else. Here
{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
are two different vector spaces, both
of dimension
L
; to emphasize that they are not the same, we now denote the second one as
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
. With this decomposition, it is now clear that the subgroup of

K={\mathrm{U}}(2L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
that commutes with
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
is
K^{\prime}={\mathrm{U}}(L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
, where the two factors act respectively on
{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
.
The group
K^{\prime}
has dimension
2L^{2}
, while
K
has dimension
5L^{2}
. The part of the Lie algebra of
K
that is orthogonal to the Lie algebra of
K^{\prime}

consists of
L^{2}
copies of the spin 1 representation of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
. (These constraints come from operators transforming as
(1,1)
under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}

and acting on an
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
-invariant Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{f}
, so they all transform with spin 1 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
.)

It turns out that the effect on the measure of
3L^{2}
symmetry generators transforming at
L^{2}
copies of spin 1 of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
cancels the effect on the
measure of
3L^{2}
constraints transforming in the same way. Before demonstrating this cancellation, we will first argue that if it does occur,
then a random realization of the
{\mathcal{N}}=4
algebra with the representation content that we have assumed is described by an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,2)
.

We simply write down the general form of the four hermitian operators
Q_{I}
, assuming that they intertwine with an action of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}\times{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
, not
just
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{R}
as required by the supersymmetry algebra:

Q_{I}=\gamma_{I}\frac{1+\gamma_{5}}{2}M+\gamma_{I}\frac{1-\gamma_{5}}{2}M^{\dagger}.

(323)

Here
M
is a completely unconstrained linear operator mapping
{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
to
\widetilde{\mathbb{C}}^{L}
, and
M^{\dagger}
is its adjoint acting in the opposite direction.
In other words,
M
is a bifundamental of
K^{\prime}={\mathrm{U}}(L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
. As we explained in section
2.2
, a bifundamental of
{\mathrm{U}}(L)\times{\mathrm{U}}(L)
is
naturally associated in random matrix theory to an AZ ensemble with
(\upalpha,\upbeta)=(1,2)
.

So all that remains is to show that there is no net effect on the measure of the symmetry generators and constraints that transform as spin 1 of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
.
The analysis can be simplified by using the residual symmetries. After putting
Q_{I}
in the canonical form (
322
) with a diagonal matrix

M={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{L})
, there is a residual
{\mathrm{U}}(1)^{L}
symmetry of diagonal matrices that commute with
M
.
A cancellation between symmetries and constraints occurs separately for each
{\mathrm{U}}(1)^{L}
representation. Symmetries and perturbations of
L
that are
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-invariant
preserve the diagonal structure of
Q_{I}
, and do not “mix” with the off-diagonal symmetries and perturbations. For each pair of distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}
, the
ij
matrix
elements of a symmetry generator or a perturbation of the
Q_{I}
transform in a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)^{L}
representation that is different for each pair
i,j
. This implies that one can
analyze the
ij
matrix elements for given
i,j
independently of the others.

To understand the cancellation that occurs for diagonal matrix elements, we do not really need to do a calculation. Diagonal symmetry generators and perturbations of the
Q_{I}

preserve the diagonal structure, and as long as this diagonal structure is preserved, the different diagonal matrix elements do not influence each other.
Therefore, to study a diagonal matrix element, it suffices to take
L=1
.
To understand why a cancellation must occur in this case, all we need to know is that for
L=1
, the
Q_{I}
are proportional to
\lambda
, the canonical form being

Q_{I}=\lambda\gamma_{I}
. This means that the vector fields corresponding to the generators of
K
that have spin 1 under
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
are all proportional to
\lambda
,
so each of these generators produces a factor
\lambda
in the measure. On the other hand, for any first order variation
\delta Q_{I}
of the
Q_{I}
, the constraints
give delta functions that assert the vanishing of the
(1,1)
part of
\{\delta Q_{I},Q_{J}\}+\{Q_{I},\delta Q_{J}\}
. This quantity is linear in the
Q_{I}
, so it is linear in
\lambda
.
So the constraints give delta functions of the general form
\delta(\lambda f)
, where
f
is independent of
\lambda
. As
\delta(\lambda f)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\delta(f)
,
each constraint gives a factor
1/\lambda
, canceling the factors that come from the symmetry generators.

To study off-diagonal symmetries and perturbations, it suffices to consider the case of
L=2
with
M={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.
The generators of
{\mathrm{SO}}(4)
acting on
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1/2}\otimes{\mathcal{S}}_{1/2}
are the antihermitian matrices
\sigma_{IJ}=\frac{1}{2}[\gamma_{I},\gamma_{J}]
. With an appropriate
orientation convention, the matrices on
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
that transform as spin 1 of
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
– that is, the
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)_{\rm gl}
generators – are
\zeta^{IJ}\sigma_{IJ}
, where
\zeta_{IJ}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{IJKL}\zeta^{KL}
is self-dual (
\epsilon_{IJKL}
is the antisymmetric tensor with
\epsilon_{1234}=1
). Thus, with

Q_{I}=\gamma_{I}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\cr 0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(324)

we can consider the off-diagonal symmetry generators

r(\zeta)=\zeta^{IJ}\sigma_{IJ}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&1\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}-{\rm h.c.}

(325)

The induced variation of
Q_{I}
is

\delta Q_{I}=[r(\zeta),Q_{I}]=-2\zeta_{IJ}\gamma^{J}\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}+2\zeta_{IJ}\gamma^{J}\gamma_{5}\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}+{\rm h.c.}

(326)

In the norm squared of
\delta Q_{I}
(namely
{\mathrm{Tr}}\,\delta Q_{I}^{2}
), there is no cross term between the parts of
\delta Q_{I}
with and without a factor of
\gamma_{5}
.
So the norm squared is a multiple of

(\zeta^{IJ}\overline{\zeta}_{IJ})\left((\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}+(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}\right)=2\zeta^{IJ}\overline{\zeta}_{IJ}(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}).

Hence,
each of the six independent real components of
\zeta_{IJ}
contributes a factor
(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
to the measure, giving an overall factor
(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{3}
.

However, the corresponding matrix elements of the constraints cancel this factor. A normalized off-diagonal perturbation
\delta^{\prime}Q_{I}
in the same
2\times 2
block that
is orthogonal to
\delta Q_{I}
is

\delta^{\prime}Q_{I}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}}}\left(\zeta_{IJ}\gamma^{J}\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}+\zeta_{IJ}\gamma^{J}\gamma_{5}\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}\right)+{\rm h.c.}.

(327)

We then find that

\{Q_{I},\delta^{\prime}Q_{J}\}+\{Q_{J},\delta^{\prime}Q_{I}\}=2\left(\sigma_{IK}\zeta_{JK}+\sigma_{JK}\zeta_{IK}\right)\begin{pmatrix}0&\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}}\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix}+{\rm h.c.}

(328)

For each independent real component of
\zeta_{IJ}
, we impose the vanishing of
\{Q_{I},\delta^{\prime}Q_{J}\}+\{Q_{J},\delta^{\prime}Q_{I}\}
as a constraint. In each case, the delta
function that imposes this constraint has an argument that is a multiple of
\sqrt{\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}}
. The resulting factor in the measure
is
1/(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2})^{3}
, canceling the measure factor that came from the corresponding symmetry generators.
